On a conjecture of Peter Neumann on fixed points in permutation groups
Daniele Garzoni, Robert M. Guralnick, Martin W. Liebeck

Abstract
. We prove a conjecture of Peter Neumann from 1966, predicting that every finite non-regular primitive permutation group of degree n n contains an element fixing at least one point and at most n 1 / 2 n^{1/2} points. In fact, we prove a stronger version, where n 1 / 2 n^{1/2} is replaced by n 1 / 3 n^{1/3} , and this is best possible. The case where G G is affine was proved by Guralnick and Malle; in this paper we address the case where G G is non-affine.



Dedicated to the memory of Peter Neumann

1.
Introduction

The study of fixed points of elements in finite primitive permutation groups has a long history. An early theme was the
minimal degree
: for
G
primitive of degree
n
, this is defined to be the smallest number of points moved by any non-identity element of
G
, denoted by
\mu(G)
. Nineteenth century results of Bochert and Jordan show that for
G\neq A_{n},S_{n}
, the minimal degree
\mu(G)
tends to infinity as
n\to\infty
, and further work of Jordan, Manning and others provides explicit lower bounds, culminating in Babai’s bound
\mu(G)>\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}
(see
[
4
]
and
[
22
, Thms. 5.3A,5.4A]
), which is not far from best possible. Babai’s proof does not use the classification of finite simple groups (CFSG). Using CFSG, the result has been much extended: for example in
[
26
]
it has been shown that
\mu(G)\geq\frac{1}{2}n
, provided certain explicit families of primitive groups
G
are excluded. To put it another way, in all primitive groups apart from these families, we have
\mathrm{fix}(g)\leq\frac{1}{2}n
for all
1\neq g\in G
(where
\mathrm{fix}(g)
is the number of fixed points of
g
).

Given these bounds for fixed point numbers covering all non-identity elements, it is natural to ask whether there exist individual elements that fix many fewer points than the overall bound.
This was the topic of Peter Neumann’s 1966 DPhil thesis
[
67
]
. By a well-known lemma of Jordan, any transitive permutation group of degree
n\geq 2
contains a derangement – that is, an element
g
with
\mathrm{fix}(g)=0
. Neumann investigated the existence of elements fixing few points (but at least one) in non-regular transitive groups, and proved that any such group
G
contains an element
g
with
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g)\leq\frac{1}{2}n
. This is sharp, as can be seen from the group
G=C_{m}\wr S_{2}
in an imprimitive action of degree
n=2m
. For primitive groups, Neumann conjectured a much stronger result:

Conjecture 1.1

(P. Neumann, 1966)
.

Let
G
be a finite primitive non-regular permutation group of degree
n
. Then, there exists
g\in G
with
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g)\leq n^{1/2}
.

Most of the work in the literature on this conjecture has concerned the case of primitive groups of affine type. For these we have
G=V\rtimes G_{0}\leq\mathrm{AGL}(V)
, where
V
is a finite vector space, and
G_{0}
is an irreducible subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}(V)
; the conjecture says that such a group
G_{0}
should possess an element
g
such that
\dim C_{V}(g)\leq\frac{1}{2}\dim V
. For
G
solvable, Neumann proved this, with the stronger upper bound
\frac{7}{18}\dim V
instead of
\frac{1}{2}\dim V
; and he conjectured that the true bound in this case should be
\frac{1}{3}\dim V
, noting that equality is achieved by the group
G_{0}=A_{4}<\mathrm{GL}_{3}(p)
for odd primes
p
. Of course Neumann did not have the CFSG available to use at the time. Using CFSG, Segal and Shalev
[
72
, Lemma 2.3]
proved the original Neumann conjecture for affine groups; following a further improvement in
[
34
]
, the affine conjecture with the bound
\frac{1}{3}\dim V
was finally established by Guralnick and Malle in
[
27
]
. Generalizing Neumann’s example, one can see that the bound is sharp for the groups
G_{0}=\mathrm{SO}_{3}(q)<\mathrm{GL}_{3}(q)
for
q
an odd prime power.

In this paper, we complete the proof of the strong version of Neumann’s conjecture, by addressing the case where
G
is non-affine. Combining with affine case
[
27
]
, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
G
be a finite primitive non-regular permutation group of degree
n
. Then there exists
g\in G
with
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g)\leq n^{1/3}
.

If
G
is not affine, we will in fact find
g\in G
with
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g)<n^{1/3}
; see Theorem
3.1
, Proposition
3.6
and the proofs of Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
.

As we have remarked already, the
n^{1/3}
bound is sharp for affine groups. For non-affine groups, the strict inequality is close to best possible. For example, if
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
with
q
even, and if we consider
G=S\times S
acting on
S
via
s^{(x_{1},x_{2})}=x_{1}^{-1}sx_{2}
, then the number of fixed points of an element of
G
is either
0
or at least
q-1=n^{1/3}(1-o(1))
. A similar example is given by the action of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
on the cosets of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
for
q
even.

The following is an immediate consequence of
Theorem

1.2
and Frobenius density theorem. For completeness, we give a proof of the deduction in
Section

10
. In the statement, a
minimal field extension
refers to an extension having no nontrivial subextensions.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
f(X)\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
be of degree
n
and irreducible in
\mathbb{Q}[X]
. Letting
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
be a root of
f(X)
, assume that
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)/\mathbb{Q}
is a minimal field extension and that it is not Galois. Then, there is a set
\mathcal{P}
of primes of positive density such that for
p\in\mathcal{P}
, the reduction of
f(X)
modulo
p
has at least one root and at most
n^{1/3}
roots in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Let us briefly discuss the proof of the theorem and the layout of the paper. In Section
3
, using the O’Nan-Scott theorem, the proof is reduced to the case where
G
is almost simple. This reduction is not entirely straightforward, and in particular primitive groups of twisted wreath type require some effort (see
Proposition

3.6
). The rest of the paper covers the case where
G
is almost simple. Let
H
be a point-stabilizer, a maximal subgroup of
G
, and write
G/H
for the set of right cosets of
H
in
G
, and
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)
for the number of fixed points of
g
in the action on
G/H
. For
g\in H
we have

(1.1)

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)=|C_{G}(g)|.\frac{|g^{G}\cap H|}{|H|}\leq|C_{G}(g)|

(see Lemma
2.1
), and so the main aim is to find an element
g\in H
for which
|C_{G}(g)|
is small (ideally, less than
|G:H|^{1/3}
). This is achieved in Sections
4
and
5
for alternating and sporadic groups, respectively. Exceptional groups of Lie type are dealt with in Section
6
. The classical groups require the most work, and are handled in the last three sections
7
,
8
and
9
.

As far as the methods of proof are concerned, the starting point is the substantial literature on the maximal subgroups of almost simple groups. For sporadic groups the information is complete (the maximal subgroups have been classified), and it is a fairly routine matter to pick an element
g
such that the right hand side of (
1.1
) is less than
|G:H|^{1/3}
, as required. For exceptional groups of Lie type the maximal subgroups have been classified except when
G
is of type
E_{7}
or
E_{8}
, but it is much less routine to find suitable elements
g
. When
G
is alternating or classical, there are well-known theorems (
[
3
]
and
[
2
]
) that partition the maximal subgroups into classes
\mathcal{C}
of known subgroups, and
\mathcal{S}
of unknown subgroups; the class
\mathcal{S}
consists of almost simple primitive subgroups in alternating groups, and almost simple irreducible subgroups in classical groups. While for alternating groups, the class
\mathcal{S}
does not cause much difficulty (see Lemma
4.6
), this is not the case for classical groups, and the longest and most difficult section of the paper (Section
9
) is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.2
for
G
classical and
H
in the class
\mathcal{S}
. We use a full range of methods and results on the structure, generation and modular representation theory of simple groups to achieve the proof in this case (see
Section

9.1
for a brief outline of the argument).

Let us offer a final word about our use of computation in the proofs, using the computer software GAP (
[
8
]
). If a group
G
and a maximal subgroup
H
are available in GAP, and also the character table of
G
, then for
g\in G
it is a routine matter to compute the number of fixed points
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)
, using the equation (
1.1
). We shall make frequent use of such computations for small simple groups
G
, and we usually suppress details, using phrases such as “we check that
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)<|G:H|^{1/3}
using GAP”.

2.
Preliminaries

We prove a few elementary results on fixed points, and conclude with two lemmas concerning simple groups and a summary of some notation used throughout the paper.
Recall that for a group
G
with a subgroup
H
, we write
G/H
for the set of right cosets of
H
in
G
, and
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)
for the number of fixed points of an element
g
in its action on
G/H
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
G
be a finite group, let
H
be a subgroup of
G
, and let
g\in H
.

(i)

Then

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)=|C_{G}(g)|.\frac{|g^{G}\cap H|}{|H|}\leq|C_{G}(g)|.

(ii)

Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}
be representatives of the
H
-classes in
g^{G}\cap H
. Then

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{H}(g_{i})|}.

Proof.

(i) It is well-known that

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)=\frac{|g^{G}\cap H|}{|g^{G}|}|G:H|.

This can be seen by counting the set
X=\{(Hx,y):x\in G,\,y\in g^{G},\,Hxy=Hx\}
in two ways; we see that
|X|=|g^{G}|.\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)=|G:H|.|g^{G}\cap H|
. Part (i) follows.

(ii) We have

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)=\frac{|g^{G}\cap H|}{|g^{G}|}|G:H|=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{|g_{i}^{H}||G|}{|g^{G}||H|}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{H}(g_{i})|},

as required.
∎

The following variant will sometimes be useful.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
G
be a finite group, let
H
be a subgroup of
G
, let
H_{0}\triangleleft H
and let
g\in H
.

(i)

Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}
be representatives of the
H_{0}
-classes in
g^{G}\cap H
. Then

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)=\frac{1}{|H:H_{0}|}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{H_{0}}(g_{i})|}.

(ii)

If for each coset
C
of
H_{0}
in
H
,
g^{G}\cap C
is either empty or equal to an
H_{0}
-class, and if
g\in g^{G}\cap H
is chosen so that
|C_{H_{0}}(g)|
is minimal, then

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/H)\leq\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{H_{0}}(g)|}.

Proof.

Part (i) follows by a similar argument to the previous lemma. As for (ii), the assumption that
g^{G}\cap C
is either empty or equal to an
H_{0}
-class implies
t\leq|H:H_{0}|
, and so the bound follows immediately from the minimality of
|C_{H_{0}}(g)|
.
∎

We shall need the following result on centralizer orders in simple groups.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
S
be finite non-abelian simple group. Then
S
contains an element
g
such that
|C_{S}(g)|<|S|^{1/3}
.

Proof.

For
S=A_{n}
with
n\geq 6
, we take
g
to be either an
n
-cycle or an
(n-1)
-cycle; and for
S=A_{5}
we take
g
to be a
3
-cycle. For
S
sporadic we use
[
12
]
.

Now suppose
S\in{\rm Lie}(p)
, and let
g\in S
be a regular unipotent element. The order of
C_{S}(g)
can be read off from results in
[
56
]
. For
S
a classical group,
[
56
, Chapters 3,4]
gives
|C_{S}(g)|\leq(2,p)q^{r}
, where
r
is the untwisted Lie rank of
S
; and for
S
of exceptional Lie type,
[
56
, Tables 22.2.1-6]
gives
|C_{S}(g)|\leq(60,p^{2})q^{r}
. It follows that
|C_{S}(g)|<|S|^{1/3}
in all cases except for
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
. Finally, for
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
with
q\geq 7
, we take
g
of order
(q-1)/(2,q-1)
.
∎

The final lemma of this section concerns the orders of simple groups. In the proof, and throughout the rest of the paper, we make use of Zsigmondy’s theorem on primitive prime divisors
[
83
]
: this states that if
q,n
are integers with
q\geq 2
,
n\geq 3
and
(q,n)\neq(2,6)
, then there is a prime number that divides
q^{n}-1
and does not divide
q^{i}-1
for
1\leq i<n
. Such a prime is called a
primitive prime divisor
of
q^{n}-1
, abbreviated as
ppd
, and denoted by
q_{n}
. Observe that
q_{n}\equiv 1\hbox{ mod }n
.

Lemma 2.4
.

If
G
is a finite simple
\{2,3,5\}
-group, then
G
is
A_{5}
,
A_{6}
or
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)
.

Proof.

If
G
is alternating this is clear, and we can verify the result for
G
sporadic by checking the orders of these groups (for example in
[
12
]
).
Assume now that
G
is of Lie type over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. If
G
is not isomorphic to one of

\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q),\,^{2}\!B_{2}(q),\,\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)^{\prime},\,\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2),\,\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(2),\,\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)

then by considering at the order formula for
|G|
, we see that
|G|
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor (ppd) of
q^{i}-1
for some
i\geq 3
with
i\neq 4
. Such a ppd is congruent to
1\pmod{i}
, and hence is at least
7
.

Let us address the remaining groups in the above list. The case
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)
is in the statement, as are
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q),q=4,5,9
and
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)^{\prime},q=2,3
. The groups
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(2),\,\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
, have order divisible by 7. For
G=\,^{2}\!B_{2}(q)
we have
q=2^{2a+1}\geq 8
and
|G|
is divisible by a ppd of
2^{2a+1}-1
.

Assume next
G=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
with
q\geq 4
. If
q=p^{a}
with
a>1
, then
|G|
is divisible by a ppd of
p^{4a}-1
; if
q=p>5
, observe that
p
divides
|G|
; and
|\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(5)|
is divisible by 13.

Assume finally
G=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
with
q=p^{a}
. We can take
p=2,3
or 5 and
a\geq 3,3
or 2 respectively. Then
|G|
is divisible by a ppd of
p^{2a}-1
, or by 7 if
(p,a)=(2,3)
, or by 13 if
(p,a)=(5,2)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Notation

For a prime
p
, we denote by
{\rm Lie}(p)
the set of simple groups of Lie type over a field of characteristic
p
; and
{\rm Lie}(p^{\prime})
is the set
\bigcup_{r\neq p}{\rm Lie}(r)
.

For
\epsilon=\pm
, we use
\mathrm{PSL}_{n}^{\epsilon}(q)
to denote
\mathrm{PSL}_{n}(q)
if
\epsilon=+
, and
\mathrm{PSU}_{n}(q)
if
\epsilon=-
. Similarly,
E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
is
E_{6}(q)
for
\epsilon=+
, and
{}^{2}\!E_{6}(q)
for
\epsilon=-
.

Finally, we remind the reader of our notation for primitive prime divisors (ppds): for
q\geq 2
,
n\geq 3
and
(q,n)\neq(2,6)
, we denote by
q_{n}
a ppd of
q^{n}-1
.

3.
Reduction to simple groups

The heart of the proof of Theorem
1.2
is the case of almost simple groups, for which we shall prove the following slightly stronger result. For
G
a non-abelian finite simple group, denote by

(3.1)

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(G)

the set of subgroups of
G
that extend to a maximal subgroup of some almost simple group with socle
G
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
G
be a non-abelian finite simple group and let
M\in\mathcal{A}
. Then, there exists
g\in M
such that
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

In this section we deduce Theorem
1.2
from this result. After this, the rest of the paper is devoted to proving Theorem
3.1
.

Assume then that Theorem
3.1
holds, and let
G
be a primitive non-regular permutation group of degree
n
on a set
\Omega
. According to the O’Nan-Scott theorem (see for example
[
48
]
),
G
is of one of the following types:

(1)

affine,

(2)

almost simple,

(3)

simple diagonal type,

(4)

product action,

(5)

twisted wreath.

Theorem
1.2
was proved for affine groups in
[
27
]
, and follows from Theorem
3.1
for almost simple groups. The remaining types (3),(4) and (5) are handled in the next three propositions.

Proposition 3.2
.

Theorem
1.2
holds for
G
of simple diagonal type.

Proof.

Assume
G
is of simple diagonal type, and let
H
be a point-stabilizer. Then
G
has socle
N=S^{r}
, where
S
is non-abelian simple,
r\geq 2
,
n=|S|^{r-1}
and
H\cap N
is a diagonal subgroup of
S^{r}
. Then for any
x\in S
, there is an element

g\in N\cap H
fixing exactly
|C_{S}(x)|^{r-1}
points.
By
Lemma

2.3
, we can find
x\in S
such that
|C_{S}(x)|<|S|^{1/3}
, and the corresponding element
g
satisfies
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g)<n^{1/3}
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Theorem
1.2
holds for
G
in product action.

Proof.

In this case we have
G\leq A\wr S_{t}
acting on
\Omega=\Delta^{t}
, where
A
is an almost simple or simple diagonal type primitive group on
\Delta
, and
{\rm Soc}(G)={\rm Soc}(A)^{t}
. Set
m=|\Delta|
. By the proof of Proposition
3.2
for the simple diagonal case, and by Theorem
3.1
for the almost simple case, there exists
x\in{\rm Soc}(A)
such that
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(x,\Delta)<m^{1/3}
. Letting
g=(x,\ldots,x)\in{\rm Soc}(G)
, we then have
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g,\Omega)<m^{t/3}=n^{1/3}
, as required. ∎

The twisted wreath case (5) requires more effort, and we need two preliminary lemmas for this.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
S
be a finite non-abelian simple group. There exists an element
x\in S
of prime order
p\geq 5
such that for any
S
-set of size
n
on which
S
has no fixed points,
the number of orbits of
\langle x\rangle
is at most
n/3
. Moreover, we can choose
x\in S
of prime power order such that the number of orbits of
\langle x\rangle
is less than
n/3
unless either

S=A_{5}
and every orbit of has size
6
or
12
, or
S=A_{6}
and every orbit has size
6
.

Proof.

Note that the result reduces to the case of transitive
S
-sets, and then to the case of primitive
S
-sets. So consider a primitive
S
-set
\Omega
of size
n
, and let
H
be a point-stabilizer.
Let
p\geq 5
be a prime dividing
|S|
. By
[
10
, Thm. 1]
, for any element
x\in S
of order
p
, one of the following holds:

(i)

\mathrm{fix}(x)\leq n/(p+1)
;

(ii)

S=A_{m}
and the action
(S,\Omega)
is on
\ell
-element subsets of
[m]
for some
\ell<m/2
;

(iii)

S
is a classical group, the action
(S,\Omega)
is a subspace action, and
(S,H,x)
are as in
[
10
, Table 6]
.

In case (i),
\langle x\rangle
has at most
2n/(p+1)
orbits, and since
p\geq 5
, this is at most
n/3
, giving the first assertion of the lemma.
Moreover, we get the strict inequality
as we long as we can choose
p>5
, and so by Lemma
2.4
we only need to consider
S=A_{5}
,
A_{6}
or
U_{4}(2)
. In the last case, strict inequality holds for an element of order
5
and in the first two cases, the strict inequality holds for an element of order
5
apart from the case where
n=6
and
S=A_{5}
or
A_{6}
; for
S=A_{5}
, equality also holds for the transitive action of degree 12.

Consider case (iii). Inspection shows that in all but one case, we can choose a different element
x\in S
of order
p
that does not lie in
[
10
, Table 6]
, hence satisfies (i). The exception is
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p+1)
with
p
a Mersenne prime. In this case, for all actions
(S,\Omega)
we have
\mathrm{fix}(x,\Omega)\leq 2n/(p+2)
(with equality for the action on the cosets of a Borel subgroup); for
p\geq 7
, this is at most
2/9
and again,
\langle x\rangle
has at most
n/3
orbits. If
p>7
, strict inequality holds and if
p=7
we choose an element of order
9
and strict inequality holds (for all actions of
S
).

Finally consider case (ii). For
m=5
(resp. 6, 7, 8, 9, 10), we choose an element
x
of order 5 (resp. 5, 7, 7, 9, 9) and check that strict inequality holds.
So assume that
m\geq 11
. We induct on
m
. Let
p
be the largest prime at most
m
; then

p>\frac{2}{3}m+1
(see for example
[
23
]
).
If
p=m
, then an element of order
p
has no fixed points on
k
-sets for any
k
and the result is clear. If
p<m
, then consider
A_{m-1}
acting.
If
A_{m-1}
has no fixed points the result holds by induction. If
A_{m-1}
does have a fixed point, then the action is the usual action on
n=m
points and

x
has
1+(n-p)<n/3
orbits and the result follows.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
S
be a nonabelian simple group and let
T=S^{k}
for some positive integer
k
. Let
T
act on a set
\Omega
of size
n
without fixed points.

(i)

If
S\neq A_{5}
or
A_{6}
, there exists an element
g\in T
such that the number of orbits of
\langle g\rangle
is less than
n/3
.

(ii)

If
S=A_{5}
or
A_{6}
, then there exists an element
g\in T
of order
5
such that
one of the following holds:

(a)

the number of orbits of
\langle g\rangle
is less than
n/3
;

(b)

each
T
-orbit has size
6
or
12
for
n=5
, or
6
for
n=6
; in particular, the kernel of
T
on each orbit contains all but one component of
T
.

Proof.

We take the element
g=(x,\ldots,x)
where
x\in S
is the element given
in the previous lemma. It suffices to consider the case that
T
acts transitively (and faithfully). Let
H
be a point stabilizer.

Suppose first that the projection of

H
into at least two of the components of
T
is non-surjective; say these projections are
M_{1}<S
and
M_{2}<S
. We can replace
H
by its overgroup
M_{1}\times M_{2}\times S^{k-2}
, so that the action is that of
S^{2}
on the cosets of
M_{1}\times M_{2}
. For
i=1,2
write
n_{i}=|S:M_{i}|
. Let
f_{i}
be proportion of fixed points of
x
on
S/M_{i}
. If
f_{1}f_{2}=0
then
g
is a derangement of odd prime power order at least
5
, so the number of orbits of
\langle g\rangle
is less than
n/3
. Assume then
f_{1}f_{2}\neq 0
, so
f_{1}f_{2}<f_{1}
.
If
|x|
is prime, it follows at once that the number of orbits of
\langle g\rangle
divided by
n
is strictly less than the number of orbits of
\langle x\rangle
on
S/M_{1}
divided by
n_{1}
, and we are done by
Lemma

3.4
. Assume now
|x|
is not prime; by inspecting the proof of
Lemma

3.4
, we have
|x|=9
(and
S=A_{9},A_{10},\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(8)
) and the number of orbits of
\langle x\rangle
on
S/M_{1}
is strictly less than
n_{1}/3
. In particular, we see that the number of orbits of
\langle g\rangle
is strictly less than
(n_{1}/3)n_{2}=n/3
, and we are done.

If the projection of
H
is not surjective for exactly one component of
T
, the result follows from Lemma
3.4
(and this is where equality can hold).

Finally, suppose all projections are surjective. We show strict inequality in this case. Replacing
H
by a maximal subgroup containing it, we are reduced to the case
k=2
and
H\cong S
is a diagonal subgroup. In this case, we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,\Omega)=|C_{S}(x)|
. We claim that
|C_{S}(x)|\leq|S|/12=n/12
. This is clear if
S
has no subgroup of index at most
12
. Otherwise,
S
embeds in
S_{12}
and one can compute the list of such simple groups and check the claim directly. (Note that equality can occur for
x
of order
5
in
A_{5}
). Exclude the cases where
x
is an element
of order
9
(i.e.
S=A_{9},A_{10}
or
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(8)
– see the proof of
Lemma

3.4
).
Thus
\mathrm{fix}(g,\Omega)\leq n/12
, and so the number of orbits of
\langle g\rangle
is at most

n/12+11n/12p\leq(4/15)n
and the result follows. In the three cases remaining, one computes directly that the result holds.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
G
be a primitive group of twisted wreath type on a set of size
n
. Then there exists an element
g\in G
such that
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g)<n^{1/3}
. In particular, Theorem
1.2
holds for
G
of twisted wreath type.

Proof.

Let
G
be a primitive group of twisted wreath type on a set
\Omega
of degree
n
.
We use information on the structure of these groups from
[
3
, Section 7]
(see also
[
5
]
). The socle
E
of
G
is a regular normal subgroup and is the unique minimal normal subgroup of
G
.
Moreover,
E\cong D^{t}
, where
D
is a non-abelian simple group and
t\geq 6
. If
H
is a point-stabilizer, then the following hold
[
3
, Theorem C, (7.1)]
:

(i)

G=EH
,
H\cap E=1
and
H
acts transitively and faithfully on the set
\Delta
of components of
E
;

(ii)

F^{*}(H)
is a direct product of isomorphic nonabelian simple groups.

We have
n=[G:H]=|E|
. Identifying
G/H
with
E
, we need to produce
an element
g\in H
with
|C_{E}(g)|<n^{1/3}
.

Let
A
be a minimal normal subgroup of
H
with
A=S^{e}
for some nonabelian simple group
S
and positive integer
e
.
No orbit of
A
on
\Delta
is trivial and so by Lemma
3.5
, there
exists
g\in A
(namely
g=(x,\ldots,x)
with
x
as in Lemma
3.4
) such that the number of orbits of
\langle g\rangle
on
\Delta
is at most
t/3
. Then
|C_{E}(g)|\leq|E|^{1/3}
. Moreover, unless
S\cong A_{5}
or
A_{6}
, the inequality is strict.

It remains to consider the cases where
S=A_{5}
or
A_{6}
and equality holds. Let
g
be as above. Every
A
-orbit has size
6
, or possibly
12
for
S=A_{5}
.
We see that
\Delta
is the disjoint union of the nontrivial orbits for each of the components of
A
. Since
A
has no fixed points, it follows that
A
is the unique minimal normal subgroup of
H
, and so
H
acts faithfully on
A
by conjugation.

Let
D
be a component of
E
. In this paragraph we will show that either the statement of the proposition holds or
N_{A}(D)=C_{A}(D)
. By
[
3
, Theorem C(1)]
,
N_{H}(D)/C_{H}(D)
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
containing all inner automorphisms. Since
N_{A}(D)/C_{A}(D)

is a normal subgroup of
N_{H}(D)/C_{H}(D)
, it follows that if
N_{A}(D)\neq C_{A}(D)
then
N_{A}(D)/C_{A}(D)
contains a subgroup inducing the full group of inner automorphisms of
D
. Assume this is the case.
By the choice of
g
, we have
|C_{E}(g)|=|E|^{1/3}
and
\langle g\rangle
has precisely
t/3
orbits on the components of
E
. In particular,
g
centralizes each component it normalizes. Let
S_{1}
be the unique component of
A
not normalizing
D
. Then
N_{A}(D)=M\times C
, where
M=N_{S_{1}}(D)
and
C
is the product of all components of
A
different from
S_{1}
.
Note that if
S=A_{6}
then
M\cong A_{5}
, and if
S=A_{5}
then
M\cong D_{10}
or
C_{5}
. We claim that the action of
N_{A}(D)
on
D
factors through one component of
N_{A}(D)
. If this is the case, then a diagonal element
g
of
A
of order
5
acts nontrivially on
D
, against our assumption. If
M\cong A_{5}
then
N_{A}(D)
is perfect, so
N_{A}(D)/C_{A}(D)\cong D
, which implies the claim (since a normal subgroup of
N_{A}(D)
is a product of components). If
M\cong D_{10}
or
C_{5}
, then the image of
M
in
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
is a solvable subgroup normalized by
D
. Such image must be trivial, that is,
M
centralizes
D
. In particular, also in this case
N_{A}(D)/C_{A}(D)\cong D
and the claim holds.

Assume therefore that
N_{A}(D)=C_{A}(D)
.
It follows that a section of
N_{H}(D)/C_{A}(D)\cong N_{H}(D)A/A
induces all inner automorphisms on
D
, and in particular

H/A
is not solvable. Thus, we can choose an
\ell
-element
w\in H\smallsetminus A
, where

\ell
is an odd prime and
\ell\neq 5
.

Since the outer automorphism group of
S
is a
2
-group, by viewing
w
as an element of
\mathrm{Aut}(A)\cong\mathrm{Aut}(S)\wr S_{e}
we see that there exists
a\in A
such that
wa
centralizes a diagonal element
g^{\prime}
of
A
of order
5
, and such that
wa
is an
\ell
-element. Now replace
w
by
wa
and
g
by
g^{\prime}
, and look at
z:=wg
, so
|C_{E}(z)|\leq|C_{E}(g)|\leq|E|^{1/3}
. It remains to prove that the inclusion
C_{E}(z)<C_{E}(g)
is strict. The inclusion is strict if
z
does not have the same orbits as
g
on components of
E
, so assume that the orbits of
z
and
g
are the same. This implies that
w
normalizes each component of
E
, and so
w
does not centralize some component of
E
, say
L
. If
g
normalizes
L
, then by the above argument
g
centralizes
L
, and so
C_{E}(z)<C_{E}(g)
. If
g
does not normalize
L
, the
g
-orbit of
L
(in the action on
\Delta
) has size
5
, so
B:=\prod_{i}L^{g^{i}}\cong L^{5}
. We have that
C_{B}(g)
is a diagonal subgroup isomorphic to
L
. Then
w
does not centralize
C_{B}(g)
, so
C_{E}(z)<C_{E}(g)
and this concludes the proof.
∎

4.
Alternating groups

The rest of the paper is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.1
. Recall that
G
is a non-abelian simple group and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(G)
is the class of subgroups of
G
defined in (
3.1
). In this section we handle the case where
G
is an alternating group.

The cases where
G=A_{5}
or
A_{6}
are easily handled by computations using the information on these groups in
[
12
]
. So assume
G=A_{n}
with
n\geq 7
. Denote by
[n]
the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. According to the theorem in
[
3
, Appendix]
, the subgroups
M\in\mathcal{A}
can be divided into six classes, as follows:

(i)

intransitive subgroups: here
M=(S_{k}\times S_{n-k})\cap G
, where
1\leq k<n/2
;

(ii)

transitive and imprimitive subgroups: here
M=(S_{k}\wr S_{t})\cap G
, where
n=kt
and
1<t<n
;

(iii)

affine subgroups:
M=\mathrm{AGL}_{d}(p)\cap G
, where
n=p^{d}
,
p
prime;

(iv)

product action subgroups:
M=(S_{k}\wr S_{t})\cap G
, where
n=k^{t}
with
k\geq 5
,
t\geq 2
;

(v)

diagonal action subgroups:
M=(S^{t}.(\mathrm{Out}(S)\times S_{t}))\cap G
, where
S
is non-abelian simple,
t\geq 2
and
n=|S|^{t-1}
;

(vi)

almost simple primitive subgroups
M
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is intransitive.

Proof.

Let
M=(S_{k}\times S_{n-k})\cap G
, where
1\leq k<n/2
. If
n
is even, we choose
g\in M
with cycle type
(k,n-k)
; if
n
is odd and
k\neq 1
, we choose

g\in M
with cycle type
(1,k-1,n-k)
; and if
n
is odd and
k=1
, we choose

g\in M
with cycle type
(1,2,n-3)
. In all cases
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
and the conclusion holds.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is transitive and imprimitive.

Proof.

Assume
M=(S_{k}\wr S_{t})\cap G
, where
n=kt
and
1<t<n
. If
n
is odd, we choose
g
an
n
-cycle, and then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
(since
g
must permute the blocks transitively and has a unique cycle). If
n
is even and
k\neq 2
, we choose
g\in M
with cycle type
(1,1,k-2,n-k)
, and we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
: indeed, since
k-2\neq n-k
, the two fixed points must necessarily belong to the same block of any
k^{t}
-partition fixed by
g
, and then the
(k-2)
-cycle must preserve that block, and the other blocks are determined as
g
acts as a cycle on their points. Finally, if
n
is even and
k=2
, we choose
g\in M
with cycle type
(n-2,2)
. Since
t>2
, the
2
-cycle must necessarily stabilize a block, and the remaining blocks are determined since
g
acts as a cycle on their points, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is an affine subgroup.

Proof.

Let
M=\mathrm{AGL}_{d}(p)\cap G
, where
n=p^{d}
,
p
prime. Suppose
p=2
, and choose

g\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(2)<M
a Singer cycle, which acts
[n]
as an
(n-1)
-cycle. The number of
M
-classes contained in
g^{G}\cap M
is half the number of classes of Singer cycles in
\mathrm{GL}_{d}(2)
, that is,
\phi(2^{d}-1)/(2d)
, where
\phi
is Euler’s totient function. (We have half the classes since
M
contains representatives for both
G
-classes of
(n-1)
-cycles.)
Moreover,
C_{G}(g)=C_{M}(g)=\langle g\rangle
, and so by
Lemma

2.1

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{\phi(2^{d}-1)}{2d}<\left(\frac{(2^{d})!}{2^{d}|\mathrm{GL}_{d}(2)|}\right)^{1/3}=|G:M|^{1/3}.

Assume now that
p>2
, and recall that
n\geq 7
.
Let
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(p)
be the square of a Singer cycle, so
g
acts on
n
points as the product of two disjoint
(n-1)/2
-cycles. Then the number of
M
-classes contained in
g^{G}\cap M
is equal to the number of classes of
\mathrm{GL}_{d}(p)
containing squares of Singer cycles, which is
\phi((p^{d}-1)/2)/d
. Moreover, for each
x\in g^{G}\cap M
we have
|C_{G}(x)|=(n-1)^{2}/4
and
C_{M}(x)=\langle x\rangle
, so by
Lemma

2.1

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{\phi((p^{d}-1)/2)\cdot(p^{d}-1)}{2d}<\left(\frac{(p^{d})!}{p^{d}|\mathrm{GL}_{d}(p)|}\right)^{1/3}=|G:M|^{1/3}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is a product action subgroup.

Proof.

In this case we have
n=k^{t}
where
k\geq 5
,
t>1
, and
M=(S_{k}\wr S_{t})\cap G
.
Let
\delta=0
(resp. 1) if
k
is odd (resp. even), let
x\in A_{k}
be a
(k-\delta)
-cycle, and let
g=(x,x,\ldots,x)\in A_{k}^{t}<M
. Then
x
fixes
\delta
points on
[n]
and has all other cycles of length
k-\delta
. If
k
is odd, then
|C_{G}(g)|=(n/k)!k^{n/k}/2=(k^{t-1})!k^{k^{t-1}}/2
, and one checks that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
.
The case
k
even is similar.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is a diagonal action subgroup.

Proof.

In this case we have
M=(S^{t}.(\mathrm{Out}(S)\times S_{t}))\cap G
, where
S
is a non-abelian simple group,
t\geq 2
, and
n=|S|^{t-1}
. If
g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{t})\in S^{t}
, then the action of
g
is given by the formula

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t-1})^{g}=(g_{t}^{-1}x_{1}g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}^{-1}x_{t-1}g_{t-1})

for
x_{i}\in S
.
In particular, the subgroup of
S^{t}
given by
g_{t}=1
acts regularly on
[n]
. For
i<t
, choose
g_{i}\in S
of order
r\geq 5
and put
g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{t-1},1)
, so
|C_{G}(g)|=r^{n/r}(n/r)!/2\leq n^{n/r}/2\leq n^{n/5}/2
. We then check that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is an almost simple primitive subgroup.

Proof.

The almost simple primitive groups of degree
n\leq 26
are all in the GAP library, and we can check these cases computationally (for example, for
n\geq 10
we can find
g\in M
with
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
).

Assume then
n\geq 27
, and let
S
be the socle of
M
.

If
S
is one of
A_{5}
,
A_{6}
, or
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)
, we again use GAP; note that in these cases, since
n\geq 27
we have
n\in\{36,40,45\}
, and as above we find
g\in M
with
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Assume that
S
is not one of
A_{5}
,
A_{6}
, or
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)
, and assume furthermore
S
is not
A_{m}
with
n=\binom{m}{\ell}
in the action on
\ell
-subsets; we will consider this case at the end of the proof.

By
Lemma

2.4
, there exists
g\in S
of prime order
r\geq 7
. In some cases, we make a specific choice, as follows. If
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{n}(q)
with
n\geq 3
, take
g
semisimple with
r
not dividing
q-1
, and if
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{n}(q)
with
n\geq 6
, take
g
semisimple (see the proof of Lemma
2.4
);
if
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
with
q\geq 7
prime, take
g
regular unipotent. We then apply
[
10
, Theorem 1]
. The specific choices make sure we are not in one of the exceptions in
[
10
, Table 6]
, and so we deduce that
g
fixes at most
n/(r+1)
points on
[n]
. Letting
c=\mathrm{fix}(g,[n])
, we have

|C_{S_{n}}(g)|=c!((n-c)/r)!r^{(n-c)/r}\leq c^{c}(n-c)^{(n-c)/r}=:f(c),

with the convention
0^{0}=1
.
Now looking at the derivative of
\ln(f(c))

we see that, for fixed
r
,
f(c)
is decreasing up to a certain value, and then it is increasing. In particular, the maximum
m
of
f(c)
is attained either when
c
is as small as possible, or when
c
is as large as possible. Assume first
c
is as large as possible; since
c\leq n/8
we have

m\leq f(n/8)=(n/8)^{n/8}(7n/8)^{7n/(8r)}\leq n^{n/4}(7/64)^{n/8},

since
r\geq 7
.
Next, by
[
69
]
we have

n!\geq\sqrt{2\pi}n^{n+1/2}e^{-n},

and for
n\geq 25
we have
|M|<n^{1+\log_{2}(n)}
by
[
64
, Theorem 1.1]
. Therefore

|G:M|>\sqrt{\pi/2}n^{n+1/2}e^{-n}n^{-1-\log_{2}(n)}.

Now we have

\displaystyle n^{3n/4}(7/64)^{3n/8}<\sqrt{\pi/2}n^{n+1/2}e^{-n}n^{-1-\log_{2}(n)}

\displaystyle\quad\iff\sqrt{\pi/2}\cdot n^{n/4}>(7e^{8/3}/64)^{3n/8}n^{1/2+\log_{2}(n)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\Longleftarrow 1.2\cdot n^{n/4}>(1.58)^{3n/8}n^{1/2+\log_{2}(n)},

which holds since we assumed
n\geq 27
. Assume now the maximum
m
of
f(c)
is attained when
c
is as small as possible. Since
c\geq 0
, we have

m\leq f(0)=n^{n/r}\leq n^{n/7},

since
r\geq 7
. But for
n\geq 14
we have
n^{n/7}<n^{n/4}(7/64)^{n/8}
, hence the conclusion holds by the work done already.

The only remaining case is
n=\binom{m}{\ell}
and
S=A_{m}
acting on
\ell
-subsets with
1<\ell\leq m/2
. Then choose
g\in S
an
m
-cycle or an
(m-1)
-cycle, so
g
fixes no point on
[n]
, and we conclude with a similar calculation as above.
∎

5.
Sporadic groups

In this section we prove Theorem
3.1
for primitive groups with socle a sporadic simple group. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{A}(G)
in (
3.1
).

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
G
be a sporadic simple group and let
M\in\mathcal{A}(G)
. Then there exists
g\in M
such that
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

The proof consists largely of routine inspection of tables of maximal subgroups of sporadic groups, found in
[
12
]
together with a few other references for some of the larger groups.
Let
G
be as in Theorem
5.1
, and let
M\in\mathcal{A}(G)
.

In the proof, for each such subgroup
M
of
G
, we shall specify an element
g\in M
by its class name in
[
12
]
. For some subgroups, the permutation character
1_{M}^{G}
is given in
[
12
]
, so we can directly compute the value of
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)
; for the other cases, we use the following consequence of Lemma
2.1
:

(5.1)

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|\cdot|g^{G}\cap M|}{|M|}\leq\frac{|C_{G}(g)|\cdot i_{r}(M)}{|M|},

where
r=|g|
and
i_{r}(M)
denotes the number of elements of order
r
in
M
.

5.1.
G=M_{11}

In this case the permutation character
1_{M}^{G}
is given in
[
12
]
for all maximal subgroups, and our choices for
g
are as follows.

\begin{array}[]{l|lllll}M&M_{10}&L_{2}(11)&M_{9}.2&S_{5}&M_{8}.S_{3}\\
\hline\cr g&5A&11A&3A&5A&4A\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&1&1&1&1\end{array}

5.2.
G=M_{12}

Our choices for
g
are as follows; the permutation character
1_{M}^{G}
is given in
[
12
]
for all but the last four maximal subgroups, in which cases (
5.1
) is used.

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllllllll}M&M_{11}&M_{10}.2&L_{2}(11)^{(1)}&M_{9}.S_{3}&2\times S_{5}&M_{8}.S_{4}&4^{2}.D_{12}&A_{4}\times S_{3}&L_{2}(11)^{(2)}&A_{5}&3^{1+2}.4\\
\hline\cr g&11A&5A&11A&3B&5A&4A&4A&6A&11A&5A&3B\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&1&1&4&1&2&3&\leq 5&1&4&\leq 9\end{array}

5.3.
G=M_{22}

The permutation character
1_{M}^{G}
is given in
[
12
]
for all maximal subgroups:

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllll}M&L_{3}(4)&2^{4}.A_{6}&A_{7}&2^{4}.S_{5}&2^{3}.L_{3}(2)&M_{10}&L_{2}(11)\\
\hline\cr g&7A&5A&5A&5A&7A&5A&11A\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&2&1&1&1&1&1\end{array}

5.4.
G=M_{23}

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllll}M&M_{22}&L_{3}(4).2&2^{4}.A_{7}&A_{8}&M_{11}&2^{6}.(3\times A_{5}).2&23.11\\
\hline\cr g&11A&7A&7A&7A&11A&15A&23A\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&1&1&2&1&1&1\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllllll}M&M_{23}&M_{22}.2&2^{4}.A_{8}&M_{12}.2&2^{6}.3S_{6}&L_{3}(4).S_{3}&2^{6}.(L_{3}(2)\times S_{3})&L_{2}(23)&L_{2}(7)\\
\hline\cr g&23A&11A&7A&11A&5A&7A&7A&23A&7A\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&1&3&1&1&1&1&1&6\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllll}M&L_{2}(11)&2^{3}.7.3&2\times A_{5}&19.6&11.10&D_{6}\times D_{10}&7.6\\
\hline\cr g&11A&7A&5A&19A&11A&5A&7A\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&2&2&3&1&1&1&1\end{array}

5.7.
G=J_{2}

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllllll}M&U_{3}(3)&3.A_{6}.2&2^{1+4}.A_{5}&[2^{6}].3.S_{3}&A_{4}\times A_{5}&A_{5}\times D_{10}&L_{3}(2).2&5^{2}.D_{12}&A_{5}\\
\hline\cr g&7A&3A&5C&6A&15A&15A&7A&5C&5C\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&2&1&5&6&2&1&1&\leq 4&\leq 20\end{array}

5.8.
G=HS

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllllllll}M&M_{22}&U_{3}(5).2&L_{3}(4).2&S_{8}&[5^{3}.2^{4}]&2^{4}.S_{6}&4^{3}.L_{3}(2)&M_{11}&[2^{6}].S_{5}&A_{6}.[2^{3}]&5.4\times A_{5}\\
\hline\cr g&11A&5C&7A&7A&5C&6B&7A&11A&10A&10B&10B\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&1&1&1&1&\leq 10&2&1&\leq 8&\leq 20&\leq 20\end{array}

5.9.
G=J_{3}

\begin{array}[]{l|lllllllll}M&L_{2}(16).2&L_{2}(19)&19.9&[2^{4}.3].A_{5}&L_{2}(17)&(3\times A_{6}).2&[3^{5}].8&2^{1+4}.A_{5}&[2^{6}].(3\times S_{3})\\
\hline\cr g&17A&19A&19A&15A&17A&15A&9A&10A&4A\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&2&1&1&\leq 6&1&\leq 6&\leq 3&\leq 4&\leq 21\end{array}

5.10.
G=McL

\begin{array}[]{l|llllllllll}M&U_{4}(3)&M_{22}&U_{3}(5)&3^{1+4}.2S_{5}&3^{4}.M_{10}&L_{3}(4).2&2A_{8}&2^{4}.A_{7}&M_{11}&5^{1+2}.3.8\\
\hline\cr g&7A&11A&7A&10A&9A&7A&7A&7A&11A&8A\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&1&2&\leq 12&\leq 3&1&1&1&1&\leq 4\end{array}

From now on, the lists of maximal subgroups become longer than the ones seen already, and rather than give a full table, we just summarise our conclusions.

5.11.
G=He

Here we need to use the permutation character or the bound (
5.1
) for the following four maximal subgroups:

\begin{array}[]{l|llll}M&Sp_{4}(4).2&2^{2}.L_{3}(4).S_{3}&7^{2}.SL_{2}(7)&7^{1+2}.(S_{3}\times 3)\\
\hline\cr g&17A&15A&7D&7D\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{fix}(g)&1&2&\leq 14&1\end{array}

For the remaining eight classes of maximal subgroups, namely
(S_{5}\times S_{5}).2
,
2^{6}.3S_{6}
,

,

,

,

,

, we can choose elements
g\in M

|C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G:M|
.

.

.

This completes the proof of Theorem

.

6.
Exceptional groups

In this section we prove Theorem
3.1
for primitive groups with socle an exceptional group of Lie type.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
G
be a simple group of exceptional Lie type and let
M\in\mathcal{A}(G)
. Then there exists
g\in M
such that
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

The proof is divided into several subsections.

6.1.
Maximal subgroups of exceptional groups

Let
G
be a simple group of exceptional Lie type over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, where
q=p^{a}
and
p
is prime. There is a simple adjoint adjoint algebraic group
\bar{G}
over
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, and a Frobenius endomorphism
F
of
\bar{G}
, such that
G=(\bar{G}^{F})^{\prime}
.

The next result, taken from
[
52
]
, together with the remarks following it, summarizes the current state of knowledge of the maximal subgroups of finite exceptional groups. In part (4) of the statement, by a subgroup of the same type we mean the normalizer of a subfield subgroup
G(q_{0})
(where
\mathbb{F}_{q_{0}}\subset\mathbb{F}_{q}
), or of a twisted version (explicitly:
{}^{2}\!E_{6}(q^{1/2})<E_{6}(q)
,
{}^{2}\!F_{4}(q)<F_{4}(q)
,
{}^{2}\!G_{2}(q)<G_{2}(q)
); such subgroups are unique up to conjugacy in
\bar{G}^{F}
, by
[
53
, Thm. 5.1]
.

Theorem 6.2
.

(
[
52
, Thm. 8]
)

Let
G
be a simple group of exceptional Lie type over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
q=p^{a}
, and let
M\in\mathcal{A}(G)
. Then one of the following holds.

(1)

M
is a parabolic subgroup.

(2)

M=N_{G}(\bar{M}^{F}\cap G)
, where
\bar{M}<\bar{G}
is connected reductive of maximal rank: the possibilities are listed in
[
57
, Tables 5.1,5.2]
.

(3)

M=N_{G}(\bar{M}^{F}\cap G)
, where
\bar{M}
is connected reductive of non-maximal rank: these are listed in
[
52
, Table 3]
, together with the following subgroups:

\begin{array}[]{l}G_{2}(q),\,\mathrm{PGL}_{3}^{\pm}(q)<\,^{3}\!D_{4}(q),\\
(2^{2}\times\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(q).2^{2}).S_{3},\;^{3}\!D_{4}(q)<E_{7}(q)\,(p\neq 2),\\
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(q)\times S_{5}<E_{8}(q)\,(p>5),\\
F_{4}(q)<E_{8}(q)\,(p=3).\end{array}

(4)

M
is of the same type as
G
.

(5)

M
is an ‘exotic local’ subgroup, or the ‘Borovik’ subgroup
(Alt_{5}\times Alt_{6}).2^{2}<E_{8}(q)
; the exotic locals are as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}2^{3}.\mathrm{SL}_{3}(2)<G_{2}(p)\;(p>2),\\
3^{3}.\mathrm{SL}_{3}(3)<F_{4}(p)\;(p>5),\\
3^{3+3}.\mathrm{SL}_{3}(3)<E_{6}^{\epsilon}(p)\;(p\equiv\epsilon\hbox{ mod }3),\\
5^{3}.\mathrm{SL}_{3}(5)<E_{8}(q)\;(q=p\hbox{ or }p^{2}),\\
2^{5+10}.\mathrm{SL}_{5}(2)<E_{8}(p)\;(p>2).\end{array}

(6)

M
is in a class
{\mathcal{U}}
of almost simple subgroups, not occurring in items (1)-(5); we divide these into subclasses, according to the socle
M_{0}
of
M
:

(a)

{\mathcal{U}}_{p,1}
:
M_{0}\in Lie(p),\,M_{0}\neq\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{a})

(b)

{\mathcal{U}}_{p,2}
:
M_{0}\in Lie(p),\,M_{0}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{a})

(c)

{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
:
M_{0}
alternating, sporadic or in
Lie(p^{\prime})
.

Note that in part (3), the last subgroup
F_{4}(q)<E_{8}(q)
with
q=3^{a}
was found in
[
18
]
(having been omitted in error from
[
52
, Table 3]
and the references cited for it).

For
G\neq E_{7}(q),E_{8}(q)
, the class
{\mathcal{U}}
is known (see
[
14
,
37
,
38
,
62
,
79
]
); and for
E_{7}(q)
,
E_{8}(q)
, while the class is not known completely, considerable restrictions on the groups in
{\mathcal{U}}_{p,1}
,
{\mathcal{U}}_{p,2}
are obtained in
[
15
,
16
]
, and on the groups in
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
in
[
13
,
60
]
.

6.2.
Parabolic subgroups

Here we prove Theorem
6.1
in the case where a point-stabilizer
M
is a parabolic subgroup. This follows from the next lemma, using the existence of regular unipotent elements.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
G
be a simple group of Lie type in characteristic
p
, and let
P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
g\in G
be a regular unipotent element. Then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/P)=1
.

Proof.

Let
B
be a Borel subgroup of
P
and
U=O_{p}(B)
, so that
U
is a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G
. We may take
g\in U
. If
g\in P^{x}
with
x\in G
, then there exists
y\in P
such that
g\in U^{yx}
. Since
g
is regular unipotent, it lies in precisely one conjugate of
U
, so
U=U^{yx}
. Therefore
yx\in N_{S}(U)=B\leq P
, so
P^{x}=P
. Thus
g
lies in precisely one conjugate of
P
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

6.3.
Subgroups of maximal rank

In this subsection we prove Theorem
6.1
in the case where a point-stabilizer
M
is a subgroup of maximal rank – that is, a maximal subgroup as in part (2) of Theorem
6.2
. Recall from Lemma
2.1
that for
g\in G
, we have

(6.1)

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|\cdot|g^{G}\cap M|}{|M|}.

In almost all cases, we shall find an element
g\in M
such that

(6.2)

|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3},

which of course suffices to give the conclusion of Theorem
6.1
.

We first handle the families of rank at most 4.

Lemma 6.4
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the cases where
M
is a subgroup of maximal rank and
G
is of type
{}^{2}\!B_{2}
,
{}^{2}\!G_{2}
,
{}^{3}\!D_{4}
,
{}^{2}\!F_{4}
,
G_{2}
or
F_{4}
.

Proof.

Recall that
M
is as in
[
57
, Tables 5.1,5.2]
.

Assume first that
G=\,^{2}\!B_{2}(q)
, with
q=2^{2a+1}
and
a\geq 1
. Then
M
is a torus normalizer
(q-1).2
or
(q\pm\sqrt{2q}+1).4
. Taking
g\in M
of order
q-1
or
q\pm\sqrt{2q}+1
respectively, we have
C_{G}(g)\leq M
and
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
by (
6.1
).

Next assume that
G=\,^{2}\!G_{2}(q)
with
q=3^{2a+1}
and
a\geq 1
. Then
M
is either
2\times L_{2}(q)
or a torus normalizer
(q+1).6
or
(q\pm\sqrt{3q}+1).6
. In the first case, an element
g\in M
of order
(q-1)/2
is regular semisimple, and
|C_{G}(g)|=q-1<|G:M|^{1/3}
, giving the conclusion. In the other cases, we choose
g\in M
of order
q+1
or
q\pm\sqrt{3q}+1
, and then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
as in the previous paragraph.

Now assume that
G=G_{2}(q)
with
q>2
. Then
M
is
(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q)\circ\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q)).(2,q-1)
,
\mathrm{SL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q).2
(with
\epsilon=\pm
) or a torus normalizer. In the first case, we choose
g\in M
of order
q^{2}-1
, and then

|C_{G}(g)|=q^{2}-1<|G:M|^{1/3}|
. In the second case, choose
g\in M
of order
q^{2}+\epsilon q+1
;
then
C_{G}(g)\leq M
and
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.
Finally, suppose
M
is a torus normalizer. Then
q=3^{a}\geq 9
and
M
is
(q\pm 1)^{2}.D_{12}
or
(q^{2}\pm q+1).6
, and we can choose
g\in M
of order
q\pm 1
or
q^{2}\pm q+1
such that
g
is regular semisimple; then
C_{G}(g)|=(q\pm 1)^{2}
or
q^{2}\pm q+1
, which is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Next let
G=\,^{3}\!D_{4}(q)
. Inspecting the semisimple classes in
[
37
, Table II]
, we see that
M
contains a regular semisimple element unless
q=2
, and

M=\mathrm{SU}_{3}(2).3.2
or
3^{2}.\mathrm{SL}_{2}(3)
. When
M
contains a regular semisimple element
g
, we have
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q^{2}+q+1)^{2}
(the size of the largest maximal torus), and this is less than

|G:M|^{1/3}
in all cases.
In the exceptional cases with
q=2
and
M=\mathrm{SU}_{3}(2).3.2
or
3^{2}.\mathrm{SL}_{2}(3)
, we pick
g\in M
of order 9 or 6 respectively, and then
|C_{G}(g)|\leq 54
or 72 (see
[
12
, p.90]
), and again
this is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Next, consider the case
G=\,^{2}\!F_{4}(q)^{\prime}
, where
q=2^{2a+1}
. For
a\geq 1
, the semisimple classes of
G
are listed in
[
74
, Table IV]
, and we see that each choice of
M
contains a regular semisimple element
g
. (Indeed, for
q>2
the only maximal tori not containing a regular semisimple element are
(q-1)^{2}
and
(q-\sqrt{2q}+1)^{2}
, only for
q=8
.) Hence
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q+\sqrt{2q}+1)^{2}

(the size of the largest maximal torus), and again this is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
in all cases.
To conclude, suppose
a=0
(so
G=\,^{2}\!F_{4}(2)^{\prime}
). The maximal subgroups are listed in
[
12
, p.74]
, and those of maximal rank are just
A_{6}.2^{2}
and
5^{2}.4A_{4}
. Both of these have an element
g
of order 6, and
|C_{G}(g)|=12
, which is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Finally, assume that
G=F_{4}(q)
.
Suppose first that
q>2
. Inspecting the semisimple classes of
G
in
[
73
,
75
]
, we see that
M
contains a regular semisimple element
g
, unless possibly we are in one of the following cases:

(i)

M=(q-1)^{4}.W(F_{4})
with
q=8
;

(ii)

M=(q+1)^{4}.W(F_{4})
with
q=4,8
.

If
M
contains a regular semisimple element
g
, then
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q+1)^{4}
(the size of the largest maximal torus), and this is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
for all possibilities for
M
in
[
57
, Tables 5.1,5.2]
.
In case (i) above, there is an element
g
in the maximal torus
7^{4}
(namely, the element
h_{7}
in
[
73
, Table II]
), such that
|C_{G}(g)|=|\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q)|^{2}(q-1)^{2}
, and this is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
. Likewise, in case (ii) there is an element
g=h_{17}
in the maximal torus
(q+1)^{4}
with centralizer order
|\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q)\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q)|(q+1)
, and this is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Now suppose
q=2
, so
G=F_{4}(2)
. The conjugacy classes and centralizer orders in
G
can be found in
[
12
, p.167-8]
. The maximal rank subgroups
B_{4}(2)
,
B_{2}(4)
,
{}^{3}\!D_{4}(2)
possess elements of orders 17,17,21 respectively, with the same centralizer orders; the subgroups
D_{4}(2)
,
B_{2}(2)^{2}
have an element of order 15 with centralizer order 90; the subgroup
3.(^{2}\!A_{2}(2))^{2}
has an element of order 9 with centralizer order 54; and the subgroup
7^{2}.(3\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(3)
has an element of order 7 with centralizer order 1176. In all cases the centralizer order is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
, completing the proof.
∎

In the proof of the next lemma, we use standard notation
\lambda_{i}
for fundamental dominant weights, and denote by
V(\lambda)
(or just
\lambda
) the irreducible module over
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
of highest weight
\lambda
for the simply connected cover of
G
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the cases where
M
is a subgroup of maximal rank and
G=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
.

Proof.

The semisimple element centralizers in
G
are given in
[
65
,
20
]
, and the unipotent element centralizers in
[
56
, Table 22.2.3]
.

Suppose first that
M
is a torus normalizer in
[
57
, Table 5.2]
,
so that
M=((q-\epsilon)^{6}/d).W(E_{6})
, where
d=(3,q-\epsilon)
and also
q\geq 5
if
\epsilon=+
. Choose an element
x\in M
projecting to an element of order 5 in
W(E_{6})
. In the root space decomposition

L(\bar{G})=H\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi}L_{\alpha}

of the Lie algebra of
\bar{G}
, the element
x
permutes the 72 root spaces
L_{\alpha}
as a permutation of cycle-shape
(5^{14},1^{2})
, and fixes at most a 2-dimensional subspace of the Cartan subspace
H
. Hence

\dim C_{L(\bar{G})}(x)\leq 18
, and it follows that
\dim C_{\bar{G}}(x)\leq 18
also. Inspecting the list of possible centralizers of such dimension in
[
65
,
20
,
56
]
, we check that
|C_{G}(x)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
except possibly when
(q,\epsilon)=(2,-)
. However in this case we see from
[
12
, p.192]
that
|C_{G}(x)|=100800
, which again is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
.

The other subgroups of maximal rank are in
[
57
, Table 5.1]
, and are the normalizers of subgroups

M_{0}=\bar{M}^{F}
, where the possibilities for
\bar{M},M_{0}
are as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\bar{M}&M_{0}\\
\hline\cr A_{1}A_{5}&A_{1}(q)A_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)\\
A_{2}^{3}&A_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)^{3},\,A_{2}(q^{2})A_{2}^{-\epsilon}(q),\,A_{2}^{\epsilon}(q^{3})\\
D_{4}T_{2}&D_{4}(q)(q-\epsilon)^{2}/d,\,^{3}\!D_{4}(q)(q^{2}+\epsilon q+1)/d\\
D_{5}T_{1}&D_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)(q-\epsilon)/d\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

For
\bar{M}=A_{1}A_{5}
, the subgroup
M_{0}
has a cyclic torus
\langle g\rangle
of order
(q+\epsilon)(q^{5}-\epsilon)/d
. From the restriction
L(E_{6})\downarrow A_{1}A_{5}=L(A_{1}A_{5})\oplus 1\otimes\lambda_{3}
(see
[
56
, 11.10]
), we see that
\dim C_{L(E_{6})}(g)=6
or 8 (the latter only when
q-\epsilon\leq 2
), and hence
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Next let
\bar{M}=A_{2}^{3}
. Here

(6.3)

L(E_{6})\downarrow A_{2}^{3}=L(A_{2}^{3})\oplus(\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{1})\oplus(\lambda_{2}\otimes\lambda_{2}\otimes\lambda_{2})

(see
[
55
, 2.1]
). The subgroups
M_{0}=A_{2}(q^{2})A_{2}^{-\epsilon}(q)
and
A_{2}^{\epsilon}(q^{3})
contain regular semisimple elements – for example, the first contains an element
g=g_{1}g_{2}
with
g_{1}\in A_{2}(q^{2})
of order

q^{4}+q^{2}+1
and
g_{2}\in A_{2}^{-\epsilon}(q)
of order
q^{2}-1
, and (
6.3
) gives
\dim C_{L(E_{6})}(g)=6
. Similarly,
M_{0}=A_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)^{3}
contains a regular semisimple element, except when
(q,\epsilon)=(2,-)
, in which case we see from
[
12
, p.191]
that it contains an element
g
of order 9 with centralizer order 162. In all cases we have
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Now suppose
\bar{M}=D_{4}T_{2}
. From
[
56
, Chap.11]
we have
L(E_{6})\downarrow D_{4}=L(D_{4})\oplus L(T_{2})\oplus(\lambda_{1}\oplus\lambda_{3}\oplus\lambda_{4})^{2}
. From this we see that the subgroup
M_{0}=\,^{3}\!D_{4}(q)(q^{2}+\epsilon q+1)/d
has a regular semisimple element. Now consider
M_{0}=D_{4}(q)(q-\epsilon)^{2}/d
. For
q=2
we have
\epsilon=-
(see
[
57
, Table 5.1]
), and
M
has an element
g
of order 21 with centralizer order at most 63 (see
[
12
, p.191]
), which is less than
|G:M|^{1/3}
. Suppose
q\geq 3
. The subgroup
M
has an
S_{3}
inducing graph automorphisms on
D_{4}
, so we may pick
\tau\in M
inducing a triality and centralizing
G_{2}(q)<D_{4}(q)
. Let
g=x\tau
, where
x
is an element of order
q^{2}+q+1
in this
G_{2}(q)
. From the restriction
L(E_{6})\downarrow D_{4}
, we see that
\dim C_{L(E_{6})}(\tau)=30
. If
p\neq 3
, then
C_{\bar{S}}(\tau)=D_{4}T_{2}
and
x
is regular in the
D_{4}
factor, hence
g
is regular in
E_{6}
. And if
p=3
, then
C_{E_{6}}(x)=T_{2}A_{2}A_{2}
with
\tau
regular unipotent in each
A_{2}
factor, so
|C_{S}(g)|\leq(q^{2}+q+1)q^{4}
. In all cases
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Finally consider
\bar{M}=D_{5}T_{1}
. Choose an element
g
of order
(q^{5}-\epsilon)/(q-\epsilon)
in a subgroup
{}^{2}\!A_{4}(q)
of
M_{0}
. From the restriction

L(E_{6})\downarrow A_{4}=L(A_{4})\oplus\lambda_{1}\oplus\lambda_{4}\oplus(\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3})^{2}\oplus 0^{4}

(see
[
56
, Chap.11]
), we see that
\dim C_{L(E_{6})}(g)=8
, and so
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
as usual.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the cases where
M
is a subgroup of maximal rank and
S=E_{7}(q)
or
E_{8}(q)
.

Proof.

First we handle the maximal rank subgroups in
[
57
, Table 5.1]
. These are normalizers of subgroups
\bar{M}^{F}
, where
\bar{M}
is a connected subgroup of
\bar{G}
listed in Table
6.1
. In the table we also list the

\bar{G}
-class of a unipotent element
u\in\bar{M}^{F}
, as given by
[
45
]
. The unipotent class is labelled as in
[
56
, Tables 22.2.1,22.2.2]
, where the centralizer order is also given, and an upper bound for this order is included in Table
6.1
. In all cases except
A_{2}^{4}\,(p=3),\,A_{1}^{8}<E_{8}
and
A_{1}^{3}D_{4}\,(q=2),\,A_{1}^{7}<E_{7}
, we have
|C_{G}u)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
, so it remains to consider these cases.

Maximal rank subgroups in
E_{7},E_{8}

\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr\bar{G}&\bar{M}&\hbox{unip. elt. }u\in\bar{M}^{F}&|C_{G}(u)|\leq\\

Consider the case where
\bar{M}=A_{2}^{4}<E_{8}
with
p=3
. Here
M
is the normalizer of one of the following subgroups
M_{0}
:

(i)

A_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)^{4}\,(\epsilon=\pm)
;

(ii)

{}^{2}\!A_{2}(q^{2})^{2}
;

(iii)

{}^{2}\!A_{2}(q^{4})
.

The restriction
L(E_{8})\downarrow A_{2}^{4}
is given by
[
55
, Prop. 2.2]
. From this we see that in cases (ii) and (iii),
M
contains a maximal torus of order
(q^{4}-q^{2}+1)^{2}
or
q^{8}-q^{4}+1
respectively, each of which has a regular semisimple element
g
, so
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
in these cases. In case (i), we choose an element
g
projecting to each of the
A_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)
factors as a regular semisimple element of order
q^{2}+\epsilon q+1
. Then we find from the restriction
L(E_{8})\downarrow A_{2}^{4}
that
\dim C_{L(E_{8})}(g)\leq 32
, giving (
6.2
) as usual.

Now consider
\bar{M}=A_{1}^{8}<E_{8}
, in which case
M
is the normalizer of a subgroup
A_{1}(q)^{8}
and
q>2
. Here
M
contains a maximal torus
T
of order
(q+1)^{8}
, and
T
lies in a maximal rank subgroup normalizing
{}^{2}\!A_{4}(q)^{2}
. Hence there is an element
g\in T
of order
q+1
with centralizer containing
{}^{2}\!A_{3}(q)^{2}\!A_{4}(q)
, and in fact
|C_{G}(x)|
is either
|^{2}\!A_{3}(q)^{2}\!A_{4}(q)|(q+1)
or
|^{2}\!A_{3}(q)^{2}\!D_{5}(q)|
(the latter only if
q=3
). Hence (
6.2
) holds as usual.

Next suppose
\bar{M}=A_{1}^{3}D_{4}<E_{7}
with
q=2
. Here
M
is the normalizer of
M_{0}=A_{1}(2)^{3}.D_{4}(2)
or

A_{1}(2^{3}).\,^{3}\!D_{4}(2)
. The restriction

L(E_{7})\downarrow A_{1}^{3}D_{4}=L(A_{1}^{3}D_{4})\oplus(1\otimes 1\otimes 0\otimes\lambda_{1})\oplus(1\otimes 0\otimes 1\otimes\lambda_{3})\oplus(0\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes\lambda_{4})

(see
[
56
, Chap.11]
). If
M_{0}=A_{1}(2)^{3}.D_{4}(2)
, choose an element
g=g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}g_{4}\in M_{0}
with
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\in A_{1}(2)
of order 3 and
g_{4}\in D_{4}(2)
of order 7. Then from the above restriction we find that
\dim C_{L(E_{7})}(g)=19
, and hence (
6.2
) holds. In the other case
M_{0}=A_{1}(2^{3}).\,^{3}\!D_{4}(2)
, choose
g=g_{1}g_{2}
with
g_{1}\in A_{1}(8)
of order 7 and
g_{2}\in\,^{3}\!D_{4}(2)
of order 13. Then
g
is regular in
E_{7}
and again (
6.2
) holds.

Finally, consider
\bar{M}=A_{1}^{7}<E_{7}
, in which case
M
normalizes
M_{0}=A_{1}(q)^{7}
or
A_{1}(q^{7})
. In the latter case
M_{0}
has an element of order
q^{7}+1
which is regular in
\bar{S}
. In the former case we have
q>2
, and
M_{0}
has a maximal torus
T
of order
(q+1)^{7}/d
(
d=(2,q-1)
). For
q>3
, note that
T
lies in a maximal rank subgroup

A_{2}^{-}(q)A_{5}^{-}(q)
, so has an element
g
of order
q+1
with centralizer containing
A_{2}^{-}(q)A_{4}^{-}(q)(q+1)/d
; this must be the full centralizer in
S
, and (
6.2
) holds. And if
q=3
,
T
has an element
g
of order 4 with centralizer
A_{1}(q)A_{3}^{-}(q)A_{3}^{-}(q)
, and (
6.2
) holds again.

Now we handle the cases where
M
is a torus normalizer in
[
57
, Table 5.2]
. For
\bar{G}=E_{7}
, we have
M=T.W(E_{7})
, where
|T|=(q-\epsilon)^{7}/d
(
\epsilon=\pm 1
), and
q\geq 5
if
\epsilon=+1
. Choose an element
g\in M
mapping to an element of order 7 in
W(E_{7})
. Then
g
permutes the root spaces
L_{\alpha}
in the root space decomposition
L(E_{7})=H\oplus\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi}L_{\alpha}
in 18 cycles of length 7, and also
\dim C_{H}(g)=1
. Hence
\dim C_{L(E_{7})}(g)\leq 19
and (
6.2
) holds.

For
\bar{G}=E_{8}
,
M
is the normalizer of a torus
T
of one of the following orders:

(i)

(q-1)^{8}
(
q\geq 5
),

(ii)

(q+1)^{8}
,

(iii)

(q^{2}+q+1)^{4}
,

(iv)

(q^{2}-q+1)^{4}
(
q>2
),

(v)

(q^{2}+1)^{4}
,

(vi)

(q^{4}+q^{3}+q^{2}+q+1)^{2}
,

(vii)

(q^{4}-q^{3}+q^{2}-q+1)^{2}
,

(viii)

(q^{4}-q^{2}+1)^{2}
,

(ix)

q^{8}+q^{7}-q^{5}-q^{4}-q^{3}+q+1
,

(x)

q^{8}-q^{7}+q^{5}-q^{4}+q^{2}-q+1
.

For tori (i) and (ii), we have
M=T.W(E_{8})
, and we argue as above using an element of order 7 in the Weyl group
W(E_{8})
.

Tori (vi) and (vii) are contained in subsytem subgroups
A_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)A_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)
, and
T
contains an element
g=g_{1}g_{2}
with each
g_{i}
in
A_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)
of order a primitive prime divisor of
q^{5}-\epsilon
. Using the restriction
L(E_{8})\downarrow A_{4}A_{4}
given in
[
56
, Chap.11]
, we see that
\dim C_{E_{8}}(g)\leq 28
, and (
6.2
) holds.

Tori (iii), (iv) and (viii) are contained in subsystem subgroups normalizing
A_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)^{4}
or
A_{2}^{-}(q^{2})^{2}
, and are handled as in the previous paragraph.

Torus (v) lies in a subsystem
D_{8}(q)
(as a subgroup of type
O_{2}^{-}(q)^{4}
), and from the restriction
L(E_{8})\downarrow D_{8}
we find that an element
g=g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}g_{4}
, with each
g\in O_{2}^{-}(q)
of order
q^{2}+1
, satisfies
\dim C_{E_{8}}(g)\leq 64
.

Finally, tori (ix) and (x) are cyclic, and their generators are regular semisimple elements
g
, so (
6.2
) holds once again.
∎

6.4.
Subgroups in (3) of Theorem
6.2

Lemma 6.7
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the case where
M
is as in part (3) of Theorem

.

Proof.

In this case we have
M=N_{G}(\bar{M}^{F}\cap G)

is a connected reductive subgroup of non-maximal rank that is either listed in
[

, Table 3]
, or is one of the four extra possibilities in (3) of Theorem

.

M
is as in
[

, Table 3]
. We pick a regular unipotent element
u
of
\bar{M}^{F}\cap G
, and in Table

we list the
\bar{G}

, as given by

]
, together with an upper bound for the centralizer order

given by

, Chap.22]
. In all cases, (

. An element

. Hence (

) holds in these cases, completing the proof.

6.5.
Subfield and twisted subgroups

Lemma 6.8
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the case where
M
is as in part (4) of Theorem
6.2
.

Proof.

Recall that the maximal subgroups
M
in (4) of Theorem
6.2
are the normalizers of subfield subgroups
G(q_{0})
(where
\mathbb{F}_{q_{0}}\subset\mathbb{F}_{q}
), or of twisted subgroups
{}^{2}\!E_{6}(q^{1/2})<E_{6}(q)
,
{}^{2}\!F_{4}(q)<F_{4}(q)
and
{}^{2}\!G_{2}(q)<G_{2}(q)
. In all cases,
M
contains a regular unipotent element
u
of
G
, and the values of
|C_{G}(u)|
are given in
[
56
, Chap.22]
(also
[
77
,
79
]
for
G
of type
{}^{3}\!D_{4}
,
{}^{2}\!B_{2}
). We list upper bounds for these values in Table
6.3
. In all but three cases, (
6.2
) holds; the exceptional case are
G=G_{2}(4)
,
G_{2}(9)
and
{}^{2}\!B_{2}(8)
. For these cases we use the bound
\hbox{fix}(u)\leq|C_{G}(u)|\cdot u(M)/|M|
(where
u(M)
is the number of elements of
M
of the same order as
u
) to obtain the conclusion.
∎

6.6.
Exotic locals

Lemma 6.9
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the case where
M
is as in part (5) of Theorem
6.2
.

Proof.

In this case
M
is one of the ‘exotic local’ subgroups listed in part (5) of Theorem
6.2
, or the Borovik subgroup
(Alt_{5}\times Alt_{6}).2^{2}<E_{8}(q)
. For the exotic locals, it is known (see
[
43
, Sect. 4.3]
) that each of them possesses an element
g
of prime order
h+1=7
, 13, 13, 31 (for
\bar{G}=G_{2}
,
F_{4}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{8}
resp.) such that
g
is a regular element of
G
, and hence (
6.2
) holds. Finally, if
M
is the Borovik subgroup, the proof of
[
54
, Lemma 3.5]
shows that
M
contains an element
g
of order 5 with
E_{8}
-centralizer
A_{4}A_{4}
, and so again (
6.2
) holds.
∎

6.7.
Almost simple subgroups in class
{\mathcal{U}}

To complete the proof of Theorem
6.1
, it remains to handle the case where
M
is as in (6) of Theorem
6.2
– that is,
M
is in the class
{\mathcal{U}}
of almost simple maximal subgroups, not occurring in items (1)-(5). These were subdivided into the classes

{\mathcal{U}}_{p,1}
,
{\mathcal{U}}_{p,2}
and
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp}
, and we shall deal with these in turn.

Lemma 6.10
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the case where
M
is the class
{\mathcal{U}}_{p,1}
of Theorem
6.2
(6).

Proof.

Let
M_{0}
be the socle of
M
, so that
M_{0}\in Lie(p)
and
M_{0}\not\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{a})
. By
[
15
, Thms. 1.1,1.2]
, we have
G=E_{8}(q)
and
M_{0}
is one of the following groups:

\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(3),\,\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4),\,\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(3),\,\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(4),\,\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(8),\,\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2).

The possible restrictions
L(E_{8})\downarrow M_{0}
are given by
[
15
, Props. 7.2,8.1,8.2]
. Using these, we choose an element
g\in M_{0}
of order as in the following table, and compute
\dim C_{L(E_{8})}(g)
:

\begin{array}[]{l|llllll}M_{0}&\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(3)&\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)&\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(3)&\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(4)&\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(8)&\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)\\
\hline\cr\hbox{order of }g&13&7&7&13&19&5\\
\hline\cr\dim C_{L(E_{8})}(g)&20&38&38&20&14&48\end{array}

Hence in all cases, (
6.2
) holds.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the case where
M
is the class
{\mathcal{U}}_{p,2}
of Theorem
6.2
(6).

Proof.

In this case the socle of
M
is
M_{0}\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{a})
. By
[
16
, Thm. 1.1]
and
[
17
, Thm. 1.1]
(for
G\neq E_{8}(q)
), and
[
50
, Thm. 6]
(for
G=E_{8}(q)
), one of the following holds:

(i)

G=E_{7}(q)
and
M_{0}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{a})
with
p^{a}=7
or 8;

(ii)

G=E_{8}(q)
and
M_{0}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(p^{a})
with
p^{a}\leq(2,p-1)\cdot t(E_{8})
, where
t(E_{8})
is a constant defined in terms of the root system of
E_{8}
; and in fact
t(E_{8})=1312
by
[
46
]
.

In case (i), the possibilities for the restriction
L(E_{7})\downarrow M_{0}
are given in
[
17
, Sect. 6]
. From this, we see that for
p^{a}=7
, an element
g\in M_{0}
of order 7 has Jordan block sizes
7^{19}
or
7^{17},5,3^{3}
, hence by
[
45
]
is in one of the unipotent classes labelled
A_{6}
or
E_{7}(a_{5})
, and satisfies
|C_{G}(g)|\leq 6q^{21}
; so (
6.2
) holds. And for
p^{a}=8
, an element
g\in M_{0}
of order 7 satisfies
\dim C_{L(E_{7})}(g)\leq 18
, and again (
6.2
) holds.

Now consider case (ii). Write
q_{0}=p^{a}
, and assume that
q_{0}\neq 4,5,9
(we shall deal with these as alternating groups in the next lemma). Let
g\in M_{0}
be an element of order
r:=(q_{0}+1)/(2,p-1)
, and note that
r\geq 4
. If (
6.2
) holds then we are done, so suppose that (
6.2
) fails, so that

(6.4)

|C_{G}(g)|\geq|G:M|^{1/3}\geq\left(|E_{8}(q)|/|{\mathrm{P\Gamma L}}_{2}(q_{0})|\right)^{1/3}.

Now
r
must divide the order of a maximal torus of
G
, which implies that
q_{0}\leq q^{8}
. From (
6.4
) and inspection of semisimple element centralizers in
E_{8}(q)
(see
[
19
]
), it follows that
C_{\bar{G}}(g)=E_{7}T_{1}
,
D_{7}T_{1}
,
E_{6}A_{1}T_{1}
or
E_{6}T_{2}
, where
T_{i}
denotes a torus of rank
i
. In the
E_{6}T_{2}
case we have
g\in T_{2}
and so
q_{0}\leq q^{2}
; but then (
6.4
) does not hold. Hence

(6.5)

C_{\bar{G}}(g)=E_{7}T_{1},\,D_{7}T_{1}\hbox{ or }E_{6}A_{1}T_{1},

and also
q_{0}\leq q
(as
g\in T_{1}
). Writing
T_{1}=\{T(c):c\in K^{*}\}
(where
K=\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
), we see from the restrictions of
L(E_{8})
to the subsystem subgroups
E_{7}A_{1}
,
E_{6}A_{1}
and
D_{7}T_{1}
given in
[
56
, 11.2,11.3]
that the eigenvalues of
T(c)
on
L(E_{8})
are as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l|l}C_{\bar{G}}(g)&\hbox{eigenvalues of }T(c)\\
\hline\cr E_{7}T_{1}&1^{134},(c^{\pm 1})^{56},(c^{\pm 2})^{1}\\
D_{7}T_{1}&1^{92},(c^{\pm 1})^{64},(c^{\pm 2})^{14}\\
E_{6}A_{1}T_{1}&1^{82},(c^{\pm 1})^{56},(c^{\pm 2})^{7}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

We have
g=T(c)
for some
c
, and also
g
has order
r=(q_{0}+1)/(2,p-1)\geq 4
. If
r\geq 5
, then the eigenvalues
c,c^{-1},c^{2},c^{-2}
are all distinct, so
g
stabilizes precisely the same subspaces of
L(E_{8})
as the torus
T_{1}
. Hence
M_{0}
stabilizes the same subspaces as the positive-dimensional subgroup
\langle M_{0},T_{1}\rangle
of
\bar{S}
. At this point the proof of
[
50
, Thm. 6]
shows that
M
is as in (2) or (3) of Theorem
6.2
, contrary to the fact that
M
is in the class
{\mathcal{U}}
.

It remains to consider the case where
r=4
. Here
q_{0}=p=7
, and we give a different argument.
Let
W=C_{L(E_{8})}(M_{0})
, the fixed point space of
M_{0}
on
L(E_{8})
. If
\dim W\geq 2
, then
M=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(7)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(7)
fixes a nonzero vector in
W
, and so by
[
16
, Prop. 4.5]
,
M\leq N(\bar{M}^{F})

for some positive dimensional proper connected subgroup
\bar{M}
of
\bar{G}
, a contradiction (since
M
is in the class
{\mathcal{U}}
). The same contradiction applies if
\dim W=1
, since then the stabilizer in
\bar{G}
of
W
is a positive dimensional subgroup containing
M_{0}
which is invariant under all automorphisms normalizing
M_{0}
.
Hence we suppose that

(6.6)

W=C_{L(E_{8})}(M_{0})=0,

and aim for a contradiction.

We can label the irreducible modules in characteristic 7 for
M_{0}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(7)
as
V_{1},V_{3},V_{5},V_{7}
, where
V_{i}
has dimension
i
and is the
i-1^{th}
symmetric power
S^{i}(V_{2})
of the natural module for
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(7)
. Let

L(E_{8})\downarrow M_{0}=V_{1}^{a}/V_{3}^{b}/V_{5}^{c}/V_{7}^{d},

meaning that the restriction has the composition factors
V_{1},V_{3},V_{5},V_{7}
with multiplicities
a,b,c,d
respectively. From
[
1
]
, we have
\dim H^{1}(M_{0},V_{i})
equal to 0 for
i=1,3
and equal to 1 for
i=5,7
. From elementary considerations (see for example
[
60
, Prop. 3.6]
), it follows that the fixed point space
W
has dimension at least
a-c-d
, and hence by our assumption (
6.6
), we have

(6.7)

a-c-d\leq 0.

We also have

(6.8)

a+3b+5c+7d=248,

and (
6.5
) gives

(6.9)

a+b+c+d=134,\,92\hbox{ or }82.

From (
6.8
) we have

3(a+b+c+d)=248+2(a-c-d)-2d,

and so it follows from (
6.7
) that
a+b+c+d=82
, and also
(a-c-d,\,d)=(0,1)
or
(-1,0)
.

Now consider an element
h\in M_{0}
of order 3. Then
C_{\bar{G}}(h)
is one of the subsystem subgroups
A_{8}
,
A_{2}E_{6}
,
D_{7}T_{1}
,
E_{7}T_{1}
, of dimensions 80, 86, 92, 134. Since the dimensions of
C_{V_{i}}(h)
for
i=1,3,5,7
are 1,1,1,3 respectively, it follows that

a+b+c+3d=80,86,92\hbox{ or }134.

This is not compatible with the equation
a+b+c+d=82
and the fact that
d=0
or 1. This final contradiction completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

Theorem
6.1
holds in the case where
M
is the class
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
of Theorem
6.2
(6).

Proof.

By
[
37
,
38
,
79
]
, the class
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
is empty when
S
is of type
{}^{2}\!B_{2}
,
{}^{2}\!G_{2}
or
{}^{3}\!D_{4}
. And from
[
62
]
together with
[
14
, 4.11]
, for type
{}^{2}\!F_{4}
the class
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
is also empty unless
q=8
, in which case it contains a subgroup
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(13)
; this subgroup has an element
g
of order 13 which has centralizer of order 65, so (
6.2
) holds.

For
G=G_{2}(q)
,
F_{4}(q)
or
E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
, the subgroups in class
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
are known (see
[
38
,
14
]
), as are their actions on the Lie algebra
L(\bar{G})
. In Tables
6.4
-
6.6
we give the socles
M_{0}
of these subgroups, together with the order of an element
g\in M_{0}
for which
\dim C_{\bar{G}}(g)
is as in the last column of the table. In all cases except for
(G,M_{0})=(G_{2}(4),J_{2})
, and recalling that semisimple and unipotent centralizer orders can be found in
[
65
,
20
,
56
]
, it follows that (
6.2
) holds; in the exceptional case, the permutation character of the action is given in
[
12
, p.97]
, from which we see that
\mathrm{fix}(g)=3
, giving the conclusion.
