Next consider
G=E_{7}(q)
. In this case, the subgroups in class
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
are given in
[
17
]
, apart from two cases. In these cases,
M_{0}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(7)
or
Alt_{6}
, and the possible actions on
L(E_{7})
are given in
[
17
, Sect.6.1]
,
[
13
, 6.1]
respectively. As in the previous cases, we give in Table
6.7
the socles
M_{0}
of the subgroups in
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
, together with the order of an element
g\in M_{0}
for which
\dim C_{\bar{G}}(g)
is as in the last column of the table; in all cases (
6.2
) holds.

Finally, suppose that
S=E_{8}(q)
. In this case, the subgroups in class
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
are not known, but all candidates can be found in
[
60
]
; also in
[
60
, Chap.6]
, with the exception of
(M_{0},p)=(Alt_{6},3)
, the possible Brauer characters of the representations on
L(E_{8})
of the candidates are given. We shall give a separate argument for alternating groups in the next paragraph, and for the non-alternating groups we give in Table
6.8
the possible socles of subgroups in
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
, together with elements
g
and values
\dim C_{\bar{G}}(g)
for which (
6.2
) holds.

To complete the proof we need to deal with alternating socles in
{\mathcal{U}}_{ASp^{\prime}}
. By
[
13
]
, the possible socles are
M_{0}=Alt_{6}
or
Alt_{7}
. For
M_{0}=Alt_{7}
, the possibilities for the action of
M_{0}
on
L(E_{8})
are given in
[
13
, Sect.7]
, from which we see that an element
g\in M_{0}
of order 7 satisfies
\dim C_{E_{8}}(g)\leq 40
, and (
6.2
) holds. Similarly, if
M_{0}=Alt_{6}
and
p\neq 3
, Section 6 of
[
13
]
gives the possibilities for
L(E_{8})\downarrow M_{0}
, and an element
g\in M_{0}
of order 5 satisfies
\dim C_{E_{8}}(g)\leq 52
.

It remains to consider
M_{0}=Alt_{6}
with
p=3
. In this case, the possible restrictions

L(E_{8})\downarrow M_{0}
are not analysed in either
[
13
]
or
[
60
]
. We argue as follows that there is an element
g\in M_{0}
that satisfies (
6.2
). Suppose this is not the case, and let
x,y\in M_{0}
be elements of orders 5 and 4, respectively. From the list of centralizers and traces of elements of such orders that can be found in
[
11
, Table 4]
, we see that the assumption that
x,y
do not satisfy (
6.2
) forces
C_{E_{8}}(x)
and
C_{E_{8}}(y)
both to be among the types
E_{7}T_{1}
,
D_{7}T_{1}
,
A_{1}E_{6}T_{1}
. Moreover, if
\chi
denotes the Brauer character of
M_{0}
on
L(E_{8})
, then for the respective centralizers, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\chi(x)=78+55\tau,\,28+50\tau\hbox{ or }28+25\tau,\\
\chi(y)=132,\,64\hbox{ or }28,\end{array}

where
\tau=\frac{1}{2}(1+\sqrt{5})
.
From
[
35
]
, we see that the 3-modular irreducibles for
Alt_{6}
are
V_{1}
,
V_{3}
,
V_{3}^{*}
,
V_{4}
and
V_{9}
, where
V_{i}
has dimension
i
. Let their multiplicities as composition factors of
L(E_{8})\downarrow M_{0}
be
a,b,c,d,e
respectively. Then the values of
\chi(1)
,
\chi(x)
and
\chi(y)
give the equations

(1)

a+3b+3c+4d+9e=248
,

(2)

a+b\tau+c\tau-4d-9e=78+55\tau,\,28+50\tau\hbox{ or }28+25\tau
,

(3)

a+b+c-2d+e=132,\,64\hbox{ or }28
.

From (2) we have
b+c=55
, 50 or 25, and
a-4d-9e=78
or 28, and adding (1) to the latter gives

2a+3(b+c)=326\hbox{ or }276.

Hence
b+c
is even, so
b+c=50
and so
a=63
. Also
a-4d-9e=28
, which gives
4d+9e=35
. Also by (3) we have
2d-e=-19
, 49 or 85. The last two equations do not have integral solutions for
d,e
. This final contradiction completes the proof.
∎

7.
Classical groups: preliminaries

It remains to prove
Theorem

3.1
for classical groups. In this section,
we establish a number of preliminary results required for the proof, which will be presented in
Sections

8
and
9
.
Throughout this section,
p
is a prime number and
K=\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

7.1.
Some representation theory

We begin with some representation theoretic lemmas.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
H
be a finite group and let
x\in H
be a
p^{\prime}
-element with
\langle x\rangle\trianglelefteq H
. Then, the action of
H
on
\langle x\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
is equivalent to the action of
H
on the set of nontrivial irreducible
K\langle x\rangle
-modules. In particular, if
H\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
then the action of
H
on the set of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
x
is equivalent to the action on a subset of
\langle x\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
.

Proof.

Let
\zeta
be an element of
K^{\times}
of order
|x|
. The map
x^{i}\mapsto\phi_{\zeta^{i}}
gives the desired equivalence of
H
-actions, where
\phi_{\zeta^{i}}
is the homomorphism
\langle x\rangle\to K^{\times}
given by
x\mapsto\zeta^{i}
. The last part (“in particular…”) follows immediately.
∎

Typically, we will apply the lemma when
H
induces a semiregular group on
\langle x\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
, say of order
t
, in which case
H
induces a semiregular group of order
t
on the nontrivial eigenspaces of
x
. In particular, in this case the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
x
occur with multiplicities that are multiples of
t
.
The following variant will sometimes be useful.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
H\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
and let
x\in H
be a
p^{\prime}
-element of prime order. Then
N_{H}(\langle x\rangle)/C_{H}(x)
has all orbits of size
|N_{H}(\langle x\rangle)/C_{H}(x)|=|x^{H}\cap\langle x\rangle|
on the set of nontrivial eigenspaces of
x
.

Proof.

Since
x
has prime order,
N_{H}(\langle x\rangle)/C_{H}(x)
acts semiregularly on
\langle x\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
. Also, the map
N_{H}(\langle x\rangle)/C_{H}(x)\to x^{H}\cap\langle x\rangle
sending
C_{H}(x)g\mapsto x^{g}
is a well-defined bijection. The statement now follows from
Lemma

7.1
.
∎

Let us also recall the following result due to Scott
[
71
]
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume that
\langle x,y\rangle\leq\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)=\mathrm{GL}(V)
is irreducible. Then, there exists
z\in\{x,y,xy\}
such that
\dim(C_{V}(z))\leq n/3
.

Proof.

Scott’s lemma
[
71
, Thm. 1]
gives
\dim(C_{V}(x))+\dim(C_{V}(y))+\dim(C_{V}(xy))\leq n
, from which the conclusion follows.
∎

The following lemma uses Green correspondence. We will usually apply it to the case where
S
is a cyclic torus in a group of Lie type
G
containing a cyclic Sylow
p
-subgroup
P
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
W
be an indecomposable
KG
-module. Let
P
be a cyclic Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G
, let
S
be an abelian subgroup of
G
containing
P
, and assume that

(
\star
)

S\trianglelefteq N_{G}(P)=N_{G}(P_{0})
for every
1\neq P_{0}\leq P
.

Then,
W\downarrow P=W_{0}\oplus U
where all indecomposable submodules of
U
have dimension
|P|
, and
W_{0}
is the direct sum of at most
|N_{G}(P):S|
indecomposable modules of the same dimension.

Proof.

By
[
6
, Corollary 3.6.10]
, every
KG
-module is projective relative to
P
, i.e., it is a direct summand of some module induced from
P
(see
[
6
, Proposition 3.6.4]
). Moreover, assumption (
\star
) implies that

(7.1)

P^{g}\cap N_{G}(P)=1\text{ for every }g\not\in N_{G}(P).

Now we apply Green correspondence. Let
D
be a vertex of
W
, see
[
6
, Definition 3.10.1]
; up to conjugacy,
D\leq P
by
[
6
, Proposition 3.6.9]
.
We may assume that
W
is not projective, as otherwise
W\downarrow P
is projective
and the lemma holds with
W_{0}=0
.
In particular,
D\neq 1
.

By
[
6
, Theorem 3.12.2(i)]
, applied with
H=N_{G}(P)=N_{G}(D)
, we have
W\downarrow N_{G}(P)=W_{1}\oplus W_{2}
, where
W_{1}
is indecomposable and
W_{2}
is projective (note that, with notation as in
[
6
, Theorem 3.12.2(i)]
,
\mathcal{Y}=\{1\}
by (
7.1
)).

Setting
U:=W_{2}\downarrow P
, we see that
U
is projective, and so, since
P
is cyclic, indecomposable submodules of
U
have dimension
|P|
.
Moreover,
W_{1}
is projective relative to
S
, and so by Clifford theory (see
[
6
, Section 3.13]
),
W_{1}\downarrow S
is a direct sum of indecomposable modules, conjugate under
N_{G}(P)/S
. When restricted to
P
, each such module remains indecomposable, and so setting
W_{0}:=W_{1}\downarrow P
, the lemma is proved.
∎

7.2.
Some optimization

The following few lemmas will be used to bound the dimension
d
of the centralizer of certain semisimple elements
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)=\mathrm{GL}(V)
. For example, assume that
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/3
and that the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
occur with multiplicity at least
4
(we will use
Lemma

7.1
in order to produce such elements). Then
Lemma

7.6
, below, applied with
A=n/3
and
B=4
, asserts that
d\leq n^{2}/4
; equality is attained when
C_{V}(g)=0
and
g
has four nontrivial eigenspaces of dimension
n/4
. As another example, assuming that
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/2
and that the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
occur with multiplicity at least
4
, we have
d\leq 5n^{2}/16
, with equality attained when
C_{V}(g)
has dimension
n/2
and there are four nontrivial eigenspaces of dimension
n/8
.

Lemma

7.5
, below, takes into account also the number of nontrivial eigenvalues of
g
(named
R(\underline{a})
), which will be useful on some occasions.
Lemma

7.7
is a useful variant for symplectic and orthogonal groups.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
n
and
B
be positive integers with
B<n
, let
1\leq A<n
be a real number, and assume
A+B\leq n
.
Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the set of
\underline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
such that for every
i
,
a_{i}=0
or
a_{i}\geq 1
;
a_{1}\leq A
;
\sum_{i}a_{i}=n
; for each
i\geq 2
,
a_{i}
is either zero or equal to
a_{t}
for at least
B
values of
t\geq 2
. Denoting by
R(\underline{a})
the number of
i\geq 2
such that
a_{i}
is nonzero, we have

\max_{\underline{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(R(\underline{a})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\right)=\begin{cases}B+A^{2}+(n-A)^{2}/B&\text{if $A(B+1)\geq 2n$}\\
B+n^{2}/B&\text{if $A(B+1)\leq 2n$}\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\underline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathcal{A}
and denote
f(\underline{a})=R(\underline{a})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}
. We claim that if
R(\underline{a})>B
, then there exists some
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathcal{A}
with
R(\underline{b})<R(\underline{a})
and
f(\underline{b})>f(\underline{a})
. In particular, this will allow us to assume
R(\underline{a})=B
.

Without loss,
0\neq a_{2}=\cdots=a_{r+1}
for some
r\geq B
, and
a_{t}\neq a_{2}
for
t>r+1
. If
r>B
, then replace
a_{i}
by
ra_{i}/B
for
i=2,\ldots,B+1
, and replace
a_{i}
by
0
for
i\in\{B+2,\ldots,r+1\}
. Since

r+\sum_{i=2}^{r+1}a_{i}^{2}=r+ra_{2}^{2}<B+r^{2}a_{2}^{2}/B=B+\sum_{i=2}^{B+1}(ra_{i}/B)^{2}

the claim is proved in this case. Assume then
r=B
. If there exists
t>B+1
with
a_{t}\neq 0
, then without loss
0\neq a_{B+2}=\cdots a_{2B+1}
and
a_{\ell}\neq a_{2},a_{B+2}
for
\ell>2B+1
. Now for
i=2,\ldots,B+1
replace
a_{i}
by
a_{i}+a_{B+i}
, and for
i=B+2,\ldots,2B+1
, replace
a_{i}
by
0
. Then

2B+\sum_{i=2}^{2B+1}a_{i}^{2}<B+\sum_{i=2}^{B+1}(a_{i}+a_{B+i})^{2}

and so the claim holds in this case also.

Therefore the initial claim is proved, so assume
R(\underline{a})=B
, and without loss,
a_{t}=0
for every
t>B+1
. We may then simply maximize
a_{1}^{2}+Ba_{2}^{2}
subject to
a_{1}+Ba_{2}=n
,
1\leq a_{1}\leq A
or
a_{1}=0
, and
a_{2}\geq 1
or
a_{2}=0
. This is a parabola in
a_{1}
and the maximum is given by either
a_{1}=0
or
a_{1}=A
, which give the values
f(\underline{a})=B+n^{2}/B
and
f(\underline{a})=B+A^{2}+(n-A)^{2}/B
respectively. Noting that
B+A^{2}+(n-A)^{2}/B\geq B+n^{2}/B
if and only if
A(B+1)\geq 2n
, the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

With notation as in
Lemma

7.5
, we have

\max_{\underline{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}=\begin{cases}A^{2}+(n-A)^{2}/B&\text{if $A(B+1)\geq 2n$}\\
n^{2}/B&\text{if $A(B+1)\leq 2n$}\end{cases}

Proof.

By the proof of the previous lemma, the maximum of
R(\underline{a})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}
is attained when
R(\underline{a})
is as small as possible, and so the same value gives the maximum of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

With notation as in
Lemma

7.5
, we have

\max_{\underline{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(R(\underline{a})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\pm a_{1}\right)=\begin{cases}B+A^{2}\pm A+(n-A)^{2}/B&\text{if $A(B+1)\pm B\geq 2n$}\\
B+n^{2}/B&\text{if $A(B+1)\pm B\leq 2n$}\end{cases}

Proof.

We follow the proof of
Lemma

7.5
, and in the same way we see that the maximum occurs when
R(\underline{a})=B
. Then we need to maximize
a_{1}^{2}\pm a_{1}+Ba_{2}^{2}
subject to the same constraints, and the maximum is attained for
a_{1}=0
or
a_{1}=A
.
∎

In the following lemma, for a partition
a_{1}\geq\cdots\geq a_{n}
of
n
, we denote by
a^{\prime}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq a^{\prime}_{n}
the transpose partition. The lemma will be applied to bound the dimension
d
of the centralizer of certain unipotent elements
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)=\mathrm{GL}(V)
, which will be produced thanks to
Lemma

7.4
. (Recall that if
a_{i}
are the sizes of the Jordan blocks, then
d=\sum(a^{\prime}_{i})^{2}
where
d
is the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
.)

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
B\geq 2,C\geq 0
, and
n
be integers, with
B,C\leq n
, and let
a_{1}\geq\cdots\geq a_{n}
be a partition of
n
. Assume that there exists a subset
I
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\leq C
, and
a_{j}\geq B
for every
j\not\in I
. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a^{\prime}_{i})^{2}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{B}+C^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{B}\right).

Proof.

The case
C=n
is trivial, so assume
C<n
. Fix any
C^{\prime}\leq C
, and assume
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}=C^{\prime}
. Assume that
a_{i}>1
for some
i\in I
; then consider the partition
b_{1}\geq\cdots\geq b_{n}
obtained by replacing
a_{i}
by a part of length
a_{i}-1
, and adding one part of length
1
. In the transpose partition, this means that
b^{\prime}_{1}=a^{\prime}_{1}+1
, and
b^{\prime}_{j}=a^{\prime}_{j}-1
for some
j>1
. Since

(a^{\prime}_{1})^{2}+(a^{\prime}_{j})^{2}<(a^{\prime}_{1}+1)^{2}+(a^{\prime}_{j}-1)^{2},

then iterating this procedure, we may assume that
a_{i}\leq 1
for every
i\in I
.
Similarly, we may assume that either
C^{\prime}=C
, or
a_{j}=B
for every
j\not\in I
.

Next we look at the transpose partition; we take
a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{n}
real numbers and we maximize
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a^{\prime}_{i})^{2}
under the given constraints.

Assume first
C^{\prime}=C
. The condition
a_{j}\geq B
for every
j\not\in I
is equivalent to
a^{\prime}_{1}-C=a^{\prime}_{2}=\cdots=a^{\prime}_{B}
. (Note that
I
is a proper subset of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
since
C<n
.)
Assume that there exists
j>B
with
0<a^{\prime}_{j}<a^{\prime}_{1}-C
; without loss,
j=B+1
. Note that

\sum_{i=1}^{B+1}(a^{\prime}_{i})^{2}<\sum_{i=1}^{B}(a^{\prime}_{i}+a_{B+1}/B)^{2}.

In particular, we may assume that
a^{\prime}_{j}=0
for every
j>B
. This choice gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a^{\prime}_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle=(\frac{n-C}{B}+C)^{2}+(B-1)\frac{(n-C)^{2}}{B^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n-C)^{2}}{B}+C^{2}+\frac{2C(n-C)}{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}}{B}+C^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{B}\right),

which concludes the proof in this case.

Assume finally
a_{j}=B
for every
j\not\in I
, so up to reordering
a^{\prime}_{1}-C^{\prime}=a^{\prime}_{2}=\cdots a^{\prime}_{B}=(n-C^{\prime})/B
and
a_{j}=0
for
j>B
. Let
\epsilon=(C-C^{\prime})/B
, so that
\epsilon+C^{\prime}+(B-1)\epsilon=C
. Then replace each
a^{\prime}_{j}
,
j>1
, by
b^{\prime}_{j}=a^{\prime}_{j}-\epsilon
, and replace
a^{\prime}_{1}
by
b^{\prime}_{1}=a^{\prime}_{1}+(B-1)\epsilon=b^{\prime}_{2}+C
. As above,
\sum(a^{\prime}_{i})^{2}\leq\sum(b^{\prime}_{i})^{2}
, which reduces to the case
C^{\prime}=C
, addressed in the previous paragraph.
∎

7.3.
Centralizer bounds

Next we need some bounds for the dimension and size of centralizers in classical groups. We begin by recording a calculation for the order of the groups.

Lemma 7.9
.

The following estimates hold for every
n\geq 1
and every prime power
q
.

\displaystyle q^{n^{2}-2}<\frac{9q^{n^{2}}}{32}

\displaystyle<|\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)|<q^{n^{2}}

\displaystyle q^{n^{2}-2}<\frac{9q^{n^{2}-1}}{16}

\displaystyle<|\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)|<q^{n^{2}-1}

\displaystyle q^{n^{2}}

\displaystyle<|\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)|\leq 1.5q^{n^{2}}<q^{n^{2}+1}

\displaystyle q^{n^{2}-2}<\frac{q^{n^{2}-1}}{1.5}

\displaystyle<|\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)|<q^{n^{2}-1}

\displaystyle q^{2n^{2}+n-1}<\frac{9q^{2n^{2}+n}}{16}

\displaystyle<|\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}(q)|=|\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}(q)|<q^{2n^{2}+n}

\displaystyle q^{2n^{2}-n-1}<2\frac{9q^{2n^{2}-n}}{32}

\displaystyle<|\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2n}(q)|<2q^{2n^{2}-n}

\displaystyle q^{2n^{2}-n}<2\frac{9q^{2n^{2}-n}}{16}

\displaystyle<|\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2n}(q)|\leq 2q^{2n^{2}-n}\leq q^{2n^{2}-n+1}\text{ if $(n,q)\neq(1,2)$}

Proof.

The estimates follow from
[
30
, Lemma 5.1(i, iii)]
, and from the order formulas for the groups under consideration. We just note that the occurrence of the factor
1.5
follows from use of the bound
q+1\leq 1.5q
.
∎

The next lemma records, in a special case, the fact that the dimension of the centralizer of an element in a symplectic or orthogonal group is approximately half the dimension of the centralizer in the general linear group.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
g\in Y:=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
or
Y:=\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
, and let
d^{\prime}
and
d
be the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
Y
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
, respectively.

(i)

Assume
Y=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
. If
g
is unipotent and
p
is odd, then
d^{\prime}\leq d/2+R/2
where
R
is the number of Jordan blocks of
g
. If
g
is semisimple, without eigenvalue
-1
if
p
is odd, then
d^{\prime}=d/2+m/2
where
m
is the dimension of the
1
-eigenspace of
g
.

(ii)

Assume
Y=\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
. If
g
is unipotent and
p
is odd, then
d^{\prime}\leq d/2
. If
g
is semisimple, without eigenvalue
-1
if
p
is odd, then
d^{\prime}=d/2-m/2
where
m
is the dimension of the
1
-eigenspace of
g
.

Proof.

If
g
is unipotent, then by assumption
p
is odd and the bounds follow from
[
56
, Theorem 3.1]
. Assume then
g
is semisimple; denote by
V_{\lambda}
the
\lambda
-eigenspace of
g
and by
m_{\lambda}
its dimension. If
\lambda\neq 1
then
m_{\lambda}=m_{\lambda^{-1}}
and the centralizer of
g
restricted to
V_{\lambda}\oplus V_{\lambda^{-1}}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{GL}_{m_{\lambda}}(K)
. On the other hand, the centralizer of the restriction of
g
to
V_{1}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sp}_{m_{1}}(K)
(if
Y=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
) or
\mathrm{O}_{m_{1}}(K)
(if
Y=\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
). Since
\dim(\mathrm{Sp}_{m_{1}}(K))=m_{1}^{2}/2+m_{1}/2
and

\dim(\mathrm{O}_{m_{1}}(K))=m_{1}^{2}/2-m_{1}/2
, the conclusion follows.
∎

In part (iii) of the next lemma, a rational canonical block of
g
refers to an indecomposable
\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle g\rangle
-submodule of the natural module. The natural module decomposes as a direct sum of such submodules; the number of summands is called the number of rational canonical blocks of
g
. Note that (iii) is a generalization of (i), but we prefer to state (i) separately for later reference.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
q
be a power of
p
, let
G
be
\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
or
\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
, let
g\in G
and let
d^{\prime}
be the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
or
\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
.

(i)

Assume
g
is unipotent, and let
R
be the number of Jordan blocks of
g
. Then
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{R}q^{d^{\prime}}\leq q^{d^{\prime}+R}
.

(ii)

Assume
g
is semisimple, and let
E
(resp.
E_{2}
) be the number of distinct irreducible factors (resp. irreducible factors of degree at least
2
) of the characteristic polynomial of
g
. If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
then
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{E_{2}}q^{d^{\prime}}\leq q^{d^{\prime}+E_{2}}
; if
G=\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
then
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{E_{2}+2}q^{d^{\prime}}\leq q^{d^{\prime}+E_{2}+2}
; and if
G=\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)
then
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{E}q^{d^{\prime}}\leq q^{d^{\prime}+E}
.

(iii)

Let
R
denote the number of rational canonical blocks of
g
. Then
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{R}q^{d^{\prime}}\leq q^{d^{\prime}+R}
.

(iv)

Assume
g
is regular. Then
|C_{G}(g)|<h^{(2,q-1)}(q+1)^{r}
where
r
is the untwisted Lie rank of
G
, and
h=1
if
G=\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)
,
h=2
if
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
,
h=4
if
G=\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
.

Proof.

(i) This can be found in
[
56
]
, as we proceed to explain. We address the case
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
; the case
G=\mathrm{O}_{n}(q)
is identical and the case
G=\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)
is easier. If
p
is odd, then
[
56
, Theorem 7.1]
tells us that
C_{G}(g)=U\rtimes H
, where
U
is unipotent and
H
is a direct product of at most
R
groups, each of which is a symplectic or orthogonal group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. By
Lemma

7.9
, each of these groups has order at most
2q^{m}
, where
m
is the dimension of the corresponding algebraic group; the claimed bound follows.
Assume then
p=2
, where there are some complications in the structure of
C_{G}(g)
. Following
[
56
]
, we may write

\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}\downarrow\langle g\rangle=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell}W(m_{i})^{a_{i}}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}V(2k_{j})^{b_{j}}

where each
W(m_{i})
is the sum of two Jordan blocks of size
m_{i}
, and each
V(2k_{j})
is a Jordan block of size
2k_{j}
. (In particular,
n=2\sum_{i}a_{i}m_{i}+2\sum_{j}b_{j}k_{j}
.) Here the
m_{i}
are pairwise distinct, and the same for the
k_{j}
; assume moreover the
k_{j}
are in decreasing order.
By
[
56
, Theorem 7.2]
we have
C_{G}(g)=U.(H\times K)
where

•

U
is unipotent;

•

H
is a direct product of
\ell
groups, the
i
-th of which is a symplectic or orthogonal group of rank
a_{i}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
;

•

K\cong C_{2}^{t+\delta}
, where
t
is the number of
j
such that
k_{j}-k_{j+1}\geq 2
, and
\delta\in\{0,1\}
, with
\delta=1
only if
r\neq 0
.

Letting
b
be the number of
i
such that
a_{i}=1
, by
Lemma

7.9
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{\ell+b+t+\delta}q^{d^{\prime}}
. (The relevance of the parameter
b
stands in the fact that, for
q=2
,
|\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2}(q)|=2(q+1)
is not less than
q^{2}
, cf. the last equation in
Lemma

7.9
; it is instead less than
2q^{2}
.) By the definitions we have
\ell+b\leq 2\sum a_{i}
and
t+\delta\leq\sum b_{j}
, so
\ell+b+t+\delta\leq 2\sum a_{i}+\sum b_{j}=R
, which implies
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{R}q^{d^{\prime}}
, as desired for part (i).

We now address (ii) and (iii) largely simultaneously. We assume
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
; the other cases are similar, as we point out at the end of the proof. For a monic polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[X]
of degree
m
, denote
f^{*}(X)=X^{m}f(1/X)/f(0)
. Writing
V:=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
, we have

V\downarrow\langle g\rangle=\bigoplus_{f}V_{f}

where
f
is an irreducible monic polynomial in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[X]
and
V_{f}
is the generalized
f
-eigenspace of
g
, and moreover
W_{f,f^{*}}:=V_{f}+V_{f^{*}}
is nondegenerate. Let
g_{f,f^{*}}
be the restriction of
g
to
W_{f,f*}
and let
S_{f,f^{*}}=\mathrm{Sp}(W_{f,f^{*}})
, so

C_{G}(g)=\prod_{f,f^{*}}C_{S_{f,f^{*}}}(g_{f,f^{*}}).

Let now
g_{f}
be the restriction of
g
to
V_{f}
. Assume
f
has degree
m
and
V_{f}
has dimension
k
. Note that
g_{f}
acts
\mathbb{F}_{q^{m}}
-linearly; let
u_{f}
be the unipotent part of
g_{f}
as an element of
\mathrm{GL}_{k/m}(q^{m})
. Write
S=S_{f,f^{*}}
,
g^{\prime}=g_{f,f^{*}}
,
u=u_{f}
, for ease of notation. Then
C_{S}(g^{\prime})\cong C_{L}(u)
, where
L=\mathrm{GU}_{k/m}(q^{m/2})
if
f=f^{*}
and
\deg(f)>1
;
L=\mathrm{GL}_{k/m}(q^{m})
if
f\neq f^{*}
;
L=\mathrm{Sp}_{k}(q)
if
f=f^{*}
and
\deg(f)=1
(i.e.,
f=X\pm 1
). Assume next
g
is as in (ii). Then
g
is semisimple, and so
u=1
and
C_{L}(u)=L
. In particular, we see that the bound follows from
Lemma

7.9
, noting that the number of
f
with
f=f^{*}
and
f\neq X+1
and
V_{f}\neq 0
is at most
E_{2}
(since each such
f
has degree at least
2
). Assume now
g
is as in (iii), so
u
may be nontrivial. The bound follows from (i), noting that the number of rational canonical blocks of
g^{\prime}
is at least the number of Jordan blocks of
u
. (More precisely, if
f=f^{*}
then it is equal to the number of Jordan blocks of
u
, and otherwise it is twice that number.)

The case
G=\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
is essentially the same; in (ii), we pay an additional factor
4\leq q^{2}

to account for the case
f=X\pm 1
, in which case the centralizer of the restriction to
V_{f}
is an orthogonal group rather than a symplectic one. In the case
G=\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)
we replace
f^{*}(X)
by
f^{\dagger}(X)=X^{n}\bar{f}(1/X)/\bar{f}(0)
where
x\mapsto\bar{x}=x^{q}
is the involutory automorphism of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
. We have a similar decomposition of
V=\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}^{n}
into nondegenerate subspaces, the only difference being that if
f=f^{\dagger}
and
\deg(f)=1
(i.e.,
f=X-\lambda
with
\lambda^{q+1}=1
) then the centralizer of the restriction of
g
to
V_{f}
is
\mathrm{GU}_{k}(q)
, and so in (ii) we pay a factor
2\leq q
for each such
f
.

(iv) We use the same notation as in the proof of (ii) and (iii). We have that
g
regular if and only if
g_{f,f^{*}}
is regular for every
f,f^{*}
, which is equivalent to
u=u_{f}
being regular.
If
L=\mathrm{GL}_{k/m}(q^{m})
(resp.
\mathrm{GU}_{k/m}(q^{m/2})
) then
|C_{L}(u)|<(q+1)^{k}
(resp.
|C_{L}(u)|<(q+1)^{k/2}
). If
L=\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{k}(q)
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{k}(q)
, then
u
has at most
h/2
Jordan blocks, so by (i) we deduce that
|C_{L}(u)|<2^{h/2}(q+1)^{k/2}=h(q+1)^{k/2}
. There are at most
(2,q-1)
choices of
f
with
L=\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{k}(q)
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{k}(q)
(namely
f=X\pm 1
), and so we deduce
|C_{G}(g)|<h^{(2,q-1)}(q+1)^{n/2}
, as desired. If
G=\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)
, we have
|C_{G}(g)|<(q+1)^{n}
since
L
is never
\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{k}(q)
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{k}(q)
.
∎

7.4.
Notation and remarks on
Tables

7.1
and
7.2

Let
S
be a simple group of Lie type over a field
\mathbb{F}_{r}
, or a sporadic group. In view of various isomorphisms, we assume
m\geq 3
if
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
,
m\geq 4
if
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{m}(r)
,
m\geq 7
if
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{m}(q)
. Moreover, in order to avoid some technical issues, we assume
S\neq\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r),\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
.

In
Tables

7.1
and
7.2
we define certain elements
x_{i}\in S
, and we now explain our notation for these elements. The notation in

is as in the ATLAS
[

]
. We mention at once that the table is
[

, Table 9]
, except that for

we chose
23A
instead of
13A
.

Let us then focus on
Table

7.1
. We denote by
\Phi_{m}(X)
the
m
-th cyclotomic polynomial, and by
\Phi^{*}_{m}(r)
the product of all primitive prime divisors (ppds) of
r^{m}-1
. Recall that by Zsigmondy’s theorem,
\Phi^{*}_{m}(r)>1
unless
m=2
and
r+1
is a
2
-power, or
(m,r)=(6,2)
. (The notation
\Phi^{*}_{m}(r)
, which is borrowed from
[
27
]
, is not accidental, in the sense that
\Phi^{*}_{m}(r)
always divides the
m
-th cyclotomic polynomials
\Phi_{m}
evaluated at
r
.)
We also denote
\Phi^{\prime}_{8}=r+\sqrt{2r}+1
,
\Phi^{\prime}_{12}=r+\sqrt{3r}+1
,
\Phi^{\prime}_{24}=r^{2}+\sqrt{2r^{3}}+r+\sqrt{2r}+1
.

Definition of
x_{i}

In
Table

7.1
, we define elements
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
via their orders, and for classical groups in each case is straightforward to deduce their action on the natural module. For example,
x_{1}\in\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
acts irreducibly on a nondegenerate
2m-2
-space, and trivially on the perpendicular complement. There are cases where we define only an element
x_{1}
, and the corresponding entry for
x_{2}
is empty.

Note that in some cases the order of
x_{i}
is
1
. For
x_{1}
, this happens when
S
is
\mathrm{PSL}_{6}(2)
or
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
. For
x_{2}
, this happens when
S
is
\mathrm{PSL}_{7}(2)
,
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)
(and
r+1
is a
2
-power),
\mathrm{PSp}_{8}(2)
,
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{12}(2)
, or
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{-}_{8}(2)
. In particular, in all these cases we regard
x_{i}
as being not defined.

As in
Table

7.1
, we set
n_{i}:=|N_{S}(\langle x_{i}\rangle)/C_{S}(x_{i})|
. In the following lemma, we verify this value (item (vi)), at the same time pointing out that the value does not change when passing to a quasisimple cover (item (ii)). Letting
X
be a simple algebraic group over
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{r}}
with a Frobenius endomorphism
F
such that
S=[X^{F},X^{F}]
, we note
that if
x_{i}
is regular then
n_{i}=|N_{X^{F}}(T)/T^{F}|
, where
T
is the maximal torus containing
x_{i}
in
X
(item (iv)); and that
N_{S}(\langle x_{i}\rangle)/C_{S}(x_{i})
acts semiregularly on
\langle x_{i}\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
(item (v)). We also include the case of sporadic groups (
Table

7.2
), where all claims can be verified by inspection of the ATLAS
[
12
]
.

Lemma 7.12
.

Assume
S\neq\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r),\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
. Let
L
be a quasisimple cover of
S
and let
x_{i}
be an element appearing in
Table

7.1
or
Table

7.2
.

(i)

|x_{i}|
is coprime to the order of the Schur multiplier of
S
, and so there exists a unique lift
y_{i}\in L
of
x_{i}
such that
|y_{i}|=|x_{i}|
. (In the items below,
y_{i}
denotes such a lift.)

(ii)

We have
C_{S}(x_{i})=C_{L}(y_{i})/Z(L)
and
N_{S}(\langle x_{i}\rangle)=N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)/Z(L)
.

(iii)

Assume that
S
is of Lie type. Then
x_{i}
is non-regular in
S
if and only if
i=2
and one of the following holds:
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m\equiv 2,3\pmod{4}
;
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)
;
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
or
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
with
m
even.

(iv)

Assume that
S
is sporadic or
S
is of Lie type and
x_{i}
is regular. Then
C_{L}(y_{i})
is abelian. Moreover, if
X
is a simple algebraic group with a Frobenius endomorphism
F
such that
L=[X^{F},X^{F}]
, and if
T
is the unique maximal torus of
X
containing
y_{i}
, we have
C_{L}(y_{i})=T\cap L
and
N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)=N_{L}(T\cap L)
.

(v)

For every nontrivial power
y
of
y_{i}
, we have
C_{L}(y)=C_{L}(y_{i})
and
N_{L}(\langle y\rangle)=N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)
, and so
N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)/C_{L}(y_{i})
acts semiregularly on
\langle y_{i}\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
.

(vi)

|N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)/C_{L}(y_{i})|
is equal to
n_{i}
as listed in
Table

7.1
or
Table

7.2
.

(vii)

Assume that
S
is sporadic or
S
is of Lie type and
x_{i}
is regular. If
\ell
is a prime divisor of
|x_{i}|
, then
\ell\geq 5
and
\langle x_{i}\rangle
contains a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
S
.

Proof.

(i) The last statement (“and so…”) follows from the first. The first statement follows from inspection of the Schur multiplier
M
of
S
and, in groups of Lie type, from the fact that each prime divisor of
\Phi^{*}_{c}(r)
is congruent to
1
mod
c
. Let us handle for example
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(r)
. Each prime divisor of
M
divides
m
,
and each prime divisor of
|x_{1}|
is at least
m+1
, so
(|x_{1}|,M)=1
. As for
x_{2}
, the only possible exception occurs when
m
is a prime divisor of
\Phi^{*}_{m-1}(r)
. But then by definition of ppd, we have that
m
does not divide
r-1
and so does not divide
M
.

(ii) follows immediately from (i).

(iii) Assume first
S
is classical, let
\tilde{S}
be the universal covering group of
S
and let
\tilde{x}_{i}
be a lift of
x_{i}
as in (i). We readily see that
\tilde{x}_{i}
has distinct eigenvalues on the natural module (in which case
x_{i}
is regular) except possibly in the following cases:
i=2
and
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r),\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r),\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\pm}_{2m}(r),\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
; or
i=1
and
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
. In the case
i=2
and
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{-}_{2m}(r)
, or
i=1
and
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
, we have that
\tilde{x}_{i}
centralizes a
2
-space and acts irreducibly on a complement, so
x_{i}
is regular. In the case
i=2
and
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)
, or
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m\equiv 3\pmod{4}
,
\tilde{x}_{i}
centralizes a
2
-space and so
x_{i}
is not regular. Let us go through the remaining cases. If
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m
even or
m\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, a straightforward calculation shows that
\tilde{x}_{2}
has distinct eigenvalues if and only if
m\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}
(that is, if and only if a totally singular irreducible
\mathbb{F}_{r}\langle\tilde{x}_{2}\rangle
-submodule has even dimension). On the other hand, if
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r),\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
then
\tilde{x}_{2}
has distinct eigenvalues if and only if
m
is odd. This concludes the proof if
S
is classical.

Assume now
S
is exceptional. Since
C_{S}(x_{i})
is a subgroup of
S
of maximal rank, it is enough to prove that
C_{M}(x_{i})
is an
r^{\prime}
-group for every maximal subgroup of maximal rank
M
containing
x_{i}
. These are listed in
[
57
]
, and the check is straightforward. For example, assume
S=E_{6}(r)
. For
x_{1}
, by order considerations the only option is
M=\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(r^{3}).3
; for
x_{2}
, the only option is a parabolic with Levi subgroup
D_{5}(r)
. In both cases
C_{M}(x_{i})
is an
r^{\prime}
-group and we are done. Assume now
S=\,^{2}E_{6}(r)
. For
x_{1}
, it must be
M=\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(r^{3}).3
; for
x_{2}
, the only option for
M
is a reductive subgroup of type
{}^{2}D_{5}(r)
, and we are done. Assume now
S=E_{7}(r)
. The options for
M
are reductive subgroups of type
{}^{2}A_{7}(r)
and
A_{1}(r^{7})
. We see that in both cases
C_{M}(x_{1})
is an
r^{\prime}
-group.

(iv) If
S
is sporadic then by inspection of
[
12
]
we have that
\langle x_{i}\rangle
is self-centralizing, from which the claim follows. Assume now
S
is of Lie type and
x_{i}
is regular.
There exists a simple algebraic group
X
and a prime power
s
with
(s,r)\neq 1
such that
L
is an
s
-cover of
[X^{F},X^{F}]
(see
[
39
, Table 5.1.D]
). In particular, it is sufficient to prove the assertion in the case where
L=[X^{F},X^{F}]
. Let us begin with centralizers. A theorem of Steinberg (see
[
63
, Theorem 14.16]
) asserts that
C_{L}(y_{i})=T
if
X
is simply connected, and the general case follows from this and (ii). For what concerns normalizers, certainly
N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)\leq N_{L}(C_{L}(y_{i}))=N_{L}(T\cap L)
. For the other inclusion, we may assume
S=L
. Except for
x_{2}
in
E^{\pm}_{6}(r)
, we see that
T\cap L
is cyclic and so the inclusion holds. For
x_{2}
in
E^{\pm}_{6}(r)
, we readily see that
T\cap L
has a unique subgroup of order
|x_{i}|
, and the inclusion holds also in this case.

(v) If
S
is sporadic, this is immediate since
|x_{i}|
is prime. If
S
is of Lie type and
x_{i}
is regular, the statement follows from the same argument of (iii),(iv) applied to
y
. Assume then
x_{i}
is not regular; the cases are listed in (iii). In all cases,
x_{i}
and every nontrivial power of it fix the same subspaces of the natural module, from which we see that

C_{L}(y_{i})=C_{L}(y)
and
N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)=N_{L}(\langle y\rangle)
, as desired.

(vi) If
S
is sporadic the information is contained in
[
27
, Table 9]
, except for
S=Co_{1}
, in which case we consult
[
12
]
. Assume then
S
is of Lie type. See
Remark

7.13
, below, for the case where
x_{i}
is regular. In the other cases we can check the value of
n_{i}
directly, as follows. Consider
x_{2}\in\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m\equiv 2\pmod{4}
; we work in
S=\mathrm{SU}_{m}(r)
for convenience. Then
N_{S}(\langle x_{2}\rangle)
is an extension field subgroup of type
\mathrm{GU}_{2}(r^{m/2}).(m/2)
, and
N_{S}(\langle x_{2}\rangle)/C_{S}(x_{2})\cong C_{m/2}
. Consider now
x_{2}\in\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
with
m
even; we work in
S=\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
. Put
A=\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{m}(r)
or
A=S
according to whether
r
is odd or even, and note that if
r
is odd then
C_{S}(x_{2})<C_{A}(x_{2})
; in particular
N_{S}(\langle x_{2}\rangle)<N_{A}(\langle x_{2}\rangle)
and so we may compute
N_{A}(\langle x_{2}\rangle)/C_{A}(x_{2})
. Now we have that
N_{A}(\langle x_{2}\rangle)
is an extension field subgroup of type
\mathrm{GU}_{2}(r^{m/2}).m
and
N_{A}(\langle x_{2}\rangle)/C_{A}(x_{2})\cong C_{m}
. The case
x_{2}\in\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
with
m
even is entirely analogous.

(vii) If
S
is sporadic we use
[
12
]
, and if
S
is of Lie type this is a straightforward check. We only note that the parameter
\varepsilon
for
S=G_{2}(r)
ensures that
|x_{1}|
is not divisible by
3
.
∎

Remark 7.13
.

Let us recall that if
x_{i}
is regular, then the value
N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)/C_{L}(y_{i})
can be read off from the Weyl group, as follows.
Assume for convenience that
L=X^{F}
is of simply connected type, so by
Lemma

7.12
(iv) we have
N_{L}(\langle y_{i}\rangle)/C_{L}(y_{i})=N_{X^{F}}(T^{F})/T^{F}
. Write
T=R_{w}
where
R
is a fixed
F
-stable maximal torus and
w\in W:=N_{X}(R)/R
(see
[
63
, Section 25.1]
for the notation used). Since in our case
N_{X^{F}}(T^{F})=N_{X^{F}}(T)
, we have that
|N_{X^{F}}(T^{F})/T^{F}|=|C_{W}(Fw)|
, where we view
Fw
as an element of the coset
FW
of
W\rtimes\langle F\rangle
; see for example
[
63
, Proposition 25.3]
.

We conclude this subsection with another lemma. Recall that
p
is a prime and
K=\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
S
be sporadic or in
\mathrm{Lie}(p^{\prime})
, let
x=x_{i}
be an element in
Table

7.1
or
Table

7.2
, and assume that
m\geq 4
if
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{m}(r)
.
If there exists an irreducible projective
KS
-representation of dimension
d<10n_{i}
, then
S
is either a sporadic group different from
ON,He,Th,Fi_{23},Fi^{\prime}_{24},B,M
, or
S
is one of the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(r),\;(m,r)=(4,2),(4,3),(5,2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r),\;(m,r)=(4,2),(4,3),(5,2),(6,2),(7,2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r),\;(m,r)=(2,3),(2,4),(2,5),(2,7),(3,2),(3,3),(4,2),(4,3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\pm}_{8}(2),\mathrm{P}\Omega_{7}(3),\,^{2}\!B_{2}(8),G_{2}(3),\,^{3}\!D_{4}(2),F_{4}(2).

Proof.

For sporadic groups we consult
[
32
]
. For groups of Lie type, we consult
[
44
]
and we are reduced to the groups in the statement, with the addition of
\mathrm{PSL}_{6}(2),\mathrm{PSL}_{7}(2)
, which can be excluded with
[
32
]
.
∎

7.5.
Invariable generation of groups of Lie type

We need a result on invariable generation of simple groups of Lie type. Recall that elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}

invariably generate
a group
G
if
\langle x_{1}^{g_{1}},\ldots,x_{t}^{g_{t}}\rangle=G
for every
g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\in G
. We write
\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\rangle_{I}=G
in this case. We consider the set of the following finite simple groups:

(7.2)

\displaystyle E_{6}(r),\,^{2}E_{6}(r);

\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(r)
,
m\geq 5
and
(m,r)\neq(5,2),(6,2),(7,2),(11,2),(13,2),(19,2),(5,3),(7,3),(7,5)
;

\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
,
m\geq 5
and
(m,r)\neq(5,2),(5,3),(13,2)
;

\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)
,
m\geq 3
,
r
odd;

\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
,
m\geq 5
odd;

\mathrm{P}\Omega^{-}_{2m}(r)
,
m\geq 4
,
r
odd;

\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
,
m\geq 4
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
S
be as in (
7.2
) and let
x_{1},x_{2}
be as in
Table

7.1
. Then
\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle_{I}=S
.

Proof.

Our main tool is
[
28
, Corollary 3.4]
. This result (mostly relying on
[
31
]
) classifies the irreducible subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}_{m}(r)
,
m\geq 5
, containing elements of order
\Phi^{*}_{e_{i}}(r)
,
i=1,2
, with
e_{1}>e_{2}>m/2
. Sometimes this result will not apply, because we have at our disposal only one such element. Whenever this is the case, we will use
[
27
, Theorem 2.2]
, which classifies the irreducible subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}_{m}(r)
containing an element of order
\Phi_{e}^{*}(r)
with
\Phi_{e}^{*}(r)>2e+1
. By
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
, the inequality
\Phi_{e}^{*}(r)>2e+1
is satisfied in all but a handful of cases, which either do not appear in (
7.2
) or will be dealt with separately. (We point out that item (2) in
[
28
, Theorem 3.3 and Corollary 3.4]
should be amended, to include the case where
m
is prime and
\Phi^{*}_{m-1}(r)=m
, in which case
\mathrm{GL}_{1}(r^{m}).m
contains elements of orders both
\Phi^{*}_{m}(r)
and
\Phi^{*}_{m-1}(r)
. By
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
, the only such cases are the ones listed in the second line of
Equation

7.2
, other than
(6,2),(7,2)
. This is why we excluded these cases for
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(r)
.)

In all cases below, we denote by
H
the subgroup of
S
generated by any conjugates of
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
, so our aim is to show that
H=S
. Assume first
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(r)
, so by assumption
m\geq 5
. Note
H
is irreducible and
H
does not preserve an extension field subgroup (see the last sentence in the previous paragraph).
Moreover, if
r=\ell^{a}
where
\ell
is prime then
|x_{1}|
is divisible by
\Phi^{*}_{am}(\ell)>1
, which rules out subfield subgroups. A classical subgroup does not have order divisible by both
|x_{1}|
and
|x_{2}|
, so it is ruled out.
Since by assumption
(m,r)\neq(6,2),(7,2)
, by
[
28
, Corollary 3.4]
there are no other possibilities (note that cases (4), (5), and (6) in that corollary are ruled out at once), so
H=S
, as desired.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
, so
m\geq 5
.
Assume first
m
is odd. Then
H
is irreducible, and we claim that
H
does not preserve an extension field subgroup. If
m\equiv 3\pmod{4}
this holds since
m
is odd and so
m
and
m-2
are coprime. If
m\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, the only option would be a subgroup
\mathrm{GU}_{1}(r^{m}).m
. But since we excluded the cases
(m,r)=(5,2),(5,3),(13,2)
, we deduce from
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
that
\Phi^{*}_{m-1}(r)>m
and so this possibility is excluded (note that
\Phi^{*}_{m-1}(r)
divides
|x_{2}|
).
Moreover, as above, a subfield subgroup does not have order divisible by
|x_{1}|
, so it is ruled out. Again by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
, we have that
\Phi^{*}_{m}(r^{2})>2m+1
(since
m
is odd), so we deduce by
[
27
, Theorem 2.2]
that there are no remaining possibilities and so
H=S
. Assume then
m
is even. Again
H
is irreducible. If
\Phi^{*}_{m-1}(r^{2})>2(m-1)+1
, then by
[
27
, Theorem 2.2]
we deduce
H=S
.
And if
\Phi^{*}_{m-1}(r^{2})\leq 2(m-1)+1
, then by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
, the only possibility is that
r^{2}=4
and
m-1=3
or
m-1=6
, none of which is under consideration in
(
7.2
), so again
H=S
.

Assume next
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)
, so by assumption
m\geq 3
and
r
is odd. Since
(m,r)\neq(3,2),(4,2)
,
[
28
, Corollary 3.4]
applies. Classical subgroups do not occur (
\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2m}(r)
is ruled out since
r
is odd). An extension field subgroup would necessarily preserve a structure over
\mathbb{F}_{r^{2}}
. A subgroup of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(r^{2})
is excluded by looking at
x_{2}
; a subgroup of type
\mathrm{GU}_{m}(r)
is excluded because one of
m
and
m-1
is even. Subfield subgroups are excluded by looking at
|x_{1}|
, as above. By
[
28
, Corollary 3.4]
if
(m,r)\neq(3,3)
there are no remaining possibilities and so
H=S
. If
(m,r)=(3,3)
, the conclusion follows from
[
28
, Corollary 3.4]
, since
A_{7}
(in the fully deleted permutation module) embeds in
\Omega^{\pm}_{6}(3)
rather than
\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(3)
.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{-}_{2m}(r)
with
m\geq 4
and
r
odd. Since
(m,r)\neq(4,2)
,
[
28
, Corollary 3.4]
applies and we argue as in the symplectic case.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
, so
m\geq 4
and
r
is odd.
Note that
H
is irreducible. (Indeed, both
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
fix a nondegenerate
1
-space, but one has square discriminant and the other has non-square discriminant.) Here
[
28
, Corollary 3.4]
does not apply and we use
[
27
, Lemma 2.1 and Theorem 2.2]
for the element
x_{1}
.
By
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
we have
\Phi^{*}_{2m}(r)>4m+1
; and since
m\geq 4
, by
[
27
, Theorem 2.2]
we deduce that
H=S
.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m}^{+}(r)
, so
m\geq 5
is odd. Note that
H
is irreducible. We use
[
27
, Lemma 2.1 and Theorem 2.2]
. Since
m
is odd,
H
does not preserve an extension field subgroup. (The crux is that
x_{1}
cannot belong to a subgroup
\mathrm{GU}_{m}(r)
, which happens instead for
m
even.)
Assume first
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)>4(m-1)+1
. Then by
[
27
, Theorem 2.2]
we see that
H=S
. Assume finally
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)\leq 4(m-1)+1
. Since
2(m-1)\geq 8
and
m
is odd, by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
one of the following holds:
(m,r)=(7,2)
(and
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)=2(m-1)+1
) or
(m,r)=(5,2),(11,2)
(and
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)=4(m-1)+1
).
If
(m,r)=(7,2)
, we have
|x_{1}|=13
and
|x_{2}|=\Phi^{*}_{7}(2)=127
. Now
[
31
]
lists the possibilities for the overgroups of
x_{1}
. With their notation, we have already excluded Examples 2.1, 2.2, 2.4. Example 2.5 does not arise as we have
r=2
, and the groups in Example 2.3 do not contain an element of order
127
. Finally, Examples 2.6–2.9 consist of an explicit list of almost simple acting (projectively) absolutely irreducibly. All these can be ruled out, as either they do not contain an element of order
127
, or the representation does not have degree
14
. The cases
(m,r)=(5,2),(11,2)
can be handled in the same way, using
[
31
]
(note that in the first case
|x_{2}|=\Phi^{*}_{5}(2)=31
, and in the second case
|x_{2}|=\Phi^{*}_{11}(2)=2047=23\cdot 89
).

Assume now
S=E_{6}(r)
; we look at the list of maximal subgroups in
[
14
, Tables 2 and 9]
. We have
|x_{1}|=\Phi^{*}_{9}(r)\geq 37
(by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
), and we see that the overgroups of
x_{1}
are of type
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(r^{3})
and
{}^{2}E_{6}(r^{1/2})
.
None of these can contain
x_{2}
and we are done.

Assume finally
S=\,^{2}E_{6}(r)
; we look at
[
14
, Tables 3 and 10]
. Note
|x_{1}|=\Phi^{*}_{18}(r)
is equal to
19
for
r=2
, and is at least
73
for
r>2
(by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
). The overgroups of
x_{1}
are a subgroup of type
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(r^{3})
, and
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(19)
(for
r=2
).
None of these can contain
x_{2}
and
H=S
.
∎

7.6.
Generation of groups of Lie type by conjugates

Next, we need a result on generation of simple groups of Lie type by two conjugate elements.
Consider the set of the following finite simple groups:

(7.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)\text{ with $m$ even and $(m,r)\neq(4,2)$ }

\displaystyle E_{7}(r)

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
S
be as in
(
7.3
)
. If
S\neq\mathrm{PSL}_{4}^{-}(r)
then let
x=x_{1}
be as in
Table

7.1
; if
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{4}^{-}(r)
then let
x=x_{2}
be as in
Table

7.1
. Then
S
is generated by two conjugates of
x
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{c}=\mathcal{M}_{c}(x)
be a set of representatives for the conjugacy classes of maximal subgroups containing
x
. For
M\in\mathcal{M}_{c}
, the number of conjugates of
M
containing
x
is
\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M)
. For each such conjugate, say
M^{g}
, the probability that a random conjugate of
x
belongs to
M^{g}
is
|x^{G}\cap M|/|x^{G}|=\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M)/|S:M|
. Letting
P
the probability that
x
and a random conjugate do not generate, we deduce by a union bound that

(7.4)

P\leq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{c}}\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M)^{2}}{|S:M|}.

In particular, it will be enough to show that the right-hand side of (
7.4
) is less than
1
, and in order to achieve this we will mostly use
Lemma

2.1
. Let also
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(x)
be the set of maximal subgroups of
S
containing
x
. Note that if
|\mathcal{M}(x)|=1
, say
\mathcal{M}(x)=\{M\}
, then the right-hand of (
7.4
) is at most
1/|S:M|<1
. When
S
is classical, we will work with the cover of
S
acting faithfully on the natural module, without changing notation.

Let us start from
S=\mathrm{SL}_{4}(r)
where
r=\ell^{a}
, so
|x|=\Phi^{*}_{4}(r)
and each prime divisor of
|x|
is
\equiv 1\pmod{4}
. The case
r=2
can be checked with GAP. Assume then
r\geq 3
. Note that
x
is contained in a unique extension field subgroup
M_{1}
of type
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(r^{2})
, see for example
[
9
, Lemma 2.12]
, so we have

\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{1})^{2}}{|S:M_{1}|}=\frac{1}{|S:M_{1}|}=\frac{2}{r^{4}(r^{3}-1)(r-1)}<r^{-6}.

Moreover,
x
is contained in a subgroup
M_{2}=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(r).(2,r-1)
, and in a subgroup
M_{3}=\mathrm{SO}^{-}_{4}(r).(4,r-1)
, the latter being maximal only if
r
is odd. In both cases, we have
x^{S}\cap M_{i}=x^{M_{i}}
, and we see from
Lemma

2.2
that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{i})^{2}}{|S:M_{i}|}\leq r^{-2}.

For example, for
i=2
, setting
M_{0}=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(r)
and
d=(2,r-1)
we have

(7.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{2})^{2}}{|S:M_{2}|}

\displaystyle=\frac{|C_{S}(x)|^{2}}{d^{2}|C_{M_{0}}(x)|^{2}}\cdot\frac{|M_{2}|}{|S|}

\displaystyle=\frac{(r^{4}-1)^{2}}{(r-1)^{2}(r^{2}+1)^{2}d^{2}}\cdot\frac{d}{r^{2}(r^{3}-1)}\leq r^{-2}.

In particular, we have

(7.6)

\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{i})^{2}}{|S:M_{i}|}\leq dr^{-2}+r^{-6}<1.

Next, recalling that
r=\ell^{a}
, we have that
\Phi^{*}_{4a}(\ell)
divides
\Phi^{*}_{4}(r)
. Since
\Phi^{*}_{4a}(\ell)>1
, subfield subgroups are ruled out. (We note at once that in this proof, subfield subgroups will always be ruled out with this argument.) Consulting the list of maximal subgroups of
S
in
[
7
, p. 381]
, we see that the only possible other overgroups of
x
are symplectic type subgroups and covers of
A_{7}
and
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)
, occuring only (possibly) when
|x|=5
. By
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
, we have
|x|=5
only if
r=2,3
; and in these cases, only
A_{7}
arises among the overgroups above, for
r=2
. We are assuming
r\geq 3
and so we conclude from (
7.4
) and
Equation

7.6
.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{SU}_{4}(r)
and
|x|=\Phi^{*}_{2}(r^{2})
. We check the cases
r=2,3
with GAP so assume
r\geq 4
. Then
|x|\geq 13
by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
. We deduce from
[
7
, p. 382]
that the only overgroups of
x
are the stabilizer
M_{1}
of a totally singular
2
-space, the stabilizer
M_{2}
of a decomposition into totally singular
2
-spaces, and subfield subgroups
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(r).d
and
\mathrm{SO}^{-}_{4}(r).e
, where
d:=(2,r-1)
and
e:=(4,r+1)/2
(there are
d
and
e
classes, respectively); the orthogonal subgroup being maximal only for
r
odd. We have
\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{i})=1
for
i=1,2
. Setting
M_{0}=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(r)
, we can calculate
\mathrm{fix}(g,S/M_{3})^{2}/|S:M_{3}|
precisely, similarly to (
7.5
); we see that this quantity is at most
1/r^{3}
, and the same holds for
M_{4}
. The same holds also for
M_{3}^{a}
and
M_{4}^{a}
with
a\in\mathrm{Aut}(S)
, therefore

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{i})^{2}}{|S:M_{i}|}+d\cdot\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{3})^{2}}{|S:M_{3}|}+e\cdot\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{3})^{2}}{|S:M_{3}|}

\displaystyle\leq|S:M_{1}|^{-1}+|S:M_{2}|^{-1}+2r^{-3}<1.

Assume now
S=\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
with
m\geq 4
even and
(m,r)\neq(4,2)
, so
|x|=\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)
. Then
x
stabilizes a unique nondegenerate
2
-space and
r+1
nonsingular
1
-spaces. Moreover,
x
lies in a unique extension field subgroup of type
\mathrm{GU}_{m}(r)
if
m
is even, and of type
\mathrm{O}_{m}(r)
if
m
is odd.

Let us first address the case
m=4
; see
[
7
, pp. 402–403]
for the list of maximal subgroups of
G
, taken from
[
36
]
. Letting
A=\langle\text{Inndiag}(\overline{S}),\tau\rangle
where
\overline{S}=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(r)
and
\tau
is a triality automorphism, we note that for
r
odd (resp.
r
even) there are six (resp. three)
\overline{S}
-classes of subgroups
\Omega_{7}(r)
(resp.
\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(r)
) conjugate under
A
, four of which (resp. two of which) act (projectively) irreducibly via the spin module. (Note that
\tau
does not lift to an automorphism of
S
; this is why we considered
\overline{S}
here.) Let
M_{1}\leq S
be the preimage of a representative for this
A
-class; we can take
M_{1}
to be the stabilizer of some nonsingular
1
-space. Furthermore, there are three
\overline{S}
-classes of subgroups isomorphic to the stabilizer of a nondegenerate
2
-space of minus type, which are conjugate under
A
, and two of which are extension field subgroups of type
\mathrm{GU}_{4}(r)
. Let
M_{2}\leq S
be the preimage of a representative for this
A
-class.

Finally, for
2<r\equiv 2\pmod{3}
, setting
d=(2,r-1)
there are
d^{2}

S
-classes of subgroups
d\times\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(r).3
, acting irreducibly via the adjoint module.
Let
M_{3}
be one such subgroup. Notice that
x^{S}\cap M_{3}
is the union of at most six
M_{3}
-classes. Indeed, if
y\in x^{S}\cap M_{3}
, then
y
is irreducible as an element of
\mathrm{PGU}_{3}(r)
; let
\alpha
be an eigenvalue of a lift of
y
to
\mathrm{GU}_{3}(r)
. Then
\beta:=\alpha^{r^{2}-1}
is an eigenvalue of
x
,
and notice that
\alpha
can be uniquely recovered from
\beta
.
Since
x
has six nontrivial eigenvalues, it follows that
x^{S}\cap M_{3}
is indeed the union of at most six
M_{3}
-classes. Letting
x_{1},\ldots,x_{c}
(
c\leq 6
) be representatives for such classes, we have
|C_{S}(x_{i})|/|C_{M_{3}}(x)|\leq r+1

and so by
Lemma

2.1
we deduce

\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M_{3})^{2}}{|S:M_{3}|}\leq\frac{36(r+1)^{2}}{|S:M_{3}|}.

Next, letting
x\mapsto\overline{x}
denote the map
S\to\overline{S}
, notice that
\overline{x}^{A}=\overline{x}^{\overline{S}}
. (Indeed,
\overline{x}
belongs to a subgroup
G_{2}(r)
and so is centralized by a triality automorphism.) In particular, for every
a\in A
and every
i=1,2,3
, we have
\mathrm{fpr}(\overline{x},\overline{S}/\overline{M_{i}})=\mathrm{fpr}(\overline{x},\overline{S}/\overline{M_{i}}^{a})
. Consulting
[
7
]
, we see that there are no other overgroups. For
r
odd, by (
7.4
) we then get

P\leq 6\frac{(r+1)^{2}}{4|S:M_{1}|}+3\frac{1}{|S:M_{2}|}+144\frac{(r+1)^{2}}{|S:M_{3}|}<1.

Assume then
m>4
. By
[
27
, Theorem 2.2]
, if
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)>4(m-1)+1
, then the only overgroups are the ones mentioned in the first paragraph handling
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
, and we conclude similarly to the case
m=4
.

Assume finally
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)\leq 4(m-1)+1
, so by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
, recalling that we are assuming
m
even, we see that
(m,r)=(6,2),(10,2)
, with
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)=2(m-1)+1
.
For
m=6
we can consult
[
7
]
and there are no other overgroups. (Recall that the imprimitive subgroup of type
\mathrm{O}_{1}(r)\wr S_{2m}
is not maximal.) For
m=10
, we can consult

[
31
]
; we find subgroups
J_{1}
and
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(19)
. (Note that
A_{22}
embeds into
\mathrm{Sp}_{20}(2)
, but not into
\Omega^{+}_{20}(2)
, via the fully deleted permutation module; see for example
[
39
, p. 187]
.)
In these cases, from
Lemma

2.1
we have

\frac{\mathrm{fix}(x,S/M)^{2}}{|S:M|}\leq\frac{|C_{S}(x)|^{2}}{|S:M|}<\frac{2^{24}}{|S:M|},

from which the result follows.

Assume finally
S=E_{7}(r)
. We have
|x_{1}|=\Phi^{*}_{14}(r)\geq 43
by
[
27
, Lemma 2.1]
. By
[
17
, Tables 1.1, 1.2 and 4.1]
, the only maximal subgroups containing
x_{1}
are
M_{1}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r^{7}).7
and a subgroup
M_{2}
of type
\mathrm{PSU}_{8}(r)
. We have
|C_{S}(x_{1})|\leq r^{7}+1
and so

P\leq\frac{|C_{S}(x_{1})|^{2}}{|S:M_{1}|}+\frac{|C_{S}(x_{1})|^{2}}{|S:M_{2}|}<1.

The proof is concluded.
∎

7.7.
Generation of alternating groups

We also need a simple result on the generation of alternating groups.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\ell
and
s
be (not necessarily distinct) primes in
(m/2,m-3]
. Then
A_{m}
is generated by one
\ell
-cycle and one
s
-cycle.

Proof.

Choose
x
an
\ell
-cycle and
y
an
s
-cycle such that
\langle x,y\rangle
is transitive. Since
x
cannot preserve nontrivial blocks,
\langle x,y\rangle
is primitive. Finally
\langle x,y\rangle
contains a cycle of prime length fixing at least three points and so
\langle x,y\rangle=A_{m}
by Jordan’s theorem.
∎

Lemma 7.18
.

Assume that
m\geq 16
. Then there are at least two distinct primes in
(m/2,m-3]
.

Proof.

If
m\geq 50
, then by
[
66
]
there is a prime in
(m/2,\left\lfloor{3m/5}\right\rfloor]
,
and a prime in
(\left\lfloor{3m/5}\right\rfloor,18m/25]
. Since
18m/25\leq m-3
, the proof in this case is complete. If
16\leq m\leq 49
, then one checks the statement directly.
∎

Lemma 7.19
.

For
m\geq 11
, there are two distinct primes
\ell
and
s
such that
\ell,s\geq 7
and
A_{m}=\langle x_{1},y_{1}\rangle=\langle x_{2},y_{2}\rangle
where
|x_{1}|=|y_{1}|=\ell
and
|x_{2}|=|y_{2}|=s
.

Proof.

For
m\geq 16
the statement follows from
Lemmas

7.17
and
7.18
. For
11\leq m\leq 15
we can use GAP.
∎

8.
Classical groups: classes
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{8}

Throughout this section,
p
is a prime number,
q
is a power of
p
, and
K=\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. Moreover,
G
is a classical group with natural module
V=\mathbb{F}_{q^{u}}^{n}
, where
u=2
if
G
is unitary and
u=1
otherwise. In view of various isomorphisms, we assume
n\geq 3
if
G=\mathrm{PSU}_{n}(q)
,
n\geq 4
if
G=\mathrm{PSp}_{n}(q)
,
n\geq 7
if
G=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. Recall also that
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(9)=A_{6}
,
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(2)=A_{8}
,
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(3)
,
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(4)=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)=A_{5}
(see for example
[
39
, Proposition 2.9.1]
).

Aschbacher
[
2
]
partitioned the members of
\mathcal{A}(G)
into nine classes, which are generally denoted
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{8},\mathcal{S}
(see also
[
39
,
49
]
). It will be convenient for us to introduce a further class
\mathcal{N}
, which we define below. In this section, we handle classes
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{8}
and
\mathcal{N}
. We will handle class
\mathcal{S}
in
Section

9
, and we postpone a brief description of it to that section.

Classes
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{8}
are sometimes called “geometric”, in that the subgroups in these classes preserve natural structures on the natural module. We devote one subsection to each class, and at the beginning of each subsection, we give brief information on the class. We refer to
[
39
, Chapter 4]
for detailed descriptions of the subgroups in each class.

Class
\mathcal{N}
is non-empty only for
G=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(q)
, and
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
with
q
even. Put
\delta=3
if
G=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(q)
, and
\delta=2
if
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
. Then
\mathrm{Aut}(G)
has a normal subgroup
\Gamma
of index
\delta
, such that
\mathrm{Aut}(G)\smallsetminus\Gamma
contains a graph automorphism of order
\delta
. We let
\mathcal{N}
be the set of members of
\mathcal{A}(G)
that do not extend to maximal subgroups of almost simple groups contained in
\Gamma
. (In particular, by the definition of
\mathcal{A}(G)
each of them extends to a maximal subgroup of some almost simple group not contained in
\Gamma
.)

For convenience, in the proofs we will replace
G
by its quasisimple cover acting faithfully on
V
. We first handle some groups computationally (also for class
\mathcal{S}
).

Lemma 8.1
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
G
is one of the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)\emph{with$q\leq 9$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(q)\emph{with$q\leq 8$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q)\emph{with$q\leq 3$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(q)\emph{with$q\leq 8$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(q)\emph{with$q\leq 3$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)\emph{with$q\leq 4$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2)

Proof.

If
G\neq\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2)
we use GAP. For each maximal subgroup
M
of
G
, we perform a random search in
M
and find
g\in M
with
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)<|G:M|^{1/3}
. For the cases where
M\in\mathcal{A}(G)
but
M
is not maximal in
G
, as well as for the case
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2)
, we directly inspect
[
12
]
and find
g\in M
with
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)<|G:M|^{1/3}
.
∎

8.1.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{1}

Subgroups
M
in class
\mathcal{C}_{1}
are stabilizers of certain subspaces
U
of the natural module
V
. If
G
preserves some nondegenerate form, then
U
is nondegenerate, or totally singular, or a nonsingular
1
-space in orthogonal groups in even characteristic. What is more, if
U
is nondegenerate, then
U
is not similar to
U^{\perp}
.
We also include the case where
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
M
is the stabilizer of a flag or antiflag.

Lemma 8.2
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.

Proof.

If
M
is parabolic, let
g\in M
be a regular unipotent element, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
by
Lemma

6.3
.
Assume then
M
is not parabolic. We go through all remaining cases.

Assume
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
, so
M
is the stabilizer of a nondegenerate subspace
U_{1}
of dimension
m_{1}<n/2
, and set
m_{2}=n-m
. Write
V=U_{1}\perp U_{2}
and, according to this decomposition, let
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}
where
g_{i}
is as follows.
If
m_{i}
is odd, then
g_{i}
acts irreducibly.
If
m_{i}=2
then
g_{i}
is diagonal with distinct eigenvalues.
If
m_{i}\neq 2
is even, then
g_{i}
acts irreducibly on each of a pair of complementary totally singular subspaces
A
and
B
, and moreover
A\not\cong B
as
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}\langle g_{i}\rangle
-modules. It is possible to choose
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
so that
\operatorname{det}(g)=1
, and such that
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
have no common eigenvalue, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.

Assume now
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, so
M
is the stabilizer of a nondegenerate subspace
U_{1}
of dimension
m_{1}<n/2
. Write
V=U_{1}\perp U_{2}
, and accordingly take
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}
where
g_{i}
acts irreducibly, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.

Assume now
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. Assume first
M
is the stabilizer of a nondegenerate subspace
U_{1}
of dimension
m_{1}\leq n/2
and type
\delta_{1}
; set
m_{2}=n-m
and let
\delta_{2}
be the type of
U_{2}:=U_{1}^{\perp}
. We have that
U_{1}
is not similar to
U_{2}
, and so
m_{1}<n/2
unless
\varepsilon=-
and
n\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.
According to the decomposition
V=U_{1}\perp U_{2}
, we choose
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}
where
g_{i}
is as follows.

If
m_{i}
is odd, then
g_{i}
acts irreducibly on a nondegenerate hyperplane of minus type and trivially on a complement. If
m_{i}
is even and
\delta_{i}=-
, then
g_{i}
acts irreducibly.
If
(m_{i},\delta_{i},q)=(4,+,2)
then
g_{i}
acts irreducibly on a nondegenerate
2
-space and trivially on a complement. If
m_{i}
is even and
\delta_{i}=+
and
(m_{i},q)\neq(4,2)
then
g_{i}
acts irreducibly on each of a pair of complementary totally singular subspaces
A
and
B
, and moreover either
(m_{i},q)=(2,3)
and
g_{i}=-1
, or
(m_{i},q)=(2,2)
and
g_{i}=1
, or
A\not\cong B
as
\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle g_{i}\rangle
-modules. It is possible to choose
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
so that
g\in\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(q)
, and such that moreover
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
have no nontrivial irreducible submodule in common. In particular, we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
unless we are in one of the following cases:

(i)

\delta_{1}=-
and
m_{2}=m_{1}+1
, in which case
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq 2
.

(ii)

m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are odd, in which case
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq q+1
(the number of
1
-subspaces of a
2
-space).

(iii)

(m_{1},\delta_{1},q)=(2,+,3)
and
m_{2}
is odd, in which case
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq 3
(one plus the number of nondegenerate
1
-subspaces of
U_{1}
).

Assume now
M=\mathrm{Sp}_{n-2}(q)
is the stabilizer of a nonsingular
1
-space with
q
even. We take
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}
where
g_{1}
acts irreducibly on a nondegenerate
(n-2)
-space and
g_{2}
is trivial on a nondegenerate
2
-space
U
.
Then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)
is the number of nonsingular
1
-spaces of
U
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq q+1
,
and
(q+1)^{3}<q^{n-1}(q^{n}-1)\leq|G:M|
.

The only remaining case is when
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
with
n\geq 3
and
M
is the stabilizer of an antiflag
V=U_{1}\oplus U_{2}
with
\dim(U_{1})\neq\dim(U_{2})
. (Note that the stabilizer of a flag in
G
is parabolic and so it has been considered in the first paragraph of the proof.)
We choose
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
where
g_{i}
is irreducible on
U_{i}
,
so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.
∎

8.2.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{2}

Subgroups
M
in class
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are stabilizers of direct sum decompositions
V=V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{t}
of the natural module, where
\dim(V_{i})=m
and
t>1
. If
G
preserves a nondegenerate form, then the
V_{i}
are either nondegenerate and similar, or totally singular; in the latter case
t=2
. In all cases we have
n=mt
and
M\leq\mathrm{GL}(V_{1})\wr S_{t}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{m}(K)\wr C_{t}
preserve the decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{t}
, inducing a
t
-cycle on the spaces, and denote by
g^{\prime}
the restriction of
g^{t}
to
V_{1}
. Then
\operatorname{det}(g)=(-1)^{m(t-1)}\operatorname{det}(g^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We have that
g
is conjugate in
\mathrm{GL}_{m}(K)\wr C_{t}
to
(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau
, where
\tau
is an element of order
t
inducing a
t
-cycle on the spaces. The lemma follows from the fact that
\operatorname{det}(\tau)=(-1)^{m(t-1)}
.
∎

Lemma 8.4
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.

Proof.

Assume
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
. Let
g=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau\in G
, where
g^{\prime}\in\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)
is irreducible,
\tau
is a
t
-cycle, and
\operatorname{det}(g^{\prime})\operatorname{det}(\tau)=1
. (By a
t
-cycle, we mean an element of order
t
inducing a
t
-cycle on the spaces.)
Then
g
is regular, and therefore
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q^{n}-1<q^{n}
.
Now by
Lemma

7.9
,
|G:M|\geq q^{n(n-m)-2}/t!
.
Bounding
t!\leq q^{n\log_{2}(n)}
,
m\leq n/2
, and
n\leq q^{\log_{2}(n)}
, we see that if
n\geq 14
then

|C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{3n}<q^{n(n-m)-2}/t!\leq|G:M|,

and the result follows from
Lemma

2.1
.
Analyzing now the cases
n\leq 13
,
we see that the inequality
q^{3n}<q^{n(n-m)-2}/t!
holds unless
(n,m)=(6,3)
or
2\leq n\leq 5
. Moreover, replacing
q^{n(n-m)-2}/t!
with the precise value of
|G:M|
, we further exclude the case
n=5
.

Now, by
[
39
,
7
]
, since
M\in\mathcal{A}
, if
m=1
then
q\geq 5
; if
m=2
then
q\geq 3
; if
n=2
then
q\neq 5
. We also have
(n,q)\neq(3,2),(3,3)
in view of
Lemma

8.1
.

In the remaining cases with
m=n/2
(namely,
(n,m)=(6,3),(4,2),(2,1)
), choose
1\neq g=(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{GL}_{n/2}(q)^{2}\cap G
such that
g_{1}
is a Singer cycle and
g_{2}
is a diagonal element satisfying
\operatorname{det}(g_{1})\operatorname{det}(g_{2})=1
.
Note that
V
does not decompose as the sum of two equivalent
\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle g^{2}\rangle
-modules.
In particular, a conjugate of
g
cannot belong to
M\smallsetminus\mathrm{GL}_{n/2}(q)^{2}
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.

The remaining cases are
(n,m)=(3,1),(4,1)
, with
q\geq 5
.
Assume
n=3
, so
q\geq 7
in view of
Lemma

8.1
.
Let
g
be a diagonal element with eigenvalues
(\lambda,\lambda^{-1},1)
where
\lambda
generates
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. Then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
, since
g^{j}
does not act homogeneously on a
j
-space for
j=2,3
.

Assume finally
n=4
. For
q=5
, let
g\in M
be a regular (semisimple) element inducing a
3
-cycle on the spaces; concretely, for example,
g=(\lambda,1,1,-1)\tau
where
\tau=(1,2,3)
and
|\lambda|=4
. For
q\geq 7
, let
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{1}(q)^{4}\cap G
be regular (semisimple).
In both cases we have
|C_{G}(g)|<(q^{4}-1)/(q-1)
, and
(q^{4}-1)^{3}/(q-1)^{3}<|G:M|
.

Assume now
G=\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}^{+}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
. In most cases, it will be sufficient to note that
M
contains a suitable regular element. First note that a
t
-cycle
\tau
of
S_{t}
belongs to
\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{n}(q)\smallsetminus\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
if and only if
q\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and

m\equiv 2\pmod{4}
and
t
is even, and otherwise
\tau\in\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
. This follows from the fact that
\tau
fixes complementary totally singular subspaces
A
and
B
, and has determinant
(-1)^{m(t-1)/2}
on both; recall that in this situation,
\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
consists of the elements whose determinant on
A
(and
B
) is a square in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
(see for example
[
39
, Lemma 4.1.9]
). Next, we have
(m,q)\neq(2,2),(2,3),(2,4),(4,2)
by
[
7
, Proposition 2.3.6]
. In particular, if
(m,q)\neq(2,5),(4,3)
, we can choose
g=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau\in\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
where as an
\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle g^{\prime}\rangle
-module
V_{1}=A\oplus B
, where
A
and
B
are totally singular and irreducible, and
A\not\cong B
. Then
g
has distinct eigenvalues, and the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{O}^{+}_{n}(q)
is isomorphic to the centralizer of a regular element of
\mathrm{GL}_{n/2}(q)
;
therefore
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{n/2}
. Similarly to the
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
case, it can be easily checked that
q^{3n/2}<|G:M|
, which concludes the proof. We are left with
(m,q)=(2,5),(4,3)
.
In these cases, setting
W=V_{1}\perp\cdots\perp V_{t-1}
, according to the decomposition
V=W\perp V_{t}
we choose
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}
, where
g_{1}=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau\in\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{n-m}(q)\smallsetminus\Omega^{+}_{n-m}(q)
is defined as above (in particular, note that
g^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{m}(q)\smallsetminus\Omega^{+}_{m}(q)
and
|g^{\prime}|=4
for
(m,q)=(2,5)
, and
|g^{\prime}|=8
for
(m,q)=(4,3)
), and
g_{2}
is defined as follows: If
(m,q)=(2,5)
then
g_{2}=g^{\prime}
, and if
(m,q)=(4,3)
then as an
\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle g_{2}\rangle
-module
V_{t}=C\perp D
, where
C
and
D
are of minus type,
g_{2}
is trivial on
D
and has order
4
on
C
. Next we divide into the two cases. Assume first
(m,q)=(4,3)
; note that
g
is regular, and
|C_{G}(g)|<2q^{n/2-2}(q+1)^{2}
, which gives the conclusion for
n\geq 12
. For
n=8
, it is easy to see that
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
(since
V
does not decompose as the sum of two equivalent
\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle g^{2}\rangle
-modules).
Assume then
(m,q)=(2,5)
and denote by
\lambda
a generator of
\mathbb{F}_{5}^{\times}
. Then
g
is not necessarily regular; specifically,
g
is regular if and only if
t
is odd.
If
g
is not regular, then for
\alpha=\lambda^{\pm 1}
there are two
\alpha
-Jordan blocks, of sizes
1
and the
5
-part of
t-1
. In particular, by working in
\mathrm{GL}_{n/2}(q)
we see that
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{n/2+2}
. We have
q^{3n/2+6}<|G:M|
and the result follows.

The other cases are similar; we will list the choice of the elements, generally omitting the details of the calculations. If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
is of type
\mathrm{GU}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
, we have
(m,q)\neq(2,2)
by
[
7
, Proposition 2.3.6]
. We choose
g=(g^{\prime},1\ldots,1)\tau
where
g^{\prime}
is irreducible for
m
odd, and irreducible on a hyperplane for
m
even, and
\tau
is a
t
-cycle. We have that
g
is regular and so
|C_{G}(g)|<(q+1)^{n}
by
Lemma

7.11
(iv). Similarly to the
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
case we reduce to the cases
n\leq 4
or
(n,m)=(6,3)
. If
m=n/2
then we choose
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}\in\mathrm{GU}_{n/2}(q)^{2}\cap G
where
|g_{1}|=q^{3}+1
for
n=6
(resp.
|g_{1}|=q^{4}-1
for
n=4
) and
g_{2}
is diagonal; we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
. The remaining cases are
(n,m)=(3,1),(4,1)
; in view of
Lemma

8.1
we have
q\geq 7
and
q\geq 5
in the respective cases and we can choose a diagonal regular (semisimple) element
g
, such that moreover
\mathrm{fpr}(g,G/M)=1
for
n=3
.
If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
, then
(m,q)\neq(2,2)
by
[
7
, Proposition 2.3.6]
. We choose
g=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau
where
g^{\prime}
is irreducible and
\tau
is a
t
-cycle; then
g
is regular and
|C_{G}(g)|<(q+1)^{n/2}
(since
g
does not have eigenvalues
\pm 1
; see the proof of
Lemma

7.11
(iv)).
As above, we reduce to the case
(n,m)=(4,2)
.
We have
q\geq 5
by
Lemma

8.1
and
we can choose a regular semisimple element
g
of
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)^{t}
of order
q+1
with
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.
Assume now
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}^{-}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
, so
\varepsilon=(-1)^{t}
. If
q
is odd and
(m,q)\neq(2,3)
, we choose
g=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau\in\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
where
g^{\prime}
irreducible, so
g
is regular. (Note
\operatorname{det}(\tau)=1
, and since
(m,q)\neq(2,3)
, we can choose
g\in\Omega^{-}_{m}(q)
or
\mathrm{SO}^{-}_{m}(q)\smallsetminus\Omega^{-}_{m}(q)
so that
g\in\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(q)
.)
If
(m,q)=(2,3)
, we write
V=W\perp V_{t}
where
W=V_{1}\perp\cdots\perp V_{t-1}
, and accordingly we choose
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}
where
g_{1}=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau\in\mathrm{SO}^{\varepsilon}_{n-2}(q)
is as above, and either
g_{2}=1
or
|g_{2}|=4
, so that
g\in\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. If
g_{2}=1
then
g
is regular; if
|g_{2}|=4
then the dimension of the centralizer in
\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
is at most
n/2+2
, which gives the conclusion. Assume then
q
is even. Then we choose
g=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau\in\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
where
g^{\prime}
is either irreducible, or irreducible on a nondegenerate codimension
2
subspace and a reflection on the perpendicular complement (according to whether
\tau\in\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
or not). Then
g
is regular. Assume now
M
is of type
\mathrm{O}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
where
mq
is odd and
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
with
\varepsilon\in\{+,-,\circ\}
. Suppose first
m\geq 3
; we have
(m,q)\neq(3,3)
by
[
7
, Proposition 2.3.6]
. We may then choose
g=(g^{\prime},1,\ldots,1)\tau\in\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
where
g^{\prime}
is irreducible on a hyperplane and
\pm 1
on the complement. Assume now
m=1
. Then the
t
-cycle
\tau
has determinant
(-1)^{t-1}
. If
t
is odd then choose
g=\tau\in\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
, so
g
is regular.
If
t
is even then choose
g=\tau^{\prime}
a
(t-1)
-cycle, so
g
is also regular since it has at most two
1
-Jordan blocks, one of which of size one (see
[
56
, Theorem 3.1]
). Assume now
M
is of type
\mathrm{O}_{n/2}(q)^{2}
where
nq/2
is odd. Then we may choose
g=g_{1}\perp g_{2}
where
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are regular and
g
is also regular. Finally, if
M
is of type
\mathrm{GL}_{n/2}(q^{u}).2
with
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q),\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
and
u=1
, or
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
u=2
, then we may choose an irreducible element
g
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n/2}(q^{u})
that is regular in
G
and the result follows.
∎

8.3.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{3}

Subgroups in class
\mathcal{C}_{3}
are normalizers of subfields
E
of
\mathrm{End}(V)
, where
E
is a field extension of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{u}}
of degree
r
and
r
divides
n
. (Equivalently, these are the normalizers of
E^{\times}
, which is a cyclic group of order
(q^{u})^{r}-1
acting homogeneously on
V
with irreducible submodules of dimension
r
.) We have
C_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)}(E)\cong\mathrm{GL}_{n/r}(q^{r})
and
N_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)}(E)\cong\mathrm{GL}_{n/r}(q^{r})\rtimes\text{Gal}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{r}}/\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We call
N_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)}(E)
an
r
-extension field subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)
. We record the following

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
g\in G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)
and assume
C_{G}(g)=C_{G}(g^{r})
. Then, the number of
r
-extension field subgroups of
G
containing
g
is equal to the number of cyclic subgroups
R
of
C_{G}(g)
order
q^{r}-1
acting homogeneously on
V=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
.

Proof.

Simply note that if
g\in N_{G}(R)
then
g^{r}\in C_{G}(R)
, and so
R\leq C_{G}(g^{r})=C_{G}(g)
.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
.

Proof.

Let us start with
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, so
M
is of type
\mathrm{GL}_{n/r}(q^{r})
for a prime divisor
r
of
n
. Let
g\in M
act irreducibly and of order
(q^{n}-1)/(q-1)
. If
n\geq 3
or
(n,q)\neq(6,2)
, then by Zsigmondy’s theorem
|g|
is divisible by a ppd of
q^{n}-1
, so
g^{r}
acts irreducibly. This holds also if
n=2
or
(n,q)=(6,2)
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
by
Lemma

8.5
(or
[
9
, Lemma 2.12]
).

Assume now
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{GU}_{n/r}(q^{r})
for an odd prime divisor
r
of
n
. If
n/r
is odd, then let
g\in M
be an element of order
(q^{n}+1)/(q+1)
acting irreducibly on
V
; as above
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
. If
n/r
is even, we let
g\in M
be of order
(q^{n}-1)/(q+1)
, acting irreducibly on each of a pair of complementary maximal totally singular spaces
A
and
B
. We see that
A\not\cong B
as
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}\langle g^{r}\rangle
-modules. In particular, setting
L=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q^{2})
we have that
C_{L}(g)=C_{L}(g^{r})=H\times K
where
H
and
K
are Singer cycles on
A
and
B
(and
H
and
K
have the same eigenvalues). Then, by
Lemma

8.5
the number of
r
-extension field subgroups of
L
containing
g
is
r
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq r
and the result follows.

Assume next
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{n/r}(q^{r})
. We choose
g\in M
of order
q^{n/2}+1
. Since
(n,q)\neq(6,2)
(see
Lemma

8.1
), by Zsigmondy’s theorem we have that
|g|
is divisible by a ppd of
q^{n}-1
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
by
Lemma

8.5
.

Assume
G=\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}^{+}_{n/r}(q^{r})
, so
n/r\geq 4
since
M\in\mathcal{A}
. Then we let
g\in\Omega^{+}_{n/r}(q^{r})
be of order
(q^{n/2}-1)/(2,q-1)
, acting irreducibly on each of a pair of complementary maximal totally singular subspaces
A
and
B
. We deduce
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq r
as in the unitary case.

Now let
G=\Omega^{-}_{n}(q)
. If
M
is of type
\mathrm{O}^{-}_{n/r}(q^{r})
with
n/r\geq 4
, we let
g\in\Omega^{-}_{n/r}(q^{r})
be of order
(q^{n/2}+1)/(2,q-1)
, and then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
. If
M
is of type
\mathrm{O}_{n/r}(q^{r})
with
n/r\geq 3
odd, then we choose
g\in\Omega_{n/r}(q^{r})
of order
(q^{(n-r)/2}+1)/(2,q-1)
, acting irreducibly on a nondengenerate
(n-r)
-space
A
and trivially on
B=A^{\perp}
. Setting
L=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)
, we have that
C_{L}(g)=C_{L}(g^{r})=H\times\mathrm{GL}_{r}(q)
where
H
is a Singer cycle on
A
. Then, by
Lemma

8.5
the number of
r
-extension field subgroups of
L
containing
g
is equal to the size of the conjugacy class of a Singer cycle in
\mathrm{GL}_{r}(q)
, which is
s:=|\mathrm{GL}_{r}(q)|/(q^{r}-1)
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq s
.

Finally, if
M
is of type
\mathrm{GU}_{n/2}(q)
and
G
is symplectic or orthogonal, we choose
g
of order
(q^{n/2}+1)/(2,q-1)
if
n
is odd, and
(q^{(n-1)/2}-1)/(2,q-1)
if
n
is even; we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq r
.
∎

8.4.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{4}

Subgroups
M
in class
\mathcal{C}_{4}
are stabilizers of tensor product decompositions
V=V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
where
\dim(V_{i})=n_{i}
and
n=n_{1}n_{2}
. If
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
then
n_{1}\neq n_{2}
. If
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are equipped with a nondegenerate form then they are not similar; so in all cases
M\leq\mathrm{GL}(V_{1})\otimes\mathrm{GL}(V_{2})
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

Proof.

Assume first
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{GL}_{n_{2}}(q)
where
1<n_{1}<n_{2}<n
and
n_{1}n_{2}=n
. In particular,
n\geq 6
. Let
g=g_{1}\otimes g_{2}\in M
where
g_{1}\in\mathrm{SL}_{n_{1}}(q)
is regular semisimple (e.g., irreducible), and
g_{2}\in\mathrm{SL}_{n_{2}}(q)
is regular unipotent. Then
g
is regular,
and so
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q^{n}-1
,
from which

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+3n}<|G|

and the conclusion follows from
Lemma

2.1
.

Assume now
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, so
M
is of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n_{2}}(q)
with
n_{1}n_{2}=n
,
q
odd,
and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-,\circ\}
.
Since
M\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
n_{2}\geq 3
(see
[
39
, Proposition 4.4.4]
). Now we let
g=g_{1}\otimes g_{2}\in M
, where
g_{1}\in\mathrm{Sp}_{n_{1}}(q)
is unipotent with a single Jordan block
and
g_{2}\in\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n_{2}}(q)
is irreducible if
\varepsilon=-
, irreducible on complementary totally singular subspaces if
\varepsilon=+
, irreducible on a nondegenerate hyperplane if
\varepsilon=\circ
.
Then
g
is regular, and moreover
|C_{G}(g)|\leq 2(q+1)^{n/2}
(since
g
does not have both
1
and
-1
as eigenvalues; see the proof of
Lemma

7.11
(iv)). We then see that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
.

The other cases are
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{GU}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{GU}_{n_{2}}(q)
;
G=\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{Sp}_{n_{2}}(q)
or
\mathrm{O}^{\xi_{1}}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{O}^{\xi_{2}}_{n_{2}}(q)
(in the latter case,
q
odd and
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\neq(\circ,-),(-,\circ)
);
G=\Omega_{n}(q)
with
nq
odd and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{O}_{n_{2}}(q)
;
G=\Omega^{-}_{n}(q)
with
q
odd and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{O}^{-}_{n_{2}}(q)
with
n_{1}
odd. Except when
M
is of type
\mathrm{O}^{\xi_{1}}_{n_{1}}(q)\otimes\mathrm{O}^{\xi_{2}}_{n_{2}}(q)
with
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
even, we can choose
g=g_{1}\otimes g_{2}
where one element
g_{i}
is unipotent with one Jordan block, and the other element
g_{j}
is semisimple with distinct eigenvalues, so
g
is regular. In the remaining case, we can choose
g_{1}
unipotent with two Jordan blocks of size
n_{1}/2
, and
g_{2}
semisimple with distinct eigenvalues. Then the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
is
n
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

8.5.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{5}

Subgroups in class
\mathcal{C}_{5}
are classical subgroups defined over a proper subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{u}}
. The possibilities are listed in
[
39
, §4.5]
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.

Proof.

Assume first
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, so
M
is of type
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q_{0})
where
q=q_{0}^{b}
and
b
is prime. Let
g\in M
be of order
(q_{0}^{n}-1)/(q_{0}-1)
. Setting
M_{0}=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q_{0})
, we claim that
g^{G}\cap M\subseteq M_{0}
. Indeed, let
x\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q_{0})
and
z\in Z(\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q))
, and assume
(xz)^{a}=g
with
a\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)
. Then
x^{a}
and
g
belong to the same maximal torus
T
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)
, and moreover they are defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q_{0}}
. In particular, either
x^{a}
acts irreducibly over
\mathbb{F}_{q_{0}}
, or it acts diagonally over
\mathbb{F}_{q_{0}}
, the latter case occurring only when
n=b
.
In the first case,
x^{a}\in\langle g\rangle
and so
z=x^{-a}g\in\langle g\rangle\leq\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q_{0})
, and so
xz\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q_{0})
, as desired. In the second case, we have that if
\alpha
is an eigenvalue of
g
then
