\alpha^{q_{0}-1}\in\mathbb{F}_{q_{0}}
,
which is easily seen to be impossible unless
(q_{0},n)=(3,2)
.
Since
b=n
, in this exceptional case we have
q=9
, which has been handled in
Lemma

8.1
.
Therefore
g^{G}\cap M\subseteq M_{0}
, as claimed. What is more,
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M_{0}}
, since all semisimple elements of
M_{0}
with the same characteristic polynomial are conjugate in
M_{0}
, and so by
Lemma

2.2
we get

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{M_{0}}(g)||M:M_{0}|}\leq\frac{(q^{n}-1)(q_{0}-1)}{(q-1)(q_{0}^{n}-1)|M:M_{0}|}<\frac{(q/q_{0})^{n-1}}{|M:M_{0}|}.

We also have

|G:M|=\frac{|G:M_{0}|}{|M:M_{0}|}>\frac{(q/q_{0})^{n^{2}-1}}{|M:M_{0}|}.

In particular, using
|M:M_{0}|\geq 1
we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)^{3}<|G:M|
.

Assume next
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
is of type
\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q_{0})
where
q=q_{0}^{b}
and
b
is an odd prime. We have
(n,q)\neq(3,8)
in view of
Lemma

8.1
. Assume first also
(n,q)\neq(4,8)
. If
n
is odd, let
g\in M
be of order
(q_{0}^{n}+1)/(q_{0}+1)
, and if
n
is even, let
g\in M
be of order
q_{0}^{n-1}+1
. (The exclusion of the two above cases ensures that
|g|
is divisible by a ppd of
q_{0}^{2n}-1
or
q_{0}^{2n-2}-1
.)
Setting
M_{0}=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q_{0})
, by the same argument as in the
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
case we have
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M_{0}}
, and so

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq\frac{(q^{n}+1)(q_{0}+1)}{(q+1)(q_{0}^{n}+1)|M:M_{0}|}.

Moreover
|G:M|=|G:M_{0}|/|M:M_{0}|
, and using
n\geq 3
we see that
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)^{3}<|G:M|
. Recall now that we excluded the case
(n,q)=(4,8)
. In this case we have
M=\mathrm{SU}_{4}(2)
and we choose
g\in M
of order
5
.
Then
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
and

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{M}(g)|}=\frac{455}{5}<|G:M|^{1/3},

which concludes the proof in this case. The other cases are similar. If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
we choose
g
of order
q^{n/2}+1
or
q^{(n-1)/2}+1
or
q^{n/2}-1
(depending on
\varepsilon
). If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, we choose
g
of order
q^{n/2}+1
. If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q_{0})
, where
q=q_{0}^{b}
, we choose
g
of order
q_{0}^{n/2}+1
. If
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}^{\xi}_{n}(q)
where
q=q_{0}^{b}
and
\varepsilon=\xi^{b}
, we choose
g
of order
(q_{0}^{n/2}+1)/(2,q-1)
or
(q_{0}^{(n-1)/2}+1)/(2,q-1)
or
(q_{0}^{n/2}-1)/(2,q-1)
(depending on
\xi
).
In all cases, a similar argument as above applies.
∎

8.6.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{6}

Subgroups
M
in class
\mathcal{C}_{6}
are normalizers of symplectic type subgroups.

Let
r
be a prime, and
E
an
r
-group of symplectic type such that
|E|=r^{2m+1}
,
E
is of exponent
r\cdot(2,r)
and
E
is as in Table
8.1
(see
[
39
, §4.6]
). We have
n=r^{m}
and
V=V_{n}(q^{u})
is a faithful absolutely irreducible
\mathbb{F}_{q^{u}}E
-module, where

q^{u}\equiv 1\hbox{ mod }r
. Moreover,
M\leq N_{\mathrm{GL}(V)}(E)
, where
N_{\mathrm{GL}(V)}(E)/EZ
is as in the table (here
Z=Z(\mathrm{GL}(V))
).

Table 8.1.

\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|}\hline\cr E&n&N_{\mathrm{GL}(V)}(E)/EZ\\
\hline\cr r^{1+2m},\,r\hbox{ odd}&r^{m}&\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(r)\\
4\circ 2^{1+2m}&2^{m}&\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(2)\\
2_{\pm}^{1+2m}&2^{m}&\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2m}(2)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

We begin with a known fact, cf.

[
76
, p. 706]
. This reference addresses only the case of elements of prime power order, but the proof works in higher generality, as we now record.

Lemma 8.9
.

With notation as above, let
g\in N_{\mathrm{GL}(V)}(E)
act with all orbits of size
|g|
on the nonzero vectors of
E/Z(E)\cong\mathbb{F}_{r}^{2m}
. Then the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
has dimension
1+(r^{2m}-1)/|g|
. In particular, if
|g|=r^{m}+1
then
g
is regular in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
.

Proof.

Let
V_{K}:=K^{n}
, let
K[E]
be the group algebra of
E
and let
\phi\colon K[E]\to\mathrm{End}_{K}(V_{K})
be the morphism corresponding to the
K[E]
-module
V_{K}
; this is also a morphism of
K\langle g\rangle
-modules. Since
E
acts irreducibly,
\phi
is surjective. Moreover, we have
J:=\mathrm{Ker}(\phi)=(z-\zeta 1)K[E]
, where
Z:=Z(E)=\langle z\rangle
and
z
acts by the scalar
\zeta
on
V_{K}
. Therefore
\mathrm{End}_{K}(V_{K})\cong K[E]/J
as
K\langle g\rangle
-modules. Let
\{a_{1}Z,\ldots,a_{t}Z\}
be an orbit of
g
on
E/Z(E)\smallsetminus\{0\}
, so
t=|g|
. Then there exist
z_{1},\ldots,z_{t}\in Z
such that
\{a_{1}z_{1},\ldots,a_{t}z_{t}\}
is an orbit of
g
on
E
. Now, any set of representatives for the cosets of
Z
in
E
maps (via the natural projection) to a
K
-basis of
K[E]/J
.
In particular, there exists a
K
-basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n^{2}}
of
K[E]/J
such that
v_{1}g=v_{1}
and such that
g
permutes the other
v_{i}
in orbits of size
|g|
. The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{6}
.

Proof.

Assume
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, so
n=r^{m}
with
r
prime and
r\neq p
.
If
r
is odd then
q\equiv 1\pmod{r}
; and if
r=2
and
n>2
, then
M
is of type
E.\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(2)
, where
E=C_{4}\circ 2^{1+2m}
, and
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
. For
n=2
it is also possible that
q\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
M\leq Q_{8}.S_{3}
.

Assume first
n=2
. Then, letting
E
be the quaternion group of order
8
, we have
M=E.S_{3}=E.\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2}(2)
or
M=E.3
. If
M=E.S_{3}
(resp.
M=E.3
), let
g\in M
be an element of order
3
(resp.
4
). Then
g
is regular in
G
and so
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q+1
. Moreover, letting
\delta
be the number of elements of
M
of order
3
(resp.
4
) in
M
, we have
\delta=8
(resp.
\delta=6
), so
|g^{G}\cap M|\leq\delta
and

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|g^{G}\cap M||G:M|}{|g^{G}|}\leq\frac{\delta(q+1)}{|M|}<|G:M|^{1/3}.

Assume next
n=3
, so
E.Q_{8}\leq M\leq E:\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(3)
, where
E
is an extraspecial group of order
27
and exponent
3
. We may assume
q\geq 7
in view of
Lemma

8.1
.
Let
g\in M
be of order
4
, so
g
is regular semisimple in
G
by
Lemma

8.9
, and
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q^{2}-1
.
The number of elements of
M
of order
4
is
54
. (Indeed, letting
Z
be the center of
M
, we have
M/Z\geq\mathbb{F}_{3}^{2}\rtimes Q_{8}
, which has
9\cdot 6
elements of order
4
,
and each of these admits a unique lift in
G
of order
4
.)
Therefore

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|g^{G}\cap M||G:M|}{|g^{G}|}\leq\frac{54(q^{2}-1)}{|M|}<|G:M|^{1/3},

since
|M|\geq 27\cdot 8
. Now assume
n=4
, so
(4\circ E).A_{6}\leq M\leq(4\circ E).\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(2)
, where
E
is any extraspecial group of order
2^{5}
. Then by
Lemma

8.9
an element of
M
of order
5
is regular in
G
, and a similar calculation as above suffices. Finally, assume
n\geq 5
, so
M=ZE.\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(r)
where
Z=Z(\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q))
(see
[
39
, Propositions 4.6.5 and 4.6.6]
). Let
g\in M
be of order
r^{m}+1
, so
g
is regular in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
by
Lemma

8.9
. Moreover, we have
C_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)}(g)\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)
, and so
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q^{n}-1)/(q-1)
.
Therefore

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<(q-1)r^{2m^{2}+m}r^{2m+2}\frac{(q^{n}-1)^{3}}{(q-1)^{3}}<|G|.

Assume now
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, so
E.\Omega^{-}_{2m}(2)\leq M\leq E.\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2m}(2)
, where
n=2^{m}
and
E=D_{8}\circ\cdots\circ D_{8}\circ Q_{8}
is an extraspecial group
of order
2^{2m+1}
. Let
g\in M
be of order
2^{m}+1
, so
g
is regular in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
by
Lemma

8.9
, and the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
is
n/2
, and moreover
|C_{G}(g)|<4(q+1)^{n/2}
by
Lemma

7.11
(iv). For
n\geq 8
we see that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
and the result follows. Assume then
n=4
. We have
q\geq 5
in view of
Lemma

8.1
. We have
|g|=5
, and if
p\neq 5
then
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q^{2}+1
, and if
p=5
then
|C_{G}(g)|=2q^{2}
. The number of elements of
E.\mathrm{O}^{-}_{4}(2)=E.S_{5}
of order
5
is
16\cdot 24=384
,
so

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|g^{G}\cap M||G:M|}{|g^{G}|}\leq\frac{384\cdot 2q^{2}}{|M|},

which is
<|G:M|^{1/3}
for
q\geq 5
.

The other cases are similar. Assume
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
E.\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(r)
where
E
is extraspecial of order
r^{2m+1}
or
E=C_{4}\circ R
where
R
is extraspecial of order
2^{2m+1}
. Exactly as in the linear case, if
n=3
(resp.
n=4
, resp.
n\geq 5
) then we choose
g
of order
4
(resp.
5
, resp.
r^{m}+1
), so
g
is regular in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
. Finally, assume
G=\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
and
E.\Omega^{+}_{2m}(2)\leq M\leq E.\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2m}(2)
, where
n=2^{m}
and
E=D_{8}\circ\cdots\circ D_{8}
is an extraspecial group
of order
2^{2m+1}
. Let
U:=E/Z(E)
and let
g\in M
be of order
2^{m}-1
, such that as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}\langle g\rangle
-module
U=A/Z(E)\oplus B/Z(E)
, where
A/Z(E)
and
B/Z(E)
are totally singular and irreducible.
By
Lemma

8.9
, the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
is
n+2
. By
Lemma

7.10
(ii), it follows that the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
is at most
(n+2)/2
, and we conclude with a similar calculation as above.
∎

8.7.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{7}

Subgroups
M
class
\mathcal{C}_{7}
are stabilizers of tensor product decompositions
V=V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{t}
where
m=\dim(V_{i})
and
n=m^{t}
. If the
V_{i}
are equipped with a nondegenerate form then they are similar; in all cases we have
M/Z\leq\mathrm{PGL}(V_{1})\wr S_{t}
.

Lemma 8.11
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{7}
.

Proof.

First, we have
n\geq 8
since
M\in\mathcal{A}
(see
[
7
]
). Let
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and let
M
be of type
\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
, with
n=m^{t}
and
m>1
,
t>1
. Since
M\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
m\geq 3
(see
[
39
, Section 4.7]
). Let
g=x\otimes\cdots\otimes x\otimes y\in\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)^{\otimes t}
where
x
has order
(q^{m}-1)/(q-1)
and
y
is regular unipotent. Then each generalized Jordan block of
g
over
K
has size
m
, and each such block occurs with multiplicity at most
m^{t-2}
. (A generalized Jordan block refers to a Jordan block corresponding to any eigenvalue in
K
.) Therefore, the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
is at most
m^{2t-2}
(cf. proof of
Lemma

7.5
). Hence
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{m^{2t-2}}
and

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<t!q^{m^{2}t+3m^{2t-2}}<|G|.

Assume now
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
, with
qt
odd.
Then we may choose an element as above but where
x
has order
q^{m/2}+1
.
Then, the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
is at most
m^{2t-2}/2+n/2
(see
[
56
, Theorem 3.1]
). Moreover,
g
has
n/m=m^{t-1}
generalized Jordan blocks over
K
, so by
Lemma

7.11
(iii) we have
|C_{G}(g)|<2^{m^{t-1}}q^{m^{2t-2}/2+n/2}\leq q^{m^{t-1}+m^{2t-2}/2+n/2}
, and so

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<t!q^{m^{2}t/2+mt/2+3m^{t-1}+3m^{2t-2}/2+3n/2}

which is less than
|G|
if
n>8
(i.e.,
n\geq 32
). If
n=8
, since
M\in A
we have
q\geq 5
(see
[
7
]
), and
M=\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)^{\otimes 3}.2^{2}.S_{3}
and
g
has two
1
-Jordan blocks of dimension
2
, and one rational
f
-block of dimension
4
, where
f
is the minimum polynomial of an element of order
(q+1)/2
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. (A rational
f
-block refers to an indecomposable
\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle g\rangle
-submodule whose minimum polynomial is
f
.) In particular, the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
is
2+4=6
(see
[
56
, Theorem 3.1]
), and
|C_{G}(g)|=q^{4}|\mathrm{GU}_{1}(q)||\mathrm{O}^{\pm}_{2}(q)|\leq 2q^{4}(q+1)^{2}

and we see that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
.

The other cases are similar. These are
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{GU}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
;
G=\Omega^{+}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}_{m}^{\pm}(q)\wr S_{t}
(
q
odd) or
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
(
qt
even);
G=\Omega_{n}(q)
with
qn
odd and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}_{m}(q)\wr S_{t}
. Except for the case where
M
is of type
\mathrm{O}_{m}^{\pm}(q)\wr S_{t}
, we can choose
g=x\otimes\cdots\otimes x\otimes y
where
x
is semisimple with distinct eigenvalues and
y
unipotent with one Jordan block, as above. In the exceptional case, we have
m\geq 4
and we choose
y
unipotent with two Jordan blocks of size
m/2
. We have that all generalized Jordan blocks of
g
over
K
have size
m/2
, and each such block occurs with multiplicity at most
2m^{t-2}
, so the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
(resp.
\mathrm{O}_{n}(K)
) is at most
2m^{2t-2}
(resp. at most
m^{2t-2}
, see
[
56
, Theorem 3.1]
), and the conclusion follows.
∎

8.8.
Class
\mathcal{C}_{8}

Subgroups in class
\mathcal{C}_{8}
are classical subgroups with the same natural module as
G
.

Lemma 8.12
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.

Proof.

Assume first
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, so
M
is of type
\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
,
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, or
\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. Assume first
M
is of type
\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
, so
n\geq 3
and
M=\langle M_{0},y\rangle
where
M_{0}=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
and
y\in\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q^{1/2})Z
with
Z=Z(\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q))
. Then choose
g\in M_{0}
of order
(q^{n/2}+1)/(q^{1/2}+1)
if
n
is odd, and order
q^{(n-1)/2}+1
if
n
is even. First note that
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M_{0}}
. Indeed, assume
g^{a}=xz
where
a\in G
,
x\in\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
and
z\in Z
. Then
x
and
g^{a}
belong to the same maximal torus
T
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(q)
. If
n
is odd then
T
is cyclic and irreducible and, since
x
and
g^{a}
both belong to unitary subgroups, we deduce that they must belong to the same cyclic subgroup
C
of
T
order
q^{n/2}+1
. In particular
g^{a}\in C\leq M
, which proves that
g^{G}\cap M\subseteq M\cap\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)=M_{0}
. Moreover,
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M_{0}}
since all irreducible elements of
M_{0}
with the same characteristic polynomial are conjugate in
M_{0}
. The case where
n
is even is analogous and so we have
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M_{0}}
in all cases. Therefore by
Lemma

2.2
we get

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|M:M_{0}||C_{M_{0}}(g)|}\leq\frac{(q^{n-\delta}-1)}{A(q^{(n-\delta)/2}+1)}=\frac{q^{(n-\delta)/2}-1}{A}

where
A=\delta=1
if
n
is even, and
A=q^{1/2}-1
and
\delta=0
if
n
is odd.
Next, we have
|M|\leq(q^{1/2}-1)|M_{0}|

and so by
Lemma

7.9
we deduce
|G:M|\geq|G:M_{0}|/(q^{1/2}-1)>9q^{n^{2}/2-1/2}/(16(q^{1/2}-1))
, and we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)^{3}<|G:M|
as soon as
n\geq 4
. Assume then
n=3
, so
q>5
in view of
Lemma

8.1
. Then we have
M=\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q^{1/2})\times(3,q^{1/2}-1)
(see
[
7
]
), and we still have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)^{3}<|G:M|
, by using the exact value of
|G:M|
, rather than the above approximation.

Assume next
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
with
\varepsilon\in\{+,-,\circ\}
. Suppose
\varepsilon=+
, so
n\geq 4
is even and
q
is odd, and
M=\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{n}(q)\cap G
.
Setting
M_{0}=\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{n}(q)
, we choose
g\in M_{0}
of order
q^{n/2}-1
. Then
g
is regular semisimple in
G
(note that
(q,n)\neq(2,4)
).
No element of
\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{n}(q)\smallsetminus\mathrm{O}^{+}_{n}(q)
can have the same eigenvalues as
g
, so as above we have
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M_{0}}

and

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{M_{0}}(g)|}\leq\frac{(q^{n/2}-1)^{2}}{(q-1)(q^{n/2}-1)}<|G:M|^{1/3}.

Assume now
n\geq 3
is odd and
M
is of type
\mathrm{O}_{n}(q)
. Setting
M_{0}=\mathrm{SO}_{n}(q)
, we choose
g\in M_{0}
of order
q^{(n-1)/2}+1
and similarly to above we have

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{M_{0}}(g)|}\leq\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q^{(n-1)/2}+1}=q^{(n-1)/2}-1<|G:M|^{1/3}.

The cases
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
M
of type
\mathrm{O}^{-}_{n}(q)
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
ar similar; in both cases we choose
g
of order
q^{n/2}+1
. Finally, for
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
M=\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
with
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
and
q
even, letting
g\in M
be an element without eigenvalue
1
, we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.
∎

8.9.
Class
\mathcal{N}

Recall that class
\mathcal{N}
was defined at the beginning of
Section

8
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
G=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(q)
, or
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
with
q
even, and
M
is in class
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Assume first
G=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(q)
; we go through the possibilities given in
[
7
, Table 8.50]
, which is taken from
[
36
]
. For convenience, we work in
G=\Omega^{+}_{8}(q)
, and put
d=(2,q-1)
. Using the notation as in the table, we are only concerned with novelties
\mathrm{N_{1}}
and
\mathrm{N_{4}}
. If
M
is parabolic, we use
Lemma

6.3
.
Assume now
M\cong d\times G_{2}(q)
. We choose
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)<M
of order a ppd of
q^{3}-1
.
Then
g
preserves a nondegenerate
6
-space
W
of plus type (acting irreducibly on complementary maximal totally singular subspaces),
so
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q^{3}-1)(q-1)
.
Moreover
|C_{M}(g)|=d(q^{3}-1)/(q-1)
and
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
(note all cyclic subgroups of
G_{2}(q)
of order
|g|
are conjugate).
Therefore

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{M}(g)|}\leq\frac{(q-1)^{2}}{d}<|G:M|^{1/3}.

Assume next
M=(\Omega^{+}_{2}(q)\times\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{GL}_{3}(q)).[2d]
with
q\geq 3
; then
M
is the stabilizer of
\{A,B\}
where
A
and
B
are totally singular
3
-spaces with trivial intersection and
A\oplus B
nondegenerate; see
[
36
, proof of Proposition 3.2.3]
. The subgroup
H=\Omega^{+}_{2}(q)\times\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{GL}_{3}(q)
preserves
A
and
B
. We let
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)<H
be of order a ppd of
q^{3}-1
, acting trivially on
(A\oplus B)^{\perp}
. Note that if
g
lies in a conjugate of
M
, then it lies in the corresponding conjugate of
H
; but
g
fixes precisely two totally singular
3
-spaces and so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.

Next let
M=(\Omega^{-}_{2}(q)\times\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{GU}_{3}(q)).[2d]
with
q\geq 3
. The subgroup
H=\Omega^{-}_{2}(q)\times\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{GU}_{3}(q)
is the stabilizer in
\mathrm{GU}_{4}(q^{2})\cap G
of a nondegenerate
1
-space for the unitary geometry (see
[
36
, proof of Proposition 3.2.2]
). We let
g\in H
be of order a ppd of
q^{6}-1
if
q\neq 2
, and of order
9
if
q=2
. Similarly to the previous case we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
.

Assume finally
M=(D_{2(q^{2}+1)/d})^{2}[2d].S_{2}
. Then
M
is the normalizer of a Sylow
r
-subgroup of
G
, where
r
is an odd prime divisor of
q^{2}+1
; we have that
M
stabilizes a decomposition
V=V_{1}\perp V_{2}
into
4
-spaces of minus type (see
[
36
, Proof of Proposition 3.3.1]
). Let
g\in M
be of order
(q^{2}+1)/d
, acting irreducibly on
V_{1}
and centralizing
V_{2}
. Then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)
is equal to the number of Sylow
r
-subgroups of
\Omega(V_{2})\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
, which is
q^{2}(q^{2}-1)/2
, and

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)^{3}=\frac{q^{6}(q^{2}-1)^{3}}{8}<|G:M|.

The proof for
\Omega^{+}_{8}(q)
is complete.

Assume now
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
with
q
even; the classes in
\mathcal{N}
can be found in
[
2
, Section 14]
(see also
[
7
]
). If
M
is parabolic, then we use
Lemma

6.3
. If
M=(C_{q-1})^{2}\colon D_{8}
, then
M
is the normalizer of a nondegenerate
2
-space of plus type in
\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{4}(q)<G
. We let
g\in C_{q-1}^{2}<M
be of order
q-1
with eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm 1},\lambda^{\pm 2}
on the natural module
V
;
then
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
, since
g
fixes only two nondegenerate
2
-spaces.
Assume next
M=(C_{q+1})^{2}\colon D_{8}
, so
M
is the normalizer of a nondegenerate
2
-space of minus type in
\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{4}(q)<G
. We let
g\in M
be of order
q+1
, with distinct eigenvalues on
V
, and similarly to the previous case we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
. Assume finally
M=C_{q^{2}+1}\colon 4
, so
M
is the normalizer of a subgroup
C_{q^{2}+1}
. Then we let
g\in M
be of order
q^{2}+1
, and we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=1
as in the proof of
Lemma

8.6
.
The proof is now complete.
∎

9.
Classical groups: class
\mathcal{S}

In this section we complete the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. The only remaining case is when
G
is classical and
M
is in the class
\mathcal{S}
of subgroups. Throughout this section,
V=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
denotes the natural module for
G
, where
q
is a power of the prime
p
.
(This is a change of notation from Section
8
, where the natural module is
\mathbb{F}_{q^{u}}^{n}
– so in this section, the unitary case is
G=\mathrm{PSU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
.)
Class
\mathcal{S}
consist of the members
M
of
\mathcal{A}(G)
that do not belong to
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{8},\mathcal{N}
. It was proved by Aschbacher
[
2
]
(see also
[
49
]
) that these subgroups
M
are almost simple. Moreover, if
L
denotes the quasisimple cover of
S=\text{Soc}(M)
acting faithfully on
V
, then
V
is an absolutely irreducible
\mathbb{F}_{q}L
-module, which cannot be realized over a proper subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and finally, if
G=\mathrm{PSL}_{n}(q)
then
L
fixes no nondegenerate unitary or bilinear form on
V
.

9.1.
Strategy of proof

We outline now the strategy of proof of
Theorem

3.1
for
M
in class
\mathcal{S}
.
Letting
L
be as in the previous paragraph, we seek an element
g\in L
such that
C_{G}(g)
is small. This is much less straightforward than in the previous section, since the embedding
L\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
is not in a known list. As we shall briefly explain now, we will be able to construct such an element merely from algebraic properties of
L
. Our element
g
will almost always be among the elements in
Table

7.1
and
Table

7.2
.

In order to bound
|C_{G}(g)|
, a first key step is to bound
\dim(C_{V}(g))
, and for this we use generation properties of
L
(borrowing ideas and results from
[
27
]
and earlier work). If
L
is generated by two conjugates of
g
, then by the irreducibility of
L
we clearly have
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/2
, which already is a useful bound. If
L
has the stronger property of being generated by three conjugates of
g
with product equal to 1, then in fact
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/3
by Scott’s lemma (
Lemma

7.3
). When
L=S
is simple,
[
27
]
produces such an element. However, when
L\neq S
, this is not so straightforward, and we use invariable generation (
Lemma

7.15
) together with a result of Gow (
[
29
]
) in order to get to the same conclusion. In fact there are some cases for which neither of these approaches works, and for these we have to content ourselves with the bound
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/2
.

For the case where
g
is semisimple in
G
, as well as the bound for
\dim(C_{V}(g))
, we need to bound the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
on
V\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}K
; we will be satisfied with an upper bound of approximately
n/4
. In order to achieve this, we exploit the action of
N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)
on
\langle g\rangle
(see
Lemma

7.12
(v)) in conjunction with the basic
Lemma

7.1
. When
g
is not semisimple in
G
, we apply the Green correspondence (
Lemma

7.4
), using the fact that
\langle g\rangle
contains a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
L
.

The plan described above will be accomplished in
Lemmas

9.6
and
9.8
. In the cases where
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r)
or
\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
, the approach does not work; however, much is known about the representation theory of these groups, and we are able to amend the methods and argue somewhat more directly (
Lemma

9.10
).

9.2.
Some preliminary lemmas

We begin with some preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
M\leq G:=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, let
g\in G
be semisimple and assume that the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
occur with multiplicity at least
B
. Assume
c,C
satisfy (
9.1
) or (
9.2
), below, and assume
|M|\leq q^{C}
,
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq cn
, and
cn+B\leq n
. Then
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

(9.1)

\displaystyle c(B+1)\leq 2\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}(B-3)\geq B(C+2)

(9.2)

\displaystyle c(B+1)\geq 2\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}\left(B-3c^{2}B-3-3c^{2}+6c\right)\geq B(C+2)

Proof.

Clearly,
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{d}
where
d
is the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
. Assume first (
9.1
), so by
Lemma

7.6
(with
A=cn
) we have
d\leq n^{2}/B
. Therefore, by
Lemma

7.9
we get

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{C+3n^{2}/B}\leq q^{n^{2}-2}<|G|

and the result follows. Case (
9.2
) is identical, using the relevant maximum in
Lemma

7.6
.
∎

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
M\leq G:=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
, let
g\in G
be semisimple and assume that the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
occur with multiplicity at least
B
. Assume
c,C
satisfy (
9.3
) or (
9.4
), below, and assume
|M|\leq q^{C}
,
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq cn
, and
cn+B\leq n
. Then
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

(9.3)

\displaystyle c(B+1)\leq 2\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}(B-3)\geq B(2C+3B+5)

(9.4)

\displaystyle c(B+1)\geq 2\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}(B-3c^{2}B-3-3c^{2}+6c)\geq B(2C+3B+5)

Proof.

Put
q_{0}=q^{1/2}
. By
Lemma

7.11
(ii) we have
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q_{0}^{d+E}
where
d
is the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
and
E
is the number of distinct irreducible factors of the characteristic polynomial. Clearly
E
is at most the number of distinct eigenvalues of
g
. Assume first (
9.3
), so by
Lemma

7.5
(with
A=cn
) we have
d+E\leq B+1+n^{2}/B
. Therefore, by
Lemma

7.9
we get

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q_{0}^{2C+3B+3+3n^{2}/B}\leq q_{0}^{n^{2}-2}<|G|.

Case (
9.4
) is identical, using the relevant maximum in
Lemma

7.6
.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
M\leq G:=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, let
g\in G
be semisimple and assume that the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
occur with multiplicity at least
B
. Assume
c,C
satisfy (
9.5
) or (
9.6
), below, and assume
|M|\leq q^{C}
,
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq cn
, and
cn+B\leq n
. Then
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

(9.5)

\displaystyle cn(B+1)+B\leq 2n\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}(B-3)\geq B(-n+2C+3B+2)

(9.6)

\displaystyle cn(B+1)+B\geq 2n\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}(B-3c^{2}B-3-3c^{2}+6c)\geq nB\left(3c-1\right)+B(2C+3B+2)

Proof.

Let
a
be the dimension of the
1
-eigenspace of
g
, let
d
be the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
and let
E_{2}
be the number of distinct irreducible factors of degree at least
2
of the characteristic polynomial of
g
. By
Lemmas

7.10
and
7.11
we have
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q^{d/2+a/2+E_{2}}
. Next, note that
E_{2}
is at most half the number of nontrivial distinct eigenvalues of
g
. Assume first (
9.5
), so by
Lemma

7.7
(with
A=cn
) we have
d+a+2E_{2}\leq B+n^{2}/B
. Therefore, by
Lemma

7.9
we get

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{C+3n^{2}/(2B)+3B/2}\leq q^{n^{2}/2+n/2-1}<|G|.

Case (
9.6
) is identical, using the relevant maximum in
Lemma

7.6
.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
M\leq G:=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
, let
g\in G
be semisimple and assume that the dimensions of the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
occur with multiplicity at least
B
. Assume
c,C
satisfy (
9.7
) or (
9.8
), below, and assume
|M|\leq q^{C}
,
\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq cn
, and
cn+B\leq n
. Then
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

(9.7)

\displaystyle cn(B+1)-B\leq 2n\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}(B-3)\geq B(n+2C+3B+16)

(9.8)

\displaystyle cn(B+1)-B\geq 2n\text{ \, and \, }n^{2}(B-3c^{2}B-3-3c^{2}+6c)\geq nB\left(1-3c\right)+B(2C+3B+16)

Proof.

Let
a,d,E_{2}
be as in the proof of
Lemma

9.3
; again,
E_{2}
is at most half the number of nontrivial distinct eigenvalues of
g
. By
Lemmas

7.10
and
7.11
we have
|C_{G}(g)|\leq q^{d/2-a/2+E_{2}+2}
. Assume first (
9.7
), so by
Lemma

7.7
(with
A=cn
) we have
d-a+2E_{2}\leq B+n^{2}/B
. Therefore, by
Lemma

7.9
we get

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{C+3n^{2}/(2B)+3B/2+6}\leq q^{n^{2}/2-n/2-2}<|G|.

Case (
9.8
) is identical, using the relevant maximum in
Lemma

7.6
.
∎

9.3.
The proof

We now fix some notation. Recall that
G
is a simple classical group with natural module
V=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
, where
q
a power of the prime
p
. We set
K=\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. We have that
M\in\mathcal{A}
is in class
\mathcal{S}
, so
M
is almost simple; we denote by
S
the socle of
M
. We will find it convenient, in the proofs, to replace
G
by the quasisimple cover acting faithfully on
V
, namely
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
,
\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
or
\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. We will still denote by
M
the preimage in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, while we will denote by
L\leq\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
a quasisimple cover of
S
with
S=L/Z(L)
.
For our choice of the element
g\in L
, we will keep throughout the following notation:

•

d
denotes the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
.

•

a
denotes the dimension of
C_{V}(g)
.

From time to time, for the reader’s convenience we will recall this notation. In some cases below we will make use of the software GAP, and specifically of the GAP Character Table Library
[
8
]
. When the modular character table of a group
L
is available, then for an element of
g
of
L
of order not divisible by
p
(i.e., an element semisimple in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
), we can compute the dimension of all eigenspaces of
g
on
V\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}K
, which gives the value of
d
. This allows us to accurately estimate
|C_{G}(g)|

and deduce, for a suitable choice of
g
, the inequality
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)<|G:M|^{1/3}
. In most cases the bound
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq|C_{G}(g)|
will be enough; in some cases, for very small
n
, we will use the equality
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=|C_{G}(g)||g^{G}\cap M|/|M|
(
Lemma

2.1
).

We start with the case where
L=A_{m}
and
V
is the fully deleted permutation module for
L
, defined as follows. Let
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
be the usual permutation module, with submodules
A=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}):\sum x_{i}=0\}
and
B=\langle(1,\ldots,1)\rangle
; then
V=A/A\cap B
, of dimension
n=m-1
if
p\nmid m
, and
n=m-2
if
p\mid m
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M\in\{A_{m},S_{m}\}
and
M\leq G
via the fully deleted permutation module.

Proof.

Note that
M
preserves a nondegenerate quadratic or bilinear form on
V
. Since
M\in\mathcal{A}
, it follows that
G
is orthogonal or symplectic over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
; see for example
[
39
, p. 187]
for the precise embeddings. We ignore the cases
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}\cong A_{6}
,
G=\Omega^{-}_{4}(2)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(4)
,
G=\Omega_{5}(2)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(2)
, andqw
G=\Omega^{\pm}_{6}(2)\cong\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(2)
, which have been handled in
Lemma

8.1
. Assume first
n=4
, so by
[
7
]
we have
G=\Omega^{-}_{4}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
and
M=A_{5}
with
q=p\geq 7
. Let
g\in M
be of order
3
, so
|C_{G}(g)|=(q^{2}-1)/2
and by
Lemma

2.1
we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=(q^{2}-1)/6<|G:M|^{1/3}
. Assume now
n=5
, so
G=\Omega_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
and either
A_{6}\leq M\leq S_{6}
with
q=p\geq 5
or
M=A_{7}
with
q=p=7
. If
M=A_{7}
we let
g\in M
be of order
7
, so
g
is regular unipotent and
|C_{G}(g)|=q^{2}
and so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq 2q^{2}/7<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
M\leq S_{6}
then we let
g\in M
be of order
5
, so
g
is regular in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q+1)^{2}
and
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq 2(q+1)^{2}/5<|G:M|^{1/3}
. Assume then
n=6
, so
G=\Omega^{\pm}_{6}(q)\cong e.\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(q)
and
M=e\times A_{7}
with
q=p\geq 11
(here
e\in\{1,2\}
). Let
g\in M
be of order
7
, so
|C_{G}(g)|\leq e(q+1)^{3}/2
; moreover
g^{G}
splits into at most two
M
-classes and so by
Lemma

2.1
we have
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq(q+1)^{3}/7<|G:M|^{1/3}
. The case
n\geq 7
is analogous. Specifically,
let
g\in A_{m}
be an
(m-\delta)
-cycle, where
\delta=1
if
m
is even and
\delta=0
if
m
is odd. In both cases,
g
is regular in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, and
g^{G}
splits into at most two
M
-classes, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq 2|C_{G}(g)|/(m-\delta)
, which is easily seen to be
<|G|^{1/3}/((2,q-1)\cdot m!)^{1/3}\leq|G:M|^{1/3}
. For example, for
n=8
and
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{8}(2)
and
A_{10}\leq M
,
g
acts on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{8}
with irreducible submodules of dimensions
2
and
6
, so
|C_{G}(g)|=(2^{3}+1)(2+1)=27
and

\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq\frac{2|C_{G}(g)|}{m-\delta}=6<\left(\frac{|\mathrm{Sp}_{8}(2)|}{10!}\right)^{1/3}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 9.6
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{S}
with alternating socle
S=A_{m}
.

Proof.

We assume
V
is not the fully deleted permutation module in view of
Lemma

9.5
. If
m\leq 10
then the modular character tables of
L
are available in
[
8
]
, and the result follows as discussed before
Lemma

9.5
. Assume then
m\geq 11
, and let
\ell
and
s
be distinct primes as in
Lemma

7.19
. Without loss of generality, assume
\ell\neq p
. As in
Lemma

7.19
, let
x
and
y
be two elements of order
\ell
of
L
such that
L=\langle x,y\rangle
. Since
L
is irreducible, there exists
g\in\{x,y\}
such that
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/2
. Note that either
\ell\geq 11
and
g^{M}
is the union of at most two
L
-classes, or
\ell=7
and
g^{M}
is an
L
-class. (In order to see this we may assume
L=A_{m}
and
M\leq S_{n}
.) In both cases we have
|g^{L}\cap\langle g\rangle|\geq 5
.
By
Lemma

7.2
, we deduce that
N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)
has all orbits of size
|g^{L}\cap\langle g\rangle|\geq 5
on the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
. We now apply
Lemmas

9.1
,
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
with
c=1/2
,
B=5
,
C=2n+5
to deduce the following:

•

If
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 24
, then (
9.2
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
and
n\geq 47
, then (
9.4
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 60
, then (
9.6
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 45
, then (
9.8
) is satisfied.

If
n
satisfies the above inequalities, then by
Lemmas

9.1
,
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
, as desired. Assume then that
n
is smaller than the above values. Then, by
[
32
]
, we see that
m\leq 14
. If
m\leq 13
, then the modular character tables of
L
are available in
[
8
]
and the result follows. If
m=14
, then the modular character tables are available unless
L=2.A_{14}
. By
[
32
]
, we see that in this case the only option is
n=32
,
p=7
and
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{32}(q)
. By
Lemma

7.17
we have that
L=A_{14}
is generated by two conjugates of an element
g
of order
11
. We deduce that either
a\leq 12
and each nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
2
, or
a\leq 2
and each nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
3
; it follows that
d\leq 184
, which implies
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.
∎

Next we address the case where
S
is not alternating. It is convenient to isolate the alternating and symmetric square for
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(q)
.

Lemma 9.7
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(q)
and
V
is the alternating or symmetric square of the natural module for
S
.

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{m}(q)
be of order
(q^{m}-1)/(q-1)
. We claim that
g
has distinct eigenvalues on
V
. In order to check this, it is enough to assume that
V
is the symmetric square. If
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
g
on the natural module, then the eigenvalues on
V
are
\lambda^{q^{i}+q^{j}}
for
i=j
or
\{i,j\}\in\binom{[n]}{2}
. It is straightforward to see that
|g|
does not divide
q^{i}+q^{j}-q^{k}-q^{\ell}
for
\{i,j\}\neq\{k,\ell\}
, which is equivalent to saying that the eigenvalues are pairwise distinct. Note that for
m=2
we have
M\in\mathcal{C}_{8}
in view of the isomorphism
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)\cong\Omega_{3}(q)
. For
m\geq 3
, using
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q^{n}-1)/(q-1)
we see that
|\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)|
and the proof is concluded.
∎

Next we address the general case. If
S
is of Lie type, we denote by
\mathbb{F}_{r}
the field of definition. We first exclude the cases
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r),\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
or
|M|\geq q^{2n+4}
, which will be handled in subsequent lemmas.

Lemma 9.8
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{S}
with
|M|\leq q^{2n+4}
, and
S
is sporadic or of Lie type with
S\neq\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r),\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
.

Proof.

We may assume
S\neq\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}\cong A_{6},S\neq\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(2)\cong A_{8}
in view of
Lemma

9.6
.
As discussed before
Lemma

9.5
, we replace
G
by its quasisimple cover acting faithfully on
V
; we have
L\trianglelefteq M\leq G
where
L
is quasisimple with
L/Z(L)\cong S
. Since we have replaced
M
by its preimage, the bound in the hypothesis now gives
|M|\leq q^{2n+5}
.

Next we partition the socles
S
into two collections,
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. See the paragraph after Collection
\mathcal{H}_{2}
for comments on this choice.

Collection
\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

The socles
S
in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
are those satisfying either (1) or (2) below.

(1)

S\neq\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r),\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r),E_{6}^{\pm}(r)
,
E_{7}(r)
, and the Schur multiplier of
S
is trivial.

(2)

Either
S=E^{\pm}_{6}(r)
, or
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
with
m
odd, or the Schur multiplier of
S
is not trivial and
S
is one of the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}^{\varepsilon}_{m}(r)\text{ with $m\geq 5$ and $(\varepsilon,m)\neq(-,6)$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)\text{ with $m\geq 3$ and $(m,r)\neq(3,2)$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega^{-}_{2m}(r)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)\text{ with $m\geq 4$}

In (2), we furthermore
exclude
the following cases:

\diamond

S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m\equiv 2\pmod{4}
and
p
a ppd of
(r^{2})^{m/2}-1
;
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
p
a ppd of
(r^{2})^{m-2}-1
;
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)
with
p
a ppd of
r^{2(m-1)}-1
;
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
with
m
even and
p
a ppd of
r^{m}-1
.

Collection
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

The socles
S
in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
are those not belonging to
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Specifically:

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}_{4}^{\pm}(r)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSU}_{6}(r)\text{ with $(6,r+1)\neq 1$}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)\text{ with $r$ odd}

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{7}(r)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)\text{ with $m$ even}

\displaystyle E_{7}(r)

the groups in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
(2)(
\diamond
) having nontrivial Schur multiplier

\displaystyle G_{2}(3),G_{2}(4),\,^{2}\!B_{2}(8),F_{4}(2),\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2)

\displaystyle M_{12},M_{22},J_{2},J_{3},HS,Suz,McL,Ru,O^{\prime}N,Co_{1},Fi_{22},Fi^{\prime}_{24},B.

At this point we have defined
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Next, for
S
in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
we will find an element
g\in L
satisfying
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/3
, while for
S
in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
we will find an element
g\in L
satisfying
a\leq n/2
. In order to orient the reader, before giving the details let us briefly motivate the choice of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, recalling also
Section

9.1
for related remarks. For the groups in
\mathcal{H}_{1}(1)
we will apply
[
27
]
. The exclusion of the groups in
\mathcal{H}_{1}(1)
(e.g.,
\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r)
) is due to the fact that in these cases the element
g
given in
[
27
]
does not work well when applying
Lemma

7.1
. For the groups in
\mathcal{H}_{1}(2)
we will apply
Lemma

7.15
. The exclusion of the groups in
\mathcal{H}_{1}(2)(\diamond)
guarantees that if our chosen element
g
is not semisimple in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, then it is regular in
L
(see
Lemma

7.12
(iii)), and so we can effectively apply
Lemma

7.4
. For the groups in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
we will use either
[
27
]
or
Lemma

7.16
. Now we address this in detail.

Assume first
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Suppose
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
(1);
then
L=S
. If
S
is sporadic, let
g=x_{1}
be as in
Table

7.2
. If
S
is of Lie type and
S\neq\mathrm{PSL}^{\varepsilon}_{m}(r)
, let
g=x_{1}
be as in
Table

7.1
. If
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\varepsilon}_{m}(r)
, set
u=1
if
\varepsilon=+
and
u=2
if
\varepsilon=-
, and let
g
be an element of order
\Phi^{*}_{um}(r)
if
m
is odd, and of order
\Phi^{*}_{u(m-1)}(r)
if
m
is even. Then, in all cases,
[
27
, Theorem 1.1, Props 3.4–3.13, Prop 4.5]
shows that
L
is generated by three conjugates of
g
whose product is
1
. It follows from
Lemma

7.3
that
a\leq n/3
, as desired.

Assume next
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
(2). Without changing notation, we let
x_{1},x_{2}\in L
be lifts of the corresponding elements of
S
in
Table

7.1
, having the same orders (see
Lemma

7.12
). Then, by
Lemma

7.15
we deduce that
L
is invariably generated by
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Moreover, in all cases
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are regular semisimple (see
Lemma

7.12
(iii))
and so by an extension of a theorem of Gow
[
24
]
(see
[
29
, Lemma 5.1]
), we can write
x_{2}=x_{1}^{y}x_{1}^{z}
for
y,z\in L
. Then
L=\langle x_{1}^{y},x_{1}^{z},x_{2}^{-1}\rangle
, and by
Lemma

7.3
, there exists
g\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
such that
a\leq n/3
, as desired.

Assume now
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. If
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m>4
, then let
g\in L
be an element of order
\Phi^{*}_{2m}(r)
if
m
is odd, and of order
\Phi^{*}_{2(m-1)}(r)
if
m
is even. In the other cases, let
g=x_{1}\in L
be a lift of the corresponding element in
Table

7.1
or
Table

7.2
, having the same order, unless
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(q)
, in which case we let
g=x_{2}\in L
be a lift of the corresponding element in
Table

7.1
. If
S\neq Co_{1}
is sporadic, or
S\neq E_{7}(r)
is exceptional, or
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(r)
, or
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)
with
m>4
, or
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)
, then by
[
27
, Theorem 1.1, Propositions 3.4–3.13]
we deduce that
L
is generated by two conjugates of
g
. If
S=Co_{1}
, the same holds by
[
25
, Proposition 6.2]
.
If
S
is
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}^{\pm}(r)
, or
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(r)
with
m
even and
(m,r)\neq(4,2)
, or
E_{7}(r)
, the same holds from
Lemma

7.16
. In conclusion, if
S\neq\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
then
L
is generated by two conjugates of
g
, and since
L
acts irreducibly on
V
we deduce
a\leq n/2
, as claimed.

In the case
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
, the modular character tables are available on GAP Character Table Library
[
8
]
, so we exclude this case from now on.

At this point, for
S
in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
we have
a\leq n/3
, and for
S
in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
we have
a\leq n/2
. Next we divide into three cases.

Case 1
:
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
g
is semisimple in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
. Then by
Lemma

7.12
(v) we have that
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|
acts semiregularly on
\langle g\rangle\smallsetminus\{1\}
. In particular, by
Lemma

7.1
we deduce that
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|
acts semiregularly on the nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
. Note that if
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{6}(r)
then
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{1}(1)
and
|g|=\Phi^{*}_{10}(r)
(since
\mathrm{PSU}_{6}(r)
does not belong to
\mathcal{H}_{1}(2)
). Then, by inspection of
Tables

7.1
and
7.2
we see that
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|\geq 4
. We then apply
Lemmas

9.1
,
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
with
c=1/3
,
B=4
and
C=2n+5
to deduce the following:

•

If
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 11
, then (
9.1
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
and
n\geq 22
, then (
9.3
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 18
, then (
9.5
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 25
, then (
9.7
) is satisfied.

If
n
satisfies the above inequalities, then by
Lemmas

9.1
,
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
, as desired. Assume then that
n
is smaller than the above values.
If
S
is in
\text{Lie}(p^{\prime})
or
S
is sporadic, then by
[
32
]
we see that all the possibilities for
S
are included in
[
8
]
and the result follows.

Assume then
S
is in
\text{Lie}(p)
. Suppose
V
is
p
-restricted for
L
. Then the possibilities are listed in
[
61
]
. Noting that for
n\geq 22

we have
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
, so
V\downarrow L
is self-dual,
we have the following possibilities:

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{5}(r)\text{ with }n=10,15,23,24

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{6}(r)\text{ with }n=15,20,21

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{7}(r)\text{ with }n=21

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)\text{ with }n=16\text{ and $r$ even}

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(r)\text{ with }n=8,13,14,21

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSp}_{8}(r)\text{ with }n=16\text{ and $r$ even}

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{9}(r)\text{ with }n=16

\displaystyle S=\,^{2}B_{2}(r)\text{ with }n=4,16

\displaystyle S=G_{2}(r)\text{ with }n=6,7,14

\displaystyle S=\,^{2}\!G_{2}(r)\text{ with }n=7

\displaystyle S=\,^{3}D_{4}(r)\text{ with }n=8

\displaystyle S=\,\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\pm}_{10}(r)\text{ with }n=16

We consider each case in turn. Assume first
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\varepsilon}_{5}(r)
with
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
, so
q\geq r
. For
n=10
or
15
, up to quasi-equivalence
V
is the alternating square or the symmetric square of the natural 5-dimensional module, so by
Lemma

9.7
we may assume
\varepsilon=-
. For
n=10
, we choose
g
of order
(r^{5}+1)/(r+1)
, and we calculate directly that
g
has distinct eigenvalues on
V
. Assume now
n=15
, so by what just proved
g
is regular on a
10
-dimensional subspace. Since
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|=5
, it follows that there are at most
5
eigenspaces of dimension
2
, so

d\leq 25
, which implies
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. (Here we used
Lemma

7.12
(v) and
Lemma

7.1
. In the course of the proof, we will often apply these results with no explicit mention.) Assume now
n=23,24
, so we have
|M|<q^{n+3}
. Choosing the original element
g
and replacing
C=2n+5
by
C=n+3
in the above calculation we see that the result follows. (In many cases, below, we will abuse notation and write that
V
is a certain module, when we really mean that
V
is quasi-equivalent to that module. This will happen for example in the next paragraph.)

Assume next
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\varepsilon}_{6}(r)
, so
q\geq r
. In the cases
n=15,21
, by
Lemma

9.7
we may assume
\varepsilon=-
. We choose
g
of order
(r^{5}+1)/(r+1)
. If
n=15
, it follows readily by what proved in the previous paragraph for
\mathrm{PSU}_{5}(r)
(by restricting to a
10
-dimensional submodule) that
g
has distinct eigenvalues. If
n=21
, then it similarly follows that
g
is regular on an
11
-dimensional subspace, so
g
has at most
5
eigenspaces of dimension
2
and
d\leq 31
, which easily gives
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. For
n=20
,
V
is the third alternating power. We have
a\leq\binom{6}{2}/3=5
; since
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|\geq 5
, each nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
20/5=4
and this is sufficient.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\varepsilon}_{7}(r)
with
n=21
. Since
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|=7
, each eigenspace for
g
has dimension at most
3
and
d\leq 63
and this gives the result.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)
with
n=16
. Then
a\leq 4
and so each eigenspace has dimension at most
4
, so
d=\dim(C_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)}(g))\leq 64
and this is sufficient.

In the case
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{9}(r)
or
\mathrm{PSp}_{8}(r)
with
n=16
, or
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(r)
with
n=8
, then
V
is the spin module, which is handled in
Lemma

9.9
, below.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(r)
with
n>8
. Note that
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|=6
. In particular, since
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/3
, for
n=13
we deduce by
Lemma

7.1
that
a=1
and the nontrivial eigenspaces have dimension at most
2
, which gives
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. By the same argument, for
n=14
(resp.
n=21
) we see that all
eigenspaces of
g
have dimension at most
2
(resp. at most
3
), and the result follows.

Assume now
S=\,^{2}B_{2}(r)
. For
n=4
we have
r=q>2
and
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
and
S=M
. An element
g
of
M
of order
q+\sqrt{2q}+1
acts irreducibly on
V
, and moreover
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=|C_{G}(g)|/|C_{M}(g)|=q-\sqrt{2q}+1<|G:M|^{1/3}
. For
n=16
, we have that
V
, as an
\mathbb{F}_{q}L
-module, is equivalent to a module that is not
p
-restricted (see
[
33
, Theorem, p. 207]
), and so does not give rise to a maximal subgroup, that is,
M\not\in\mathcal{A}
.

Next let
S=\,^{2}G_{2}(r)
. Then
n=7
, but in this case
S<G_{2}(r)<G
and
M\not\in\mathcal{A}(G)
.

Assume now
S=G_{2}(r)
. If
n=6
then
q=r
is even,
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(q)
and
S=M
. An element
g\in M
of order
(q^{3}+1)/(q+1)
acts irreducibly on
V
, and
g^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
, so
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)=|C_{G}(g)|/|C_{M}(g)|=q+1<|G:M|^{1/3}
. The case
n=7
is entirely analogous; in this case
G=\Omega_{7}(q)
and
g
acts irreducibly on a hyperplane. Assume finally
n=14
; since
|N_{G}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{G}(g)|=6
and
a\leq n/3
, we deduce by
Lemma

7.1
that all eigenspaces of
g
have dimension at most
2
, which gives the result.

Assume now
S=\,^{3}D_{4}(r)
with
n=8
, so
q=r^{3}
and
G=\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
. Then
g
is regular, with
|C_{G}(g)|\leq(q^{2}+1)^{2}
and
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Assume finally
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{10}(r)
with
n=16
, so
V
is the spin module. The case
\varepsilon=+
is covered in

Lemma

9.9
, below, and the case
\varepsilon=-
can be handled in the same way, by taking
g
of order
q^{4}+1
.
This completes the argument when
V\downarrow L
is
p
-restricted.

If
V
is not
p
-restricted, then by Steinberg’s twisted tensor product theorem we have
n\geq h^{2}
where
h
is the smallest degree of a faithful representation of
L
. Since
h\geq 4
and
n\leq 24
, it must be
h=4
and
n=16
. By
[
70
]
, there is no case with
M\in\mathcal{A}(G)
(note that
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, so if
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)
then
r
is even, and also
S\neq\mathrm{PSL}_{4}^{\pm}(r)
).

Case 2
:
S
is in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
g
is semisimple in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
. By our choice of the element
g
, we have
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|\geq 4
(recall that we chose
g=x_{2}
for
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(r)
). In particular, the overall argument of Case 1 goes through to give the following, except that we apply
Lemmas

9.1
,
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
with
c=1/2
(instead of
c=1/3
),
B=4
and
C=2n+5
.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 36
, then (
9.2
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
and
n\geq 71
, then (
9.4
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 77
, then (
9.6
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 66
, then (
9.8
) is satisfied.

If
n
satisfies the above inequalities, then by
Lemmas

9.1
,
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
, as desired. Assume then
n
is smaller than above values. Suppose
S
is in
\text{Lie}(p^{\prime})
or
S
is sporadic. By
[
32
]
, either the modular character tables of
S
are available in
[
8
]
, in which case the result follows, or we are in one of the following cases:

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(4)\text{ with }n=51,52

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(7)\text{ with }n=24,25

\displaystyle S=F_{4}(2)\text{ with }n=52

\displaystyle S=Co_{1}\text{ with }n=24.

Assume first
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(4)
. Then
|M|<3^{25}<q^{n/2}
, so we may replace
C=2n+5
by
C=n/2
in the above calculation and this gives the conclusion.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(7)
with
n=24,25
; by
[
32
]
we have
p\neq 7
and
G=\mathrm{SL}^{\pm}_{24}(q)
. Let
g\in S
be of order
7
and with centralizer of order
4116
; there is only one such
S
-class and so
|g^{S}\cap\langle g\rangle|=6
. We check with GAP that
S
is generated by two conjugates of
g
. If
n=24
, then
a=\dim(C_{V}(x))\leq n/2=12
and by
Lemmas

7.1
and
7.6
we have that
d=\dim(C_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)}(g))\leq 168
, from which we see that
|C_{G}(x)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
n=25
, then in fact we must have
a\leq 7
, so
d\leq 103
and this is sufficient.

Assume now
S=F_{4}(2)
with
n=52
; we have
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. We have
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{L}(g)|=12
and
a\leq n/2=26
, so in fact
a\leq 16
and by
Lemma

7.6
we see that
d\leq 364
, which gives
|C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G:M|
.

Assume finally
S=Co_{1}
with
n=24
. Since
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|=11
and
a\leq n/2=12
, it follows that all eigenspaces of
g
have dimension at most
2
, and this gives
|C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G:M|
.

Suppose now
S
is in
\text{Lie}(p)
. The cases
S=G_{2}(3),G_{2}(4),^{2}\!B_{2}(8),\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2)
appear in
[
8
]
and the conclusion follows. Assume we are not in these cases. Suppose
V
is
p
-restricted as
S
-module. Then the possibilities are listed in
[
61
]
; these are as follows:

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r)\text{ with }n\leq 76

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSU}_{6}(r)\text{ with }n=15,20,21,34,35,50,56,70

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)\text{ with }n\leq 76

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{7}(r)\text{ with }n=8,21,26,27,35,40,48,63,64

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(r)\text{ with }n=26,28,35,48,56

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{12}(r)\text{ with }n=32,64,66,76

\displaystyle S=F_{4}(2)\text{ with }n=26.

(Note that the case
S=E_{7}(r)
with
n=56
is excluded in view of the assumption
|M|\leq q^{2n+4}
.)
Assume first
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{6}(r)
, so
q\geq r
; we have
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{L}(g)|=5
. If
n\geq 34
then
|M|<q^{n+5}
; in the above calculation we may replace
C=2n+5
by
n+5
and
B=4
by
5
and we see that
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. The cases
n=15,20,21
were addressed already in Case 1.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{7}(r)
, so
q=r
and
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{L}(g)|=6
. If
n\geq 26
then
|M|<q^{n-2}
; the usual calculation with
c=1/2
,
B=6
and
C=n-2
gives the result. If
n=8
then
G=\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
and
S
is conjugate in
\mathrm{Aut}(G/Z(G))
to a reducible subgroup, so we can exclude this case. If
n=21
then
V
is the alternating square; we have
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))=3
, and so every eigenspace has dimension at most
3
, which implies
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{12}(r)
, so
q=r
. We have
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{L}(g)|=6
; if
n\geq 64
we have
|M|<q^{n+4}
and the result follows with the usual calculation. If
n=32
then
V
is the spin module. The restriction of
V
to
\text{Spin}^{+}_{8}(q)
is the sum of four half-spins (two of each type). Letting
g
be an element of order
q^{4}-1
, it follows that each eigenspace of
g
on
V
has dimension at most
4
, which is sufficient.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(r)
, so
q=r
. We have
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{L}(g)|=6
. If
n\geq 48
then
|M|<q^{n}
; the usual calculation with
B=6
and
C=n
gives the result. The remaining cases are
n=26,28,35
. If
n=26
then
p=2
and
V
is a composition factor of the alternating square. We have
a=2
, so each nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
4
and this is sufficient. If
n=28
, then
V
is the alternating square, so
a=4
and each nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
4
, and the result follows. If
n=35
then
V
is a composition factor of the symmetric square. We have
a=5
, so all eigenspaces have dimension at most
5
, which gives the result.

Assume now
S=E_{7}(r)
and
n=56
. In this case
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{L}(g)|=14
. Replacing
B=4
by
B=14
in the usual calculation gives the result.

If
S=F_{4}(2)
, then
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)|/|C_{L}(g)|=12
, so in fact all eigenspaces have dimension at most
2
and the result follows.

Assume then
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r)
, so
q\geq r
. If
n\geq 32
, note first that by
[
61
]
, either
n\geq 44
or
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
or
\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
. In any case we have
|M|<q^{n/2+2}
,
and the conclusion follows by the usual calculation with
B=4
and
C=n/2+2
. The remaining cases are
n=6,10,14,15,16,19,20
. We address the case
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{4}^{-}(r)=\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(r)
; the case
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(r)
is nearly identical. If
n=6
then
M
is in class
\mathcal{C}_{8}
in view of
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(r)\cong\mathrm{P}\Omega^{-}_{6}(r)
. If
n=10
then
V
is the symmetric square and
G=\mathrm{SU}_{10}(q^{1/2})
. Then
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))=2
, so every nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
2
, so
d=\dim(C_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)}(g))\leq 20
and this implies
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
n=14
then
p=2
and
V
is a composition factor of the adjoint module. We have
a=2
, so every nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
12/4=3
and this gives the result. If
n=15
then
p\neq 2
and
V
is the adjoint module; we have
a=3
and every nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
3
and we are done. If
n=16
then
p=3
and
V\cong S^{3}(W)/W
where
W
is the natural module. One calculates that
a\leq 4
, so
d\leq 64
(obtained when
a=0
) and the result follows. If
n=19
then
a\leq 9
; it follows that in fact
a\leq 7
and all other eigenspaces have dimension at most
3
, so
d\leq 85
and this is sufficient. If
n=20
then it must be
a\leq 8
and
d\leq 100
, so
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
also in this case.

Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)
, so
q=r
. If
n\geq 35
then
|M|<q^{n/3+1}
; we conclude with the usual calculation with
B=4
and
C=n/3+2
. The remaining cases are
n=10,12,13,14,16,20,24,25,30
. For
i=4,5
, let
W_{i}
be the natural
i
dimensional module for
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)
(recall
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)\cong\Omega_{5}(r)
). If
n=10
then
V=S^{2}(W_{4})
. We have
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))=2
and so each eigenspace has dimension at most
2
, which gives
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
n=12
then
p=5
and the highest dominant weight is
(1,1)
; it follows that
V
is a composition factor of
U:=W_{4}\otimes W_{5}
. Let
g\in L
be an element of order
13
; then
g
has eigenvalues
x,x^{5},x^{-1},x^{-5}
on
W_{4}
and eigenvalues
x^{3},x^{2},x^{-3},x^{-2}
on
W_{5}
.
It follows from an easy calculation that
C_{U}(g)=0
and each nontrivial eigenspace on
U
has dimension at most
2
.
This implies that
d\leq 24
, which gives
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
n=13
(resp.
n=14
) then
p=5
(resp.
p\neq 5
) and
V
is a composition factor of
S^{2}(W_{5})
. We have
a=1
(resp.
a=2
) and each nontrivial eigenspace has dimension at most
12/4=3
, which is sufficient. If
n=16
then the highest dominant weight is
(1,1)
; as in the case
n=12
we have that
V
is a composition factor of
U:=W_{4}\otimes W_{5}
. We easily calculate that
C_{U}(g)=0
, and in particular each nontrivial eigenspace on
U
has dimension at most
4
; it follows that
d\leq 64
, which gives the result. If
n=20
then as in the
\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r)
-case we see that
d\leq 100
and the result follows. If
n=25
then

a\leq 12
, so in fact
a\leq 9
and so
d\leq 145
and we conclude. If
n=24
then
p=7
and the highest dominant weight is
(1,2)
(where the second weight corresponds to the short root); in particular
W
is a composition factor of
U:=W_{4}\otimes A
where
A
is a
14
-dimensional composition factor of
S^{2}(W_{5})
. Let
g
be an element of order
25
. We calculate that
\dim(C_{A}(g))=2
and each nontrivial eigenspace has dimension
1
; it follows immediately that each eigenspace on
U
has dimension at most
4
, from which
d\leq 96
and
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Assume finally that
V
is not
p
-restricted as
S
-module. By
[
70
]
, the possibilites for the embedding
S<G/Z(G)
are as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(q^{2})<\mathrm{PSL}_{m^{2}}(q),\,m=4,5\\
\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(q)<\mathrm{PSU}_{m^{2}}(q^{1/2}),\,4\leq m\leq 8\\
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(q^{3/2})<\mathrm{PSU}_{64}(q^{1/2})\\
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q^{3})<\mathrm{PSp}_{64}(q),\,q\hbox{ odd}\\
\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q^{2})<\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{4m^{2}}(q),\,m=2,3,4,\,\varepsilon=(-1)^{m}\\
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{2m}(q^{2})<\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\delta}_{4m^{2}}(q),\,m=3,4\\
\end{array}

If
S\neq\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r),\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)
then
n\geq 25
and
V=W\otimes W^{(q)}
where
W
is the natural module for
S
. Since
g
has distinct eigenvalues on
W
, it follows that each eigenspace of
g
on
V
has dimension at most
\dim(W)=n^{1/2}
, which is sufficient. Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{4}(r)
.
If
n=16
, we see that
C_{V}(g)=0
and we see that each eigenspace has dimension at most
3
(in order to prove this, it is enough to check that at least one eigenspace has dimension at most
3
, since the eigenspaces are permuted in orbits of size
4
). This gives the result. If
n=64
then we see similarly that each eigenspace of
g
on
W\otimes W^{(q)}
has dimension at most
3
, from which each eigenspace on
V=W\otimes W^{(q)}\otimes W^{(q^{2})}
has dimension at most
12
and this is enough. The case
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(r)
can be handled by the same argument.

Case 3
:
g
is not semisimple in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
. We want to apply
Lemma

7.4
. Note that in all cases if
S
is of Lie type then
g
is regular in
S
. (The exclusion of the groups in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
(2)(
\diamond
) is crucial for this. These groups are included in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, in which case the element
g=x_{1}
from
Table

7.1
is chosen, and this element is regular by
Lemma

7.12
(iii).)
Then, by
Lemma

7.12
(vii) we have that
\langle g\rangle
contains a Sylow
p
-subgroup
P
of
L
with
|P|\geq 5
. Moreover, for every
1\neq P_{0}\leq P
we have
N_{L}(P_{0})=N_{L}(P)=N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)
by
Lemma

7.12
(v). Let now
K=C_{L}(g)
.
Lemma

7.12
(iv) implies that
K
is abelian and
K\trianglelefteq N_{L}(P)
; so assumption (
\star
) in
Lemma

7.4
is satisfied. Therefore, by
Lemma

7.4
we have
V\downarrow P=V_{0}\oplus U
where all Jordan blocks on
U
have size
|P|
, and
V_{0}
is the sum of at most
|N_{L}(P):K|
Jordan blocks of the same size, say
t
. Note that
|N_{L}(P):K|=|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle):C_{L}(g)|
appears in
Table

7.1
or
Table

7.2
under
n_{i}
. Now, for convenience, we replace
\langle g\rangle
by a power of it generating
P
, so now
g
is unipotent. (This makes it more convenient to compute centralizers.) As always, we have
d=\dim(C_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)}(g))
and
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))
.

Assume first that
t\geq 4
, so all Jordan blocks of
g
have size at least
\mathrm{min}\{|P|,t\}\geq 4
. Then by
Lemma

7.8
with
C=0
and
B=4
we deduce
d\leq n^{2}/4
. Moreover, denoting by
R
the number of Jordan blocks of
g
, we have
R\leq n/4
. Next, we can upper bound
|C_{G}(g)|
as in
Lemmas

7.10
and
7.11
.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 11
, we have
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{d}
and so

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{2n+5+3n^{2}/4}\leq q^{n^{2}-2}<|G|.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
and
n\geq 22
, then setting
q_{0}=q^{1/2}
, we have
|C_{G}(g)|<q_{0}^{n/4+n^{2}/4}
and so

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q_{0}^{4n+10+3n/4+3n^{2}/4}\leq q_{0}^{n^{2}-2}<|G|.

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 24
, then letting
d^{\prime}
be the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(K)
, we have
d^{\prime}\leq d/2+R/2
and
R\leq n/4
, so
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{d^{\prime}+R}\leq q^{n^{2}/8+3n/8}
and

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{2n+5+9n/8+3n^{2}/8}\leq q^{n^{2}/2+n/2-1}<|G|.

•

If
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 28
, then letting
d^{\prime}
be the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{SO}_{n}(K)
, we have
d^{\prime}\leq d/2
and
R\leq n/4
, so
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{d^{\prime}+R}\leq q^{n^{2}/8+n/4}
and

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{2n+5+3n/4+3n^{2}/8}\leq q^{n^{2}/2-n/2-2}<|G|.

By
[
32
]
, we see that for
n\leq 27
, either the modular character tables of
S
and its covers are available in
[
8
]
, or
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(7)
(with
n=24,25
) or
S=Co_{1}
(with
n=24
). If
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(7)
then
G=\mathrm{SL}^{\pm}_{n}(q)
, in which case we already have the result for
n\geq 22
. If
S=Co_{1}
, we have
p=q=23
and
M=2.Co_{1}
and
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. In this case it is convenient to change our choice of the element
g
. The restriction of
V
to
H=Co_{2}
has composition factors of degree
23
and
1
. Now the modular character table of
Co_{2}
is available in
[
8
]
; we see that an element
g
of order
11
has
10
nontrivial eigenspaces of dimension
2
and fixed space of dimension
4
. Therefore
d=56
and
d^{\prime}=d/2-4/2=26
, where
d^{\prime}
is the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{SO}_{n}(K)
, which implies
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
.

Assume then
t\leq 3
, so
\dim(V_{0})\leq t|N_{L}(A):S|\leq 3n_{i}=:3f
(here as usual
n_{i}=|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|
).
Applying
Lemma

7.8
with
C=3f
and
B=5
, we deduce

d\leq\frac{n^{2}}{5}+\frac{36f^{2}}{5}.

Assume first
f\leq n/10
, so
d\leq 0.272n^{2}
. Moreover, letting
R
denote the number of Jordan blocks of
g
, we have
R\leq 3f+(n-3f)/5\leq 11n/25
. Since
f\geq 4
, we deduce that
n\geq 10f\geq 40
. Using
|M|\leq q^{2n+5}
and
Lemmas

7.10
and
7.11
we calculate similarly to above that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
, unless
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
with
40\leq n\leq 43
.
In these exceptional cases, since
f\leq n/10
we have
f=4
. We have
\dim(V_{0})\leq 12
and all Jordan blocks on
U
have odd size
|g|\geq 5
. If the Jordan block on
V_{0}
have size at least
2
, it is easy to calculate that
d\leq n^{2}/5+96
. Using
R\leq 11n/25
, we see that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
for
n\geq 39
. The remaining case is where
g
is trivial on
V_{0}
. Then by
[
56
, Theorem 3.1]
we have
d^{\prime}=d/2-R/2
, where
d^{\prime}
is the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{SO}_{n}(K)
, so
d^{\prime}+R=d/2+R/2
. Using as above
R\leq 22n/50
and
d\leq 0.272n^{2}
, we see that
|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<|G|
for
n\geq 36
, and so this case is done.

Assume now
f>n/10
. Then by
Lemma

7.14
,
S
is one of the following (recall we are excluding
\mathrm{SL}_{4}(2)\cong A_{8}
and
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}\cong A_{6}
and we already handled
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(r)\text{ with }(m,r)=(4,3),(5,2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(r)\text{ with }(m,r)=(4,2),(4,3),(5,2),(6,2),(7,2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(r)\text{ with }(m,r)=(2,3),(2,4),(2,5),(2,7),(3,2),(3,3),(4,2),(4,3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega^{-}_{8}(2),\mathrm{P}\Omega_{7}(3)

{}^{2}B_{2}(8),G_{2}(3),\,^{3}D_{4}(2),F_{4}(2)

\displaystyle\text{a sporadic group not }ON,He,Th,Fi_{23},Fi^{\prime}_{24},B,M.

In all these cases we have
f\leq 22
, and therefore
n<10f\leq 220
, and moreover
p
divides the order of
g
.
For all the groups except for
\mathrm{PSU}_{7}(2),\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(7),\mathrm{PSp}_{8}(3),F_{4}(2),Co_{1},Ly,J_{4}
, the modular character table is available in
[
8
]
, in which case the result follows.

Assume now
S
is one of
\mathrm{PSU}_{7}(2),\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(7),\mathrm{PSp}_{8}(3),F_{4}(2),Co_{1},Ly,J_{4}
. The group
S=Co_{1}
was handled above in the case
t\geq 4
(and we did not use this assumption). In order to handle the other groups, we keep the notation
t
,
V_{0}
and
U
as above. It is sufficient to prove that
t\geq 4
, as this reduces to a case already considered; also we may assume
f>n/10
and in particular
n<220
. If
S=\mathrm{PSU}_{7}(2)
then by
[
32
]
we have
n=42,43
. We have
|g|=\Phi^{*}_{14}(2)=43
, and so
t\geq 4
(since otherwise
\dim(V_{0})\leq 3f\leq 21
; but all Jordan blocks on a complement have size at least
43
). If
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(7)
then by
[
32
]
we have
n=24,25
or
n\geq 126
. Since
f=4
we may assume
n=24,25
. But
|g|=\Phi^{*}_{4}(7)=25
, so as in the previous case we have
t\geq 4
and we are done. Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSp}_{8}(3)
. Then by
[
32
]
we have
n=40,41
. Since
|g|=\Phi^{*}_{8}(3)=41
we deduce
t\geq 4
exactly as above. Assume
S=F_{4}(2)
, so
n=52
and
|g|=13
. We have
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
. If
t\leq 3
then
\dim(V_{0})\leq 3f=36
. But all Jordan blocks on a complement have size
13
, so in fact there are at least two such blocks, and so
\dim(V_{0})\leq 26
. But also
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/2=26
and all Jordan blocks on
V_{0}
have the same size, so
d\leq 2\cdot 15^{2}+11\cdot 2^{2}=494
(attained if
g
has two Jordan blocks of size
13
and the others of size
2
), which implies
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.
If
S=Ly
or
J_{4}
, then since
p
divides
|g|
, by
[
32
]
we see that there are no cases with
n<220
. The proof is finally concluded.
∎

The remaining cases are
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r),\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
or
|M|\geq q^{2n+4}
. We address now the case
|M|\geq q^{2n+4}
; we use the main result of
[
47
]
asserting that the possibilities for
M
are rather restricted.

Lemma 9.9
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{S}
and
|M|\geq q^{2n+4}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the socle of
M
. By
[
47
, Theorem 4.2]
, we deduce that we are in one of the following cases:

(i)

S=A_{m}
and
V
is the fully deleted permutation module with
n=m-1
or
m-2
;

(ii)

S=\mathrm{PSL}_{m}(q)
and
V
is the alternating square of the natural module;

(iii)

S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{7}(q)
,
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{9}(q)
or
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{10}(q)
and
V
is a spin module of dimension
8
,
16
or
16
(here we allow
q
even for
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{7}(q)
and
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{9}(q)
);

(iv)

S=E_{6}(q)
or
E_{7}(q)
and
n=27
or
56
;

(v)

S=M_{24}
or
Co_{1}
and
n=11
or
24
.

We consider each case in turn, noting that (i) has been handled in
Lemma

9.5
and (ii) has been handled in
Lemma

9.7
. As in the previous proof, we replace
G
by its cover acting faithfully on
V
.

(iii) For
n=8
we have
G=\Omega^{+}_{8}(q)
, and
S
is conjugate in
\text{Aut}(G/Z(G))
to the stabilizer of a nonsingular vector, which has been handled already. For
n=16
and
S=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{9}(q)
or
\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{10}(q)
, the restriction of
V
to
\text{Spin}^{+}_{8}(q)
is the sum of the two half-spin modules of dimension
8
, so by letting
g
be an element of order
q^{4}-1
, we have that each eigenspace of
g
has dimension at most
2
, and the result follows.

(iv) For
S=E_{6}(q)
or
E_{7}(q)
, we choose
g
as in the proof of
Lemma

9.8
. If
S=E_{6}(q)
with
n=27
, then
G=\mathrm{SL}_{27}(q)
. We have
|M|<q^{3n}
and, arguing exactly as in the proof of
Lemma

9.8
, we apply
Lemma

9.1
with
c=1/3
,
B=8
, and
C=3n
; we see that (
9.2
) holds and the conclusion follows. If
S=E_{7}(q)
with
n=56
, then
|M|<q^{3n}
,
G
is symplectic or orthogonal and we apply
Lemma

9.3
or
Lemma

9.4
with
c=1/2
,
B=14
, and
C=3n
; we see that (
9.6
) and (
9.8
) are satisfied and the conclusion follows.

(v) If
S=M_{24}
we can use
[
8
]
. If
S=Co_{1}
then
q=2
and
G
is orthogonal. We choose
g
as in
Table

7.2
; we argue as in the proof of
Lemma

9.8
and we
apply
Lemma

9.4
with
c=1/2
,
B=11
, and
C=3n
to see that (
9.8
) holds and the proof is concluded.
∎

The remaining cases are
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r)
or
\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
.

Lemma 9.10
.

Theorem

3.1
holds if
M
is in class
\mathcal{S}
with
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r)
or
\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
.

Proof.

As usual, we replace
G
by its cover acting faithfully on
V=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
, and
L\trianglelefteq M\leq G
where
L
is quasisimple with
L/Z(L)\cong S
.

Assume first
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(r)
. We may assume
r\geq 7
and
r\neq 9
in view of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(4)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)\cong A_{5}
and
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(9)\cong A_{6}
.
Suppose
p\mid r
, that is,
S\in\text{Lie}(p)
. If
V
is
p
-restricted as
L
-module, then
r=q
,
V
is a symmetric power of the natural 2-dimensional module, and a regular unipotent element
g
of
L
is regular in
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
. Moreover,
g^{G}
splits into at most
(2,q-1)
-classes, and by
[
7
]
we have
n\geq 4
; it follows that
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq(2,q-1)|C_{G}(g)|/q<|G:M|^{1/3}
. For example, for
n=4
we have
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
,
p\geq 5
, and
|C_{G}(g)|=(2,q-1)q^{2}
, from which
\mathrm{fix}(g,G/M)\leq(2,q-1)^{2}q<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
V
is not
p
-restricted, then by
[
70
]
the possibilities are
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{f})\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q^{f})<\mathrm{PSp}_{2^{f}}(q)
with
qf
odd, and
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{f})\cong\Omega_{3}(q^{f})<\Omega_{3^{f}}(q)
with
qf
odd. We have
V=W\otimes W^{(q)}\otimes\cdots\otimes W^{(q^{f-1})}
where
\dim(W)=2
or
\dim(W)=3
in the respective cases. We let
g\in L
be of order
\Phi^{*}_{2}(q^{f})
.
Assume first
n=8
. We see that
g
has an eigenvalue generating
\mathbb{F}_{q^{6}}
, so
g
has six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues and the conclusion follows. In all the other cases, we see that
g
has distinct eigenvalues on
W\otimes W^{(q)}
. In particular, each eigenspace on
V
has dimension at most
\dim(W)^{f-2}
, which is sufficient.

Assume now
p\nmid r
, that is,
S\in\text{Lie}(p^{\prime})
, and denote by
\ell
the prime divisor of
r
. If
r=7,11,13
we can use
[
8
]
; assume then
r\geq 16
. Denoting
e=(2,r-1)
, the possibilities for
n
are
(r\pm 1)/e
,
r\pm 1
and
r
. The case
n=r+1
is excluded since this representation is imprimitive. Let
g
be a generator of a split torus of
L
, and let
P
be a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
L
. By Brauer’s permutation lemma,
\langle g\rangle
permutes the nontrivial irreducible characters of
P
in orbits of size
(r-1)/e
. It follows that if
n=(r-1)/e
then
g
is regular; if
n=(r+1)/e
then
g
is regular on a codimension
2/e
-subspace and
\dim(C_{V}(g))=4-e
; if
n=r-1
with
r
odd then
V
is the sum of two cyclic
K\langle g\rangle
-modules of dimension
(r-1)/2
; if
n=r
then
V=U\oplus W
where
W
is a one-dimensional trivial
K\langle g\rangle
-module, and
U
is a cyclic
K\langle g\rangle
-module (for
r
even), or the sum of two cyclic
K\langle g\rangle
-modules of dimension
(r-1)/2
(for
r
odd).

Assume now
S=\mathrm{PSL}^{\pm}_{3}(r)
with
r\geq 3
. We choose
|g|=\Phi^{*}_{3}(r)
for
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(r)
and
|g|=\Phi^{*}_{6}(r)
for
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(r)
, so
N:=N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)
acts with all orbits of size
3
on the set of nontrivial eigenspaces of
g
on
V
. Note also that by
[
27
, Propositions 3.11 and 3.13]

S
is generated by two conjugates of
g
, so
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/2
.

Assume first
p\mid r
. Let
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
be simple roots for the algebraic group
A_{2}
corresponding to
S
, with corresponding fundamental dominant weights
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
. For integers
x
and
y
, we will denote the character
x\lambda_{1}+y\lambda_{2}
by
(x,y)
. Let
\lambda=(x,y)
be the highest dominant weight of
V
, where
x\geq y
.

Suppose
x=y
, that is,
V
is self-dual. Then, for each
N
-orbit
\mathcal{O}
of nontrivial eigenspaces, there is another orbit whose eigenspaces have the same dimension (and whose corresponding eigenvalues are inverses of those in
\mathcal{O}
). Since
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))\leq n/2
, we may apply
Lemmas

9.3
and
9.4
with
c=1/2
and
B=6
to deduce:

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 16
, then (
9.6
) is satisfied.

•

If
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
and
n\geq 12
, then (
9.8
) is satisfied.

If
n
is at least these values, then by
Lemmas

9.3
and
9.4
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. Assume then
n
is smaller than these values; by
[
61
]
, the only remaining possibility is the adjoint module for
S
, with
n=7
or
8
according to whether
p=3
or
p\neq 3
. For
n=7
we have
M\not\in A
by
[
7
]
. For
n=8
we have
a=2
, and as above every nontrivial eigenspace of
g
has dimension
1
. Moreover
G=\Omega^{\pm}_{8}(q)
; by
Lemma

7.10
, the dimension of the centralizer of
g
in
\mathrm{SO}_{8}(K)
is
4
and we see that
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
.

Suppose now
x>y
, so
V
is not self-dual. We claim that
g
has at least six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues on
V
.

Assume
V
is
p
-restricted as
L
-module. It follows from Premet’s theorem
[
68
, Theorem 1]
that every dominant weight
\gamma=(c,d)
with
\gamma\in X:=\lambda-\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{1}-\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{2}
is a weight of
V
. Suppose
x\geq y+2
. Since
(x-y,0)=\lambda-(y,y)=\lambda-y\alpha_{1}-y\alpha_{2}\in X
, again by Premet’s theorem,
V
contains all the weights corresponding to the representation with highest weight
(x-y,0)
, which is the
(x-y)
-th symmetric power. Since
x-y\geq 2
, and since
g
has at least six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues on
(2,0)
, we deduce that
g
has at least six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues on
V
, as claimed.
Now suppose
x=y+1
. Then
(2,1)=\lambda-(y-1,y-1)\in X
, so
V
contains all the weights corresponding to the representation with highest weight
(2,1)
. This has dimension
15
and is a constituent of
S^{2}(W)\otimes W^{*}
where
W
is the
3
-dimensional natural module. We can then calculate that
g
has at least six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues on
V
.

Assume now
V
is not
p
-restricted. The possible embeddings are of type
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(q)<\mathrm{PSU}_{9}(q^{1/2})
, or
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(q^{f})<\mathrm{PSL}_{3^{f}}(q)
, or
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(q^{f/2})<\mathrm{PSU}_{3^{f}}(q^{1/2})
with
f
odd. In all cases, it is easy to check that
g
has at least six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues. For example, in the first case we see that
g
has two eigenvalues both of which generate
\mathbb{F}_{q^{3}}
and that are not
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-conjugate (and so we get six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues by taking
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-conjugates). In the latter two cases, we see that
g
has an eigenvalue generating
\mathbb{F}_{q^{3f}}
.
Then, in all cases
g
has at least six distinct nontrivial eigenvalues, as claimed.

Denote by
E
the number of distinct eigenvalues of
g
. Since
a\leq n/2
and since nontrivial eigenspaces occur in orbits of size
3
, it can be seen that
d+E
is at most

7+\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+3\left((\frac{n}{2}-3)/3\right)^{2}+3=\frac{n^{2}}{3}-n+13

(attained when
g
has trivial eigenspace of dimension
n/2
, three eigenspaces of dimension
1
and three eigenspaces of dimension
(n/2-3)/3
), from which it also follows that
d\leq n^{2}/3-n+6
.
If
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
then we deduce
|C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{n^{2}-3n+18}
, which is
<|G:M|
if
n\geq 15
. If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
then, setting
q_{0}=q^{1/2}
, by
Lemma

7.11
we deduce
|C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q_{0}^{3(d+E)}\leq q_{0}^{n^{2}-3n+39}
, which is
<|G:M|
if
n\geq 24
.

Let us address the remaining cases (i.e. the case
n\leq 14
for
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
and the case
n\leq 23
for
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
). Assume first
V
is
p
-restricted; the options are
n=6,10
for
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
; and
n=6,10,15,18,21
for
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
. If
n=6
then
V=S^{2}(W)
(where
W
is the natural 3-dimensional module),
g
is regular and we have
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
n=10
then
V=S^{3}(W)
; we see that
a=\dim(C_{V}(g))=1
and so
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
. If
n=15
then it must be
a\leq 6
, which implies
d\leq 51
and the result follows. If
n=18
then
p=5
and
V
is a constituent of
S^{3}(W)\otimes W^{*}
. Since by the above the largest eigenspace on
S^{3}(W)
has dimension at most
2
, it follows that
a\leq 6
, which gives the conclusion. Finally, if
n=21
then
a\leq 9
, so
d\leq 111=3\cdot 6^{2}+3
and this is sufficient. Assume now
V
is not
p
-restricted with
n\leq 23
; by
[
70
]
, the possible embeddings are of type
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q^{2})<\mathrm{SL}_{9}(q)
and
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)<\mathrm{SU}_{9}(q^{1/2})
. In the first case,
a=3
and every nontrivial eigenspace has dimension
1
, so
d=15
and the result follows. In the second case, there are three nontrivial eigenspaces of dimension
1
, and three nontrivial eigenspaces of dimension
2
, so again
d=15
and
|C_{G}(g)|<|G:M|^{1/3}
also in this case.

Assume finally
p\nmid r
. By
[
32
]
, if
n\leq 40
then we have
r\leq 5
and we can use
[
8
]
. From now on, assume
n\geq 41
. We first claim that
|M|<q^{n}
. This is the case if
r\leq 4
, so assume
r\geq 5
. By
[
44
]
, if
S=\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(r)
(resp.
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(r)
) then
n\geq r^{2}-1
(resp.
n\geq r(r^{2}-1)/(r+1)
), and we deduce that
|M|\leq q|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|<q^{n}
, as claimed. So from now on we have
n\geq 41
and
|M|<q^{n}
.

Suppose
g
is semisimple. Note that every prime divisor of
|g|
is at least
7
and also we have
|N_{L}(\langle g\rangle)/C_{L}(g)|=3
. By
[
80
, Theorem 1.2]
, we deduce that
g
has at least
5
distinct eigenvalues. Since these are permuted in orbits of size
3
, we deduce that
g
has at least
6
distinct eigenvalues. In particular, by the same calculation as in the case
p\mid r
we see that
d\leq n^{2}/3-n+6
and
d+E\leq n^{2}/3-n+13
, where
E
denotes the number of distinct eigenvalues of
g
. Denote also by
E_{2}
the number of distinct irreducible factors of degree at least two of the characteristic polynomial of
g
. Next we upper bound
|C_{G}(g)|
using
Lemmas

7.10
and
7.11
.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}(q)
, we have
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{d}
and so

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{n+n^{2}-3n+18}\leq q^{n^{2}-2}<|G|.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q^{1/2})
, then setting
q_{0}=q^{1/2}
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<q_{0}^{d+E}
and so

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q_{0}^{2n+n^{2}-3n+39}\leq q_{0}^{n^{2}-2}<|G|.

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, then
d/2+E_{2}+a/2\leq n^{2}/6-2n+26+n/4
. By
Lemmas

7.10
and
7.11
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{d/2+E_{2}+a/2}
and so

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{n+n^{2}/2-6n+78+3n/4}\leq q^{n^{2}/2+n/2-1}<|G|.

•

If
G=\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{n}(q)
, then
d/2+E_{2}\leq n^{2}/6-2n+26
. By
Lemmas

7.10
and
7.11
we have
|C_{G}(g)|<q^{d/2+E_{2}+2}
and so

|M||C_{G}(g)|^{3}<q^{n+n^{2}/2-6n+78+6}\leq q^{n^{2}/2-n/2-2}<|G|.

Suppose finally
g
is not semisimple; we apply
Lemma

7.4
. Letting
P
be a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\langle g\rangle
, we have that
V\downarrow P=V_{0}\oplus U
where all Jordan blocks on
V_{0}
have size
|P|\geq 7
, and
U
is the sum of at most
3
indecomposable module of dimension
t
. If
t\geq 4
then applying
Lemma

7.8
with
C=0
and
B=4
we deduce
d\leq n^{2}/4
. In particular, the exact same computation as in
Lemma

9.8
reduces to the case
n\leq 29
, for which we are done (as we are assuming
n\geq 41
).

Assume then
t\leq 3
; so
\dim(V_{0})\leq 9
. By
Lemma

7.8
with
C=9
and
B=7
we see that
d\leq n^{2}/7+70
. The number
R
of Jordan blocks of
g
is at most
9+(n-9)/7=n/7+54/7
. Then we can calculate similarly to above that, since
n\geq 27
, the conclusion holds. The proof is now complete.
∎

10.
Proof of
Corollary

1.3

For completeness, we give a proof of the deduction of
Corollary

1.3
from
Theorem

1.2
, which is an immediate application of Frobenius density theorem.

Proof of
Corollary

1.3
.

Let
L
be the Galois closure of
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and let
G=\mathrm{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})
be the Galois group. Then
G
acts faithfully and transitively on the roots of
f
and
M:=\mathrm{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q}(\alpha))
is a point stabilizer. The assumption that
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)/\mathbb{Q}
is minimal is equivalent to the fact that the action is primitive, and the assumption that
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)/\mathbb{Q}
is not Galois is equivalent to the fact that the action is not regular. In particular, by
Theorem

1.2
there exists
g\in G
with
1\leq\mathrm{fix}(g)\leq n^{1/3}
. By Frobenius density theorem (see for example
[
78
, Theorem p. 11]
), the (natural or analytic) density of primes
p
such that
f
has at least one root and at most
n^{1/3}
roots in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
exists, and is equal to the proportion of elements of
G
with at least one fixed point and at most
n^{1/3}
fixed points. This proportion is at least
1/|G|>0
and the statement follows.
∎
